# Software Engineering Methods

**Subject**: CSC3003S Advanced Programming — Advanced Software Development (ASD)
**Source**: [ASD02 Methods.pdf](../../Sources/CSC3003S%20-%20Advanced%20Programming/ASD/ASD02%20Methods.pdf), Johan Bontes, University of Cape Town, pages 1–40
**Generated**: 2026-11-06

> [!info] <span style="color:#66b3ff">Lecture reference</span>
> Faster/better/cheaper (pick two), why software projects fail, naïve approaches (code-and-fix, waterfall), wicked problems, and iterative development.

---

## Core Content

### Estimation Is The Core Problem

- The most common problem in software systems is not **construction** but **estimation**.
- Projects fail to meet cost and schedule because those **targets are wrong**.
- Costing software is difficult, and we know little about accurate estimation, so targets are unreasonable.
- Targets are often set by the people least able to make them: marketers, managers and customers.
- Communicating abstract or conceptual ideas is hard.

### Faster, Better, Cheaper: Pick Two

In the 1990s NASA adopted the slogan "Faster, better, cheaper". After a series of unsuccessful projects, they abandoned it: all three can't be satisfied at once. Software engineers restate the goal as **"Faster, better, cheaper: pick 2 out of 3."**

![Faster, better, cheaper: pick two](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/02_Software_Engineering_Methods/pick-two.svg)

*The project triangle: time, cost, and scope & quality. Fixing two determines the third.*

| Corner | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Time | Faster |
| Scope & quality | Better |
| Cost | Cheaper |

### The Standish CHAOS Report

The Standish Group's CHAOS Report (2015) surveys project outcomes.

Projects meeting each target (FY2011–2015):

| Target | Yes | No |
|---|---|---|
| On budget | 44% | 56% |
| On time | 40% | 60% |
| On target (scope) | 56% | 44% |

Traditional resolution for all projects:

| Outcome | 2011 | 2012 | 2013 | 2014 | 2015 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Successful | 39% | 37% | 41% | 36% | 36% |
| Challenged | 39% | 46% | 40% | 47% | 45% |
| Failed | 22% | 17% | 19% | 17% | 19% |

Resolution by project size:

| Size | Successful | Challenged | Failed |
|---|---|---|---|
| Grand | 6% | 51% | 43% |
| Large | 11% | 59% | 30% |
| Medium | 12% | 62% | 26% |
| Moderate | 24% | 64% | 12% |
| Small | 61% | 32% | 7% |

Resolution by method (all sizes):

| Method | Successful | Challenged | Failed |
|---|---|---|---|
| Agile | 39% | 52% | 9% |
| Waterfall | 11% | 60% | 29% |

**Caveat**: Standish's methods have been increasingly criticised. Eveleens and Verhoef ("The Rise and Fall of the Chaos Report Figures", *IEEE Software*, 2010) argue the figures for individual organisations don't reflect reality and are highly influenced by forecasting biases, so any aggregation is unreliable.

### Think Big, Act Small

"Just say no" to large projects:

- The secret to project success is to **limit size and complexity**; these outweigh all other success factors.
- Break large projects into a sequence of smaller ones, prioritised by direct business value.
- Use stable, full-time, cross-functional teams following a disciplined approach.
- The quick solution is to say no to large projects; more sensibly, adopt a **small-project strategy** — lower cost, with fewer defects.
- Projects too often get too big to succeed. Teams are constantly asked to do more for less, but the real key to success is **doing less for less**.

### Wasted Effort And YAGNI

#### Definitions

> **YAGNI ("You Ain't Gonna Need It")**: Build only what you need now; avoid speculative development.

> **Gold plating (bells and whistles)**: Adding features or polish beyond what is required.

Feature usage:

| Used | Share of features |
|---|---|
| Always | 7% |
| Often | 13% |
| Sometimes | 16% |
| Rarely | 19% |
| Never | 45% |

Almost two thirds of features are rarely or never used. Stop developing them and productivity could double.

YAGNI counters thinking like "I'm sure I'll need extra functionality later, so I'll write it now". Speculative development adds complexity to the code prematurely.

### The Economics Of Adding Features

![Economics of adding features](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/02_Software_Engineering_Methods/feature-economics.svg)

*Returns from features grow roughly linearly while production cost grows exponentially, so after some point extra features give negative return on investment.*

| Project type | Shape |
|---|---|
| Profitable | Returns outpace costs throughout |
| Ultimately unprofitable | Early positive ROI, then features become a drag and ROI turns negative |

The cost curve of software is **exponential**:

- Fred Brooks attributes this to the **cost of communication** — e.g. customer and developer must understand each other perfectly.
- New projects succeed because the cost curve is still flat.
- Once costs start rising, they quickly overwhelm the value added by new features.
- This applies to maintenance costs, not just new features.

### Problems: Bad Communication And Crunch

**Bad communication** — *Age of Booty* (Certain Affinity, Max Hoberman): there were many disconnects between the perceived and actual state of the game. Designers kept refining plans for sophisticated features (matchmaking, party support) long after programmers had drastically simplified or cut them. Lack of attention, poor communication and wishful thinking meant designers believed features were far more advanced than they were, and only found out very late.

**Crunch** — *Drawn to Life* (5th Cell, Joseph Tringali): a "perfect storm" of inexperience with the platform, an ambitious game and a schedule that was too short. With little pre-production, the team played catch-up from day one, taking shortcuts and working late weekdays and Saturdays for the whole second half of the project.

- **Crunch** is a side effect of other problems and a cause of **burnout**.

### Software Entropy And Human Factors

#### Definitions

> **Entropy**: A measure of disorder in a physical system.

> **Software entropy**: A measure of code complexity; it tends to increase over time ("software rot").

> **Technical debt**: Accumulated structural degradation from shortcuts, fixes and enhancements that makes future change harder.

- Over-scoped design adds complexity at the start.
- Bug fixes and enhancements increase complexity and degrade structure (technical debt).
- Most applications grow by 5–10% a year.
- Entropy makes code hard to change, fix and understand.
- The cure: **YAGNI at the start** and **refactoring as you go**.

Human factors that waste effort:

| Factor | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **Yak shaving** | Pointless work only tangentially related to the goal |
| **Procrastination** ("student syndrome") | Starting work only near the deadline |
| **Gold plating** | Unneeded extras |
| **Task switching** | Losing time and focus moving between tasks |

### Laziness And Choosing A Methodology

"We'll solve it later" is costly:

- **Essential problems** should be specified in the early phases. Otherwise you do the requirements work later, plus extra tear-down and repair.
- **Accidental problems** should be flagged and analysed when found:
  - solution known → refactor;
  - solution unknown → back to requirements.

| Methodology | Approach | Risk | Fix |
|---|---|---|---|
| Agile | One big project → many small iterative projects | Too little preparation; late fixes cost up to 100× more | Take time to work out the basics, e.g. throw-away prototypes to shake out wicked problems |
| Waterfall | One big project — "eat the whole elephant" | Requirements and analysis must be near perfect | Use only when the problem is well understood (few accidental problems); reuse lessons from earlier projects; prototype anything not fully worked out |

### Code And Fix

Code-and-fix is the naïve first approach — really the **absence** of a methodology.

- Little or no planning; dive straight into implementation.
- Reactive; ends with bugs.
- If bugs multiply faster than they can be fixed: a **death spiral**, and the project is cancelled.
- The non-fix is **crunch time**, sometimes worn as a badge of honour, but it causes burnout.

### The Waterfall Method

#### Definitions

> **Waterfall method**: A sequential process — requirements specification, analysis, design, implementation, testing and integration, operation and maintenance — where each phase completes before the next begins.

![Waterfall method](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/02_Software_Engineering_Methods/waterfall.svg)

*Each phase flows into the next with no way back, which suits fully understood problems only.*

Waterfall comes from building projects, where it suits because:

- changes are too hard or expensive, so only minor tweaks are possible;
- the problem is fully understood and can be fully simulated;
- the core structure is preserved and mostly standard patterns are used.

**Case: FIU pedestrian bridge**

| Phase | What happened |
|---|---|
| Requirements | "Must have redundancy" (i.e. use a traditional design), but FIU wanted it to "be unique" |
| Design | A single-truss design, the first and only one in the world; the primary engineer's calculations were wrong and the second reviewer missed it |
| Implementation | Built as per the faulty design |
| Testing | Cracks appeared; the fix (loosen and re-tension a tensioner) caused the bridge to collapse |

Why pure waterfall cannot work for software:

- Every preceding phase must be perfect, but software is infinitely changeable.
- The pure model allows no going back, yet coding, testing, user acceptance, operational and maintenance issues can all require redesign.
- Big projects fail much more often than small ones.

### Wicked Problems And The Problem Matrix

#### Definitions

> **Wicked problem**: An "unknown unknown" — a problem that cannot be anticipated and only becomes visible late (in implementation, testing or operation). Software is full of them, because it is much more complex than hardware.

The Tacoma Narrows bridge collapse (7 November 1940) is the classic physical example: a failure mode nobody had foreseen.

| | Solved: yes, clear | Solved: no, foggy |
|---|---|---|
| **Foreseen: yes** | **Known known** — use a standard solution | **Unknown known** — refactor |
| **Foreseen: not yet** | **Known unknown** — fix in requirements/analysis | **Unknown unknown** (wicked) — go back to the start |

Known unknowns show up during requirements and analysis; unknown unknowns show up during coding, testing or deployment.

### Change Gets Exponentially More Expensive

- The cost of change rises exponentially with time, which motivates conservative, fear-driven design.
- A change late in the process can cost **1000 times** as much as early on:

| Stage | Cost of the same change |
|---|---|
| Writing the spec | 5 minutes |
| Programming the feature | 2 days |
| Testing before deployment | 2 weeks |
| Patching after deployment | 1 month |

### Modern Processes: Prototyping And RAD

Modern processes are lighter than waterfall:

- less documentation and fewer procedures;
- multiple versions released during development;
- parallel development and prototypes;
- "**skateboard**" first: build only what's required, no extra requirements;
- **design for change**, because change is inevitable.

**Prototyping** cycles between requirements, a prototype and tests.

**Rapid Application Development (RAD)** cycles: requirements → design → develop → deploy (test) → learn (reflect) → requirements.

### Iterative Development

#### Definitions

> **Iterative (incremental) development**: A process that reaches the goal through a series of ever-improving delivery cycles.

The cycle: plan → develop → test → release → feedback → plan …

![Iterative cycle](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/02_Software_Engineering_Methods/iterative-cycle.svg)

*Each loop produces a working version and user feedback that steers the next one.*

![Iterative steering](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/02_Software_Engineering_Methods/iterative-steering.svg)

*Each iteration corrects course toward the real target using feedback, instead of aiming once and hoping.*

**The big difference**: instead of 18 months to create and evaluate one big project, build and show a new version to users every 2–4 weeks. This requires:

- team members close together and close to the customer;
- agreement on good ideas in hours, not months;
- teams that become experts through intense hands-on problem solving and testing.

The result is a real system meeting users' real, tested needs, not "perceived" needs.

| Benefits | Danger |
|---|---|
| Favoured by small start-ups | Intense focus on only high-priority features means many alternative concepts are never explored |
| Greatly reduces the risk of failure — with only one shot at success, you steer using information instead of launching blindly | Good ideas can be lost: you may iterate to a **small success** and miss bigger opportunities elsewhere |
| Long term: more value sooner, with lower overall risk | |

### Iterative Method Flavours

| Method | Originators | Key features |
|---|---|---|
| **Agile** ("Agile Manifesto") | — | Mini software projects; face-to-face communication |
| **RAD** (Rapid Application Development) | James Martin | Voice of the customer; collaborative; rigid schedule |
| **XP** (Extreme Programming) | Kent Beck | Design on the fly; unit testing of all code; pair programming; refactoring |
| **Scrum** | Takeuchi, Nonaka, Schwaber | Teams "scrum down" in short iterations (sprints); empirical process |

# Agile

**Subject**: CSC3003S Advanced Programming — Advanced Software Development (ASD)
**Source**: [ASD03 Agile.pdf](../../Sources/CSC3003S%20-%20Advanced%20Programming/ASD/ASD03%20Agile.pdf), Johan Bontes, University of Cape Town, pages 1–40
**Generated**: 2026-11-06

> [!info] <span style="color:#66b3ff">Lecture reference</span>
> The Agile manifesto and principles (and their limits), barely sufficient requirements and user stories, fixed timescales with variable scope, a working product at all times, and pair programming.

> [!note] <span style="color:#ffc778">Examinability</span>
> All material is examinable unless stated otherwise (purple slide topics are not). The exam is mostly about understanding — don't just memorise lists.

---

## Core Content

### The Agile Manifesto

#### Definitions

> **Agile Manifesto (2001)**: "We are uncovering better ways of developing software by doing it and helping others do it. Through this work we have come to value: individuals and interactions over processes and tools; working software over comprehensive documentation; customer collaboration over contract negotiation; responding to change over following a plan. That is, while there is value in the items on the right, we value the items on the left more."

![Agile manifesto values](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/03_Agile/manifesto-values.svg)

*Each value pair keeps the right-hand item but prioritises the left-hand item.*

| Valued more | Over |
|---|---|
| Individuals and interactions | Processes and tools |
| Working software | Comprehensive documentation |
| Customer collaboration | Contract negotiation |
| Responding to change | Following a plan |

Signatories include Kent Beck, Mike Beedle, Arie van Bennekum, Alistair Cockburn, Ward Cunningham, Martin Fowler, James Grenning, Jim Highsmith, Andrew Hunt, Ron Jeffries, Jon Kern, Brian Marick, Robert C. Martin, Steve Mellor, Ken Schwaber, Jeff Sutherland and Dave Thomas.

### The Manifesto Fifteen Years Later

By 2015 the balance had shifted back: the right-hand items (processes, documentation, contracts, plans) proved to matter more than the manifesto suggested. Each side has trade-offs.

**Processes and tools versus individuals and interactions:**

| Aspect | Processes and tools | Individuals and interactions |
|---|---|---|
| Communication | Easy | Hard |
| Division of labour | Clear | Less so |
| Task switching | Few distractions | Many |
| Team structure | Hierarchy | Democracy |

**Waterfall (comprehensive documentation, contract negotiation) versus Agile (working software, customer collaboration):**

| Aspect | Waterfall | Agile |
|---|---|---|
| Communication | Easy | Hard |
| Division of labour | Clear | Not clear |
| Works well for | Known problems | Novel development |
| Final product = plan? | ✓ | ✗ |

### Business Reality: Security First

In 2025 the top three business problems are security, security and security. Security must be addressed in the **architecture**:

- More planning is needed.
- Defined secure patterns must be followed (memory, encryption, authentication).
- Security cannot be "bolted on" in the final iterations.
- It is hard to test for.
- Users don't understand security and don't want it, because more security means less usability.
- Insecure software is a high-probability, high-cost risk, so it must be avoided.

This cuts against Agile's "design as you go" instincts.

### The Twelve Agile Principles

1. **Satisfy the customer** through early and continuous delivery of valuable software.
2. **Welcome changing requirements**, even late in development.
3. **Deliver working software frequently**.
4. Business people and developers must **work together** daily.
5. Build projects around **motivated individuals**; give them the support they need and **trust** them.
6. The most efficient and effective way to convey information is **face-to-face conversation**.
7. **Working software** is the primary measure of progress.
8. Sponsors, developers and users should be able to **maintain a constant pace** indefinitely.
9. Continuous attention to **technical excellence** and good design.
10. **Simplicity** — the art of maximising the amount of work not done — is essential.
11. The best architectures, requirements and designs emerge from **self-organising teams**.
12. The team regularly **reflects** on how to become more effective and adjusts its behaviour.

### Empowered Teams

- The project team must have **sole responsibility** for delivering the product.
- Any outside interference is disruptive and reduces motivation.
- Together, the team must establish and clarify the requirements, prioritise them, agree the tasks to deliver them, and estimate the effort.
- This ensures buy-in and commitment from the outset, and a real sense of ownership when challenges arise.

### Pareto's Law

#### Definitions

> **Pareto's law (80/20 rule)**: Typically about 80% of results come from about 20% of the effort. Named after Vilfredo Pareto (1848–1923).

- The split is arbitrary — it could as well be 75/35, or simply "most/minority".
- Try to focus on the important 20% of effort that gives the best results.
- The problem: no-one knows **which** 20% will deliver 80% of the results.

### Barely Sufficient Requirements

Agile requirements are **barely sufficient**:

- Captured at a high level, piecemeal, **just in time** for each feature to be developed.
- The minimum needed to enable development and testing; minimise time on anything that isn't part of the product.
- Understood well enough to set the scope and give high-level budget estimates.
- Captured in **collaborative workshops** so all team members understand them and can contribute, challenge and understand what's needed and why.

The lecturer's critique of each point:

| Agile claim | Caveat |
|---|---|
| Capture requirements piecemeal, just in time | Only acceptable for low-probability, low-cost risks; a risk assessment is needed |
| Barely sufficient detail | Only for features not yet well understood; you must still understand interactions and security |
| Collaborative workshops where everyone contributes | An extreme form of democracy ("anarchy") that disregards formal and informal power |

### User Stories

#### Definitions

> **User story**: A simple statement, in business language everyone understands, of what a user wants to do with a feature. Similar to a use case but lighter and simpler. Most Agile teams represent each requirement as a user story.

Form:

> As a **[user role]**, I want to **[task]**, so I can **[goal]**.

A story covers the **who, what and why** of a feature — not the **how**. Job-listing site examples:

- As a job seeker, I want to search for a job, so I can advance my career.
- As a recruiter, I want to post a job vacancy, so I can find a new team member.

Capturing stories:

- At the start, capture an initial list of stories up front, useful for estimating and planning.
- Defer the details until the story is prioritised and due for development.
- Users often tell stories about their current system's failings and how things could work better; capture these as user stories as they are told.
- Traditional projects capture them in a lengthy analysis process and a lengthy document — not user-friendly.

**First rule of usability: don't listen to users.**

> "To design the best UX, pay attention to what users do, not what they say. Self-reported claims are unreliable, as are user speculations about future behavior. Users do not know what they want." — Jakob Nielsen

> "A lot of times, people don't know what they want until you show it to them." — Steve Jobs

What Jobs meant: users know what they (don't) want, but don't know what they **need** until you show it to them.

### Recording User Stories And Sizing Work

User stories are written on postcard-sized cards in three parts:

| Part | Content |
|---|---|
| Heading | Name/description, reference numbers, estimated size, etc. |
| Conversation (front) | Background (who, why) and what the system should do; a sketch or diagram; notes on how it should function |
| Acceptance criteria (back) | Test cases that reveal scenarios users, developers or analysts may not have thought of |

A card forces requirements into small, manageable pieces of functionality — single features.

- Cards can be supported by documentation, but keep it to the bare minimum, always in very small units.
- Break requirements into tasks of **at most 16 hours**, preferably **8 hours**, so progress can be measured daily.
- All items are **deliverables**, not activities or tasks: you can see a deliverable to judge its quality and completeness, but you can't see a task.

### Incremental Design

Agile does not follow top-down design (as in Fred Brooks's waterfall view).

| Top-down design | Agile incremental design |
|---|---|
| Time spent on design is worth it if it saves reworking the design many times | Design is always the same size as the system |
| Plan the structure up front | "You can't possibly anticipate the problems and alternatives that will arise once you start coding" |

Critique: that Agile claim describes unknown unknowns, which is **not true for most problems**. If a new need requires major changes, Agile accepts that as the price of flexibility — perhaps the feature wasn't known at the start, or the need might have gone away had it been known.

### Fixed Timescale And Budget, Variable Scope

In Agile development **requirements evolve but timescales are fixed**.

| Traditional development | Agile development |
|---|---|
| Capture all known requirements up front | Assume requirements will change |
| Changes go through change control | However much analysis you do, you can't know what you want until you see and use the software |
| Users are told late changes are expensive, so they include everything they ever dreamed of | External conditions may change while you analyse and design |
| Projects start bloated; users may use only 20% of the product, and no-one knows which 20% | Build the core first |

Business expects teams to deliver the agreed requirements on time, within budget and at acceptable quality. In Agile:

- **scope is variable**; cost and timescale are not;
- this only works if the core, highest-priority features are delivered in the earliest iterations;
- result: fixed budget, fewer resources, fixed launch date.

### The Agile Development Cycle And Definition Of Done

The cycle is **analyse → develop → test → repeat**, one feature at a time, in priority order. Each feature must be fully developed to the point it could ship.

| Advantages |
|---|
| Some risks reduced (though others increase) |
| Higher perceived value: some benefits delivered early |
| More flexibility |
| Better cost management |

#### Definitions

> **Done**: In Agile, "done" means **shippable**. Every feature in an iteration must be 100% complete by its end.

![Definition of done](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/03_Agile/definition-of-done.svg)

*A feature is done only when every check passes and the product owner accepts it.*

A feature is done when:

1. unit tests pass,
2. code is reviewed,
3. acceptance criteria pass,
4. functional tests pass,
5. non-functional requirements are met,
6. the product owner accepts the user story.

Ideally each iteration produces a release. A feature may depend on work in progress, but on its own merit it should be shippable. Finishing each feature before the next avoids a system where several features are "90% complete" or untested.

### Working Product At All Times

- The best way to get user feedback is to give users a product, even a work in progress. Prototypes beat documents.
- Effort spent getting a broken product working again is a missed opportunity for valuable work.

**Agile sandcastles**: waterfall builds a very detailed specification, then a design, then builds and tests, then drowns in change requests ("Err…") before a patched-up deployment. Agile builds a small but complete sandcastle in Sprint 0 and grows it sprint by sprint, so there is always a working castle.

**Prototypes** are an inexpensive way to try out ideas and understand as many issues as possible before the real implementation. Prototyping risky problems raises the chance of always having a working product.

| Prototype class | Purpose |
|---|---|
| **True prototype** | Test implementation to understand a problem before implementing it for real (thrown away) |
| **Skeleton** | Intended to gradually grow into the final solution |

**Continuous integration**:

- Integrate changes continuously, at least daily, so modules fit together and the product keeps working.
- Avoid the bad habit of checking files out and not checking in until the work is "done".
- Gradual change means integration problems and regressions are caught early.

**Nightly builds**:

- Rebuild the software from scratch daily; the result should be an installable product image.
- Include as many automated tests as possible.
- If the build or tests fail, fix that first. No-one integrates more work until the build succeeds again; otherwise bad changes pile up and threaten quality.

**Don't neglect performance**:

| View | Argument |
|---|---|
| Clarity first | Get code clear, then optimise the 1–3% that needs it |
| Performance first | If you don't code for performance from the start, the code will always be slow |

A few critical loops dominate performance — use **profiling tools** to find them.

### Pair Programming

#### Definitions

> **Pair programming**: Two programmers sit together and write every line of code, one at the keyboard and the other supporting. Two programmers plus one computer form the atomic unit of XP development. Pairs form dynamically; development is "egoless".

> **Refactoring**: Improving the quality of code without changing its functionality.

| Advantages |
|---|
| Informal review: every line is seen by at least two people |
| Common ownership: individuals aren't blamed for problems; the team is collectively responsible for the system and for fixing it |
| Spreads knowledge across the team, reducing risk if someone leaves |
| Motivates refactoring, since the whole team benefits |

**Productivity**: similar to two people working independently, because of fewer false starts and less rework, and fewer bugs thanks to informal inspection. Pairing experienced with inexperienced developers helps training, but experienced developers may lose productivity in exchange for quality. Pair for complex tasks, perhaps not simple ones.

| Challenges |
|---|
| Working at a partner's pace is exhausting — too slow and too fast are both tiring |
| Constant communication needs empathy and interpersonal skills |
| Different skill levels make contribution and pace hard to estimate |
| Power dynamics (gender, seniority, race, degree) |
| No time alone — introverts recharge alone |
| Pair rotation causes context switching |

### Code Review

A good code review process also ensures every change is seen by at least one other person. Its challenges, compared with pairing:

- It is easy to do sloppy or superficial reviews.
- The developer may over-rely on the reviewer.
- Little communication between programmer and reviewer.
- **Sunk cost**: the code is already written, so asking for a refactor feels like wasted effort.
- A long gap between making and finding a bug ("what was I thinking when I wrote that?").

---

# Scrum

**Subject**: CSC3003S Advanced Programming — Advanced Software Development (ASD)
**Source**: [ASD04 Scrum.pdf](../../Sources/CSC3003S%20-%20Advanced%20Programming/ASD/ASD04%20Scrum.pdf), Johan Bontes, University of Cape Town, pages 1–45
**Generated**: 2026-11-06

> [!info] <span style="color:#66b3ff">Lecture reference</span>
> Scrum as a concrete Agile implementation: early learning, roles, artefacts and events, the sprint flow and meetings, backlogs and burndown charts, and common ways Scrum goes wrong.

---

## Core Content

### What Scrum Is

#### Definitions

> **Scrum**: A concrete implementation of Agile. Agile is a philosophy; Scrum is a specific way of doing it.

- According to the State of Agile survey (just another online survey), 63% of Agile projects use Scrum.
- **Warning**: Scrum is not a silver bullet or a panacea for all development ills. It does not compensate for lack of training or experience, and the team has defined roles that must be filled properly.

### Late Versus Early Learning

| | Waterfall | Scrum |
|---|---|---|
| Strategy | **Late learning**: delays true understanding of the system | **Early learning**: it pays to learn early |
| Integration | Big-bang integration at the end | Early, continuous integration |
| Knowledge growth | Almost no knowledge (risk reduction) until the "moment of truth" — final integration | Knowledge and risk reduction grow steadily |
| Up-front design | Required | Only reduces risk if the problem is well understood |

![Late versus early learning](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/04_Scrum/knowledge-curves.svg)

*In waterfall, knowledge stays flat until final integration; in Scrum it rises with each integrated increment.*

### Scrum Vocabulary

| Term | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Goal | The end objective |
| **Sprint** | A fixed time-box of at most one month |
| Planning | Items to do plus the schedule |
| Product | The software to be built; must be an independent artefact |
| **Backlog** | Work items, ordered by priority |
| Team | At most 10 people, no sub-teams, no hierarchy |
| **Scrum Master (SM)** | Coach and mentor of the team; liaison to management |
| **Daily scrum** | A 15-minute meeting to discuss progress |
| **Increment** | The outcome of a sprint: a concrete stepping stone toward the product goal |
| **Definition of done** | The agreed quality; the work is finished |
| Undone work | Unfinished, or quality not OK |
| **Sprint review** | Inspect the outcome and future adaptations |
| **Sprint retrospective** | How to improve quality and effectiveness |

### Scrum Roles

A Scrum team has 10 people or fewer (fewer is better). Need more people? Split the product and form several Scrum teams.

| Role | Count | Responsibilities |
|---|---|---|
| **Scrum Master** | 1 | Guards quality; removes obstacles to progress; chairs Scrum events; trains the team (e.g. in pair programming); is **not a leader but a servant**; can be shared by several teams |
| **Product Owner** | 1 | Owns the project goal; plans the product backlog and its priorities; communicates with customers; liaises with the organisation, which provides funding |
| **Developers** | $n - 2$ | Must have all the skills needed; develop quality work to the definition of done; do sprint and daily planning |

![Scrum roles](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/04_Scrum/scrum-roles.svg)

*The Product Owner faces the customer and organisation, the Scrum Master serves and protects the team, and developers build the increment.*

The team itself looks at the requirements, considers the available technology, evaluates its own skills, and decides how to build the functionality — modifying its approach daily. The heart of Scrum is the iterations plus this creative process.

### Pigs And Chickens

| | Pigs | Chickens |
|---|---|---|
| Who | Team members: SM, PO, developers | Customers, users, vendors, management |
| Involvement | **Committed** | **Involved** — needed, but indirectly |

From the joke: in a ham-and-eggs breakfast, the chicken is involved but the pig is committed. Scrum is about empowering the pigs and getting the chickens out of the kitchen.

### Scrum Artefacts And Commitments

Artefacts represent work or value. Each contains a **commitment** that adds transparency and focus, against which progress can be measured.

| Artefact | What it is | Commitment | Commitment meaning | Characteristics |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Product backlog** | Emergent, ordered list of what's needed to improve the product | Product goal | Future state of the product, used to plan sprints | Clear, concise, transparent in the backlog |
| **Sprint backlog** | Set of product backlog items to work on in the sprint | Sprint goal | Single objective for the sprint | Flexible in how it's achieved; if impossible, cancel the sprint |
| **Increment** | Concrete stepping stone toward the product goal | Definition of done | Formal quality standard all developers commit to | A sprint makes at least one increment; not quality → not done |

### Scrum Events

#### Definitions

> **Scrum event**: A formal opportunity to inspect and adapt Scrum artefacts. All events happen inside a sprint, at a regular time and place; ideally there are no meetings outside events.

| Event | Time-box | Inputs (subject) | Output | Participants |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Sprint | ≤ 1 month (typically 2 weeks) | — | Increments | Mostly developers |
| Sprint planning | ≤ 8 hours (for a 1-month sprint) | Product backlog, product goal, definition of done | Sprint backlog, sprint goal | Scrum team |
| Sprint review | ≤ 4 hours | Increment, sprint, product backlog, progress toward goal | Updated product backlog | Scrum team, stakeholders |
| Sprint retrospective | ≤ 3 hours | Sprint, definition of done | Actionable improvements, updated definition of done | Scrum team |
| Daily scrum | 15 minutes, daily | Progress toward sprint goal | Progress toward sprint goal | Developers |

### Scrum Rules

- The Scrum Master is the **rule-master**; rules mean everyone knows how to play.
- If rules aren't enforced, people waste time working out what to do; if disputed, everyone waits for a resolution.
- The rules have worked in thousands of successful projects.
- To change rules, use the sprint retrospective:
  - changes should come from the team, not management;
  - only once the Scrum Master is convinced everyone understands how Scrum works — no rule changes until then.

### Scrum Flow And Sprints

1. Every project starts with a **vision** (in XP terms, the "system metaphor"), which leads to the logical architecture.
2. The **Product Owner** produces the **product backlog**: functional and non-functional requirements that, once built, deliver the vision. It is prioritised by likely value and divided into proposed releases.
3. Work is done in **sprints** of at most 20 consecutive workdays (a month), each starting with a sprint planning meeting.
4. Each day of the sprint has a 24-hour cycle with a daily scrum.
5. Each sprint ends with a **potentially shippable product increment**.

![Scrum sprint flow](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/04_Scrum/sprint-flow.svg)

*The prioritised product backlog feeds a sprint backlog; daily scrums run inside the sprint loop, which produces a shippable increment.*

### Sprint Planning Meeting

Time-boxed to **8 hours**, which prevents hand-wringing about what's possible: the goal is to get to work, not think about working.

Inputs: product backlog, team capabilities, business conditions, technology, the current product. Outputs: sprint goal and sprint backlog.

It has two segments of at most 4 hours each:

| Segment | Purpose | Who decides |
|---|---|---|
| 1. Select product backlog | The PO says what's desired; the team says how much it believes it can turn into an increment, to demonstrate at the sprint review | Team suggests and decides **how many** items; the PO makes the **selection** |
| 2. Prepare sprint backlog | The team works out how to turn the selected items into an increment; the PO answers questions; others only observe or answer | Team manages its own work |

The output of segment 2 is the sprint backlog: tasks, time estimates and assignments. It needn't be complete, but must be complete enough for every member to commit, and to carry them through the first part of the sprint while they devise more tasks.

### Daily Scrum Meeting

- Time-boxed to **15 minutes**, regardless of team size.
- Same place, same time every workday — ideally first thing, so members think about yesterday and today.
- All team members attend (remotely if necessary, or another member reports why they're absent).
- The Scrum Master starts on time regardless of who's present; latecomers pay a fine to the Scrum Master immediately.

The Scrum Master asks everyone three questions:

1. What did you do yesterday?
2. What will you do today?
3. What stands in the way of getting your work done?

One person talks while others listen. Answers are short — no digressions:

$$
\frac{15\text{ min}}{3\text{ questions} \times \text{people present}} \approx 30 \text{ seconds per answer}
$$

Purpose: synchronise the team's work daily and schedule any meetings needed to help progress. Chickens may attend but may not communicate (verbally or otherwise) or initiate follow-up meetings with pigs.

### During The Sprint

**Time-boxing**: a sprint is at most 30 days (more typically 2 weeks). This is:

- the time a team needs to build something of significant interest to the PO and stakeholders and make it potentially shippable;
- the maximum time to work without artefacts and documentation supporting the thought process;
- the maximum time most stakeholders will wait without losing interest or belief that the team is doing something meaningful.

**Rules during the sprint**:

- The team may seek outside advice, help and support, but receives **no unsolicited advice**. It is self-managing.
- The team has committed to the selected backlog, which is **frozen** until the sprint ends.

**Changing the sprint backlog**:

| Situation | Action |
|---|---|
| Team can't complete the sprint backlog | Consult the PO on which items to remove; if too many go, the Scrum Master can terminate the sprint |
| Team wants to do more | Ask the PO which product backlog items to add |

**Non-viable sprint**: the Scrum Master may terminate the sprint (at the team's or PO's request) and start a new planning meeting if:

- the technology proves unworkable;
- business conditions change so the sprint has no business value;
- anyone outside the team interferes during the sprint.

**Team responsibilities** — two administrative tasks:

1. Attend the daily scrum.
2. Keep the sprint backlog up to date and publicly visible: add new tasks as they arise and update each task's estimated remaining hours daily.

### Sprint Review And Retrospective

**Sprint review** (≤ 4 hours, ≤ 1 hour of preparation):

- The team presents increments to the PO and stakeholders.
- "Done" means complete and shippable; if "done" means anything else, make sure the PO and stakeholders understand. Anything not done cannot be presented.
- Topics: sprint goal, sprint backlog, completed product backlog items; what went well and what didn't; stakeholder questions; a stakeholder poll on impressions, needs and their priorities.
- The PO then adds and reorders product backlog items.

**Sprint retrospective** (≤ 3 hours; team, Scrum Master and optionally the PO):

1. Each member briefly answers: What went well last sprint? What could be improved next sprint?
2. The Scrum Master summarises the answers.
3. The team prioritises the issues and discusses improvements, facilitated by the Scrum Master.
4. Actionable items can become high-priority non-functional product backlog items.

### Product Backlog And Sprint Backlog

Scrum's artefacts — product backlog, sprint backlog and burndown charts — can be managed in a spreadsheet or cloud tools (consider support for distributed teams).

**Product backlog**: a prioritised list of first-cut refinements. The PO can reorder items and add new ones at any time, which brings agility and change.

| Backlog item | Estimate (person-weeks) |
|---|---|
| Break props into separate rigid bodies | — |
| Arrow projectiles stick to environment | 0.5 |
| Ragdolls can lose limbs | — |
| NPCs avoid dynamite thrown at them | — |
| Improve exception handling | 0.5 |

**Sprint backlog**: what will be "done" during the sprint, each item with an estimate (usually hours). The team must update it throughout. Keeping it current lets you measure the team's **velocity** and gives early warning of trouble.

Example update (Sprint 1): remaining hours fall day by day — 152, 144, 136, 124, 116, 112, 106 — across items such as fixing login-screen formatting, correcting legacy report code, modifying user-file handling, adding network error handling, adding company logos, a de-jitter algorithm, a workflow design change and supervision; 19 days of work in the sprint.

### Burndown Charts

#### Definitions

> **Burndown chart**: A graph of the sprint backlog's remaining hours against sprint days, showing the rate at which work is completed.

- It should show a steady drive to zero hours remaining — a sustainable pace.
- Some work takes longer and some shorter, so it won't be a perfect straight line.
- At the start nothing is done, so the chart starts at the full estimate.
- You can see at a glance whether work is on track.

![Sprint burndown chart](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/04_Scrum/burndown.svg)

*Remaining hours plotted against sprint days; a line staying above the ideal diagonal means the team is behind.*

| Burndown | Likely causes |
|---|---|
| Too slow | Inexperienced developers, hard roadblocks, distractions, bad planning |
| Too fast | Overestimated work (also a planning problem) |

### What Can Go Wrong

| Problem | Description | Remedy |
|---|---|---|
| **Loss of rhythm** | Sprints vary in length, so they feel like arbitrary units chosen by outside forces and are hard to plan | Keep every sprint the same length |
| **Talking chickens** | Chickens ask questions or comment at the daily scrum; allowing useful comments is a slippery slope | Be strict: only pigs speak |
| **Missing pigs** | Flex time and night-working developers mean members miss or are late for daily scrums | Same time and place daily builds rhythm; if many miss often, the meeting is probably too long or straying from the three questions; skipping only for emergencies |
| **Specialised job roles** | Architect, designer, DBA, tester roles undermine "we're all in this together" | Teams needn't be all generalists, but every specialist must accept responsibility for the whole system |
| **Daily scrum for the Scrum Master** | It becomes a status report to the SM, who notes who committed to what and why tasks slipped | The SM is a servant. The scrum coordinates everyone; members commit in front of peers. The SM doesn't rebuke unmet commitments — peers' awareness is enough |
| **Scrum Master assigns work** | Work is assigned instead of signed up for | Self-organisation is fundamental; even occasional assignment undermines developers' responsibility. Teams must feel completely in control of their work |

---

# Project Management

**Subject**: CSC3003S Advanced Programming — Advanced Software Development (ASD)
**Source**: [ASD05-ProjMan.pdf](../../Sources/CSC3003S%20-%20Advanced%20Programming/ASD/ASD05-ProjMan.pdf), Johan Bontes, University of Cape Town, pages 1–47
**Generated**: 2026-11-06

> [!info] <span style="color:#66b3ff">Lecture reference</span>
> Projects versus processes, managing complexity, good design properties, the six design levels (methodology to routines), requirements and scope, planning (SMART goals, deliverables, schedules, the mythical man-month, supporting plans), milestones, and metrics (DORA, KPIs, pitfalls).

---

## Core Content

### Project Versus Process

| Aspect | Process | Project |
|---|---|---|
| Nature | Routine | Ad hoc |
| Duration | Continuous | Fixed start and end |
| Inputs, outputs, tasks | Known and documented | Uncertain |
| Success rate | > 99% | ~50% |
| Emphasis | Small improvements | Creation from scratch |
| Control | Fixed measurements | Outcome versus desires |
| Measurement | \$ input = \$ output | Meets requirements |

Process good, project bad: projects are inherently riskier, which is why they need management.

### What Project Management Is

#### Definitions

> **Project management**: Ensuring software is delivered on time, within budget and as per requirements (at the required quality).

Software development is always subject to budget and time constraints. It needs careful management because of **dependencies, people and organisation**:

- Software artefacts depend on each other, so production must be planned, monitored and coordinated to be efficient, effective and on time.
- Many developers are involved, some with specialised skills needed at different times.
- Activities must follow a sequence — e.g. you can only test components once they're developed, though test scripts and harnesses can be prepared earlier.

### No Silver Bullet: Managing Complexity

> "There is no single technique that fixes the inherent complexity of software design and development." — Fred Brooks, *No Silver Bullet* (1987)

#### Definitions

> **Essential problems**: Difficulties inherent in the problem — requirements and scope.

> **Accidental problems**: Difficulties from how we work — process, tools and people.

- Complexity is the problem, and it must be managed. Computer science uniquely spans about $10^{13}$ orders of magnitude.
- Ineffective designs come from:
  - a complex solution to a simple problem;
  - a simple, faulty solution to a complex problem;
  - a complex, faulty solution to a complex problem.

**Dijkstra: manage complexity.** All other goals are secondary.

- **Minimise essential complexity** held in your head at any one time:
  - decompose the problem into simple pieces,
  - keep coupling loose,
  - use clean interfaces with no hidden gotchas.
- **Keep accidental complexity from proliferating**: tools (language) and frameworks.

### Good Design Properties

| Property | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Minimal complexity | Easy to understand |
| Ease of maintenance | Documented and understandable |
| Loose coupling | Interfaces using abstraction, encapsulation and information hiding |
| Extensible | Easy to extend |
| Reusable | **DRY** — don't repeat yourself |
| High fan-in | Low-level classes are reused often |
| Low fan-out | A high-level class or function uses few lower-level ones |
| Leanness | "Make everything as simple as possible, but not simpler" |
| Stratified | A consistent view at every level of decomposition |
| Standardised | Uses common, proven patterns |

Project management tools (many exist) can be mixed and matched for the capstone project.

### The Design Levels

![Design levels](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/05A_Project_Management/design-levels.svg)

*Design proceeds from choosing a methodology down through project, subsystems, classes and routines to source code.*

| Level | Focus |
|---|---|
| 0 | Methodology |
| 1 | Project: requirements, scope, architecture |
| 2 | Subsystems |
| 3 | Classes / packages |
| 4 | Routines: methods and data in classes |
| 5 | Routine design and source code |

### Level 0: Pick A Methodology

#### Definitions

> **SDLC (software development life cycle)**: The overall sequence of phases a methodology follows.

Options include the **Unified Process** and **waterfall** (Royce, 1970): system requirements → software requirements → analyse → design → code → test → deploy.

### Level 1: Requirements And Scope

**Project requirements** describe:

- the functions and features delivered to end users;
- the data input and output;
- the content presented to users as they use the software;
- the performance, constraints, external interfaces and reliability that bound the system.

**Scope** is defined by:

| Means | Description |
|---|---|
| Narrative descriptions | Written after communicating with stakeholders |
| **Use cases** | Scenario-based descriptions of the user's interaction, from the user's point of view |
| Requirements | Functionality, performance, … |
| Constraints | Limits: external hardware, memory, existing infrastructure, time and budget |

**Why prepare?**

- **Reduce risk**: more preparation, greater chance of success.
- **Efficiency**: 80% of development time is spent finding and fixing bugs (McConnell, *Code Complete*, 2004).

### Stakeholders And Feasibility

#### Definitions

> **Stakeholder**: Someone impacted by the project — the sponsor, the client for the deliverables, system users, the project manager and team.

Goals (scope) derive from stakeholders' needs and expectations. Find the **true needs** that create real benefits, and prioritise them.

![Stakeholder onion](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/05A_Project_Management/stakeholder-onion.svg)

*Rings closer to the product are more directly affected: users and operators, then the business (management, sales, legal, purchasing), then the wider environment (regulators, media, suppliers, the public).*

**Feasibility**: given the scope, determine the resources required — and answer "Do we have the resources to build this?" Resources are not just money:

- people,
- hardware and tools,
- reusable components,
- time and money.

### Why Explicit Requirements?

- They let the **client**, not the programmer, drive functionality.
- The client can review explicit requirements; they can't review code.
- Changing requirements later is more expensive.
- Specifying requirements is key to success; **stable requirements are the holy grail** — no cost overruns.
- Make sure users know the cost of requirement changes.
- Use an approach that allows for change: a change control procedure, or Agile/evolutionary delivery.

### Requirements Checklist

Have you specified:

| Functional | Quality (non-functional) — see ISO 25010 |
|---|---|
| All inputs and all outputs | Response time (latency) |
| File formats | Performance |
| Hardware and software interfaces | Security |
| External interfaces (including errors and protocols) | Reliability |
| All user tasks | Resource use (memory, storage) |
| User interface | Maintainability |
| Data into and out of user tasks | Definition of success/failure (benchmark) |

Are the requirements:

| Complete | Correct |
|---|---|
| Missing information at the start documented? | Written in the users' language, and do users agree? |
| If the program satisfies them, will it be accepted? | No conflicting requirements |
| Realistic and relevant — impossible and nonsense requirements removed? | Acceptable trade-offs for competing requirements (e.g. speed vs quality) |
| | Detailed enough, and consistently so |
| | Understandable to coders, and do coders agree? |
| | Relevant and testable |
| | Adaptable — possible changes specified |

### Architecture Checklist

Architecture decisions with system-wide implications:

- programming languages and tools;
- program organisation into subsystems;
- major classes;
- data design;
- business rules;
- user interface;
- resources (database, memory, threads);
- security, performance, scalability;
- I/O;
- error processing and fault tolerance;
- buy versus build (frameworks).

### Levels 2–5: Subsystems, Classes And Routines

**Level 2 — subsystems**:

- Plan subsystems as self-contained units that can be assigned to different people.
- Pick frameworks (buy vs build).
- Plan interactions based on a hierarchy.

![Subsystem interactions](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/05A_Project_Management/subsystems.svg)

*Left: unrestricted interactions and high fan-out make a complex system. Right: rule-based, hierarchical interactions with low fan-out keep it simple.*

**Level 3 — classes**: divide each subsystem into classes and packages.

**Level 4 — routines**: divide classes into routines. The steps are iterative and can be done out of order:

1. Design object attributes: methods and data.
2. Decide what can be done to each object.
3. Design interactions with other objects.
4. Partition into private, protected and public parts.
5. Define the public interface.

This depends on paradigm: OOP models real-world objects; data-oriented design (DOD) models the program's data flows.

**Level 5 — routines and data**:

- Check requirements (performance, functionality).
- Give it a good name in **verbObject** form, so the name documents behaviour; follow a naming convention.
- Add necessary documentation, especially requirements.
- Follow design patterns (language-dependent).
- Design parameters and return values (const correctness).
- Design invariants and checks (assertions, exceptions) to defend against bad input:
  - **pre-conditions** (inputs, the object's start state),
  - **post-conditions** (outputs, the object's end state),
  - assertions.
- Implementation (later in some methodologies) — start with pseudocode.

### Project Planning: Goals, Deliverables And Schedule

**Goals** should be **SMART**:

| Letter | Goal setting |
|---|---|
| S | Specific |
| M | Measurable |
| A | Agreed upon |
| R | Realistic |
| T | Time-based |

**Deliverables** are the items that must be delivered to meet the goals.

- Verifiable and specific (e.g. a report, executable code).
- Each has a stakeholder who needs it and a delivery date.
- Each has quality standards, e.g. the software runs; unit-test coverage; zero compiler warnings at a high warning level; documents meet a specified format.

**Schedule**: the effort (time), people and other resources required. Updating the deliverables updates the schedule. If the schedule is unrealistic, either:

1. delay the deadline;
2. add resources — more expensive, and see the mythical man-month; or
3. reduce scope (fewer deliverables) — the only option that makes sense for the capstone.

### The Mythical Man-Month

#### Definitions

> **The mythical man-month** (Brooks, 1975): In theory adding designers reduces completion time; in reality productivity per designer falls because of team management and communication overheads, and at some point adding people lengthens the project ("too many cooks").

Example: a 1-million-transistor design. One designer produces 5000 transistors/month, and each additional designer reduces everyone's output by 100/month (so 2 designers produce 4900 each).

$$
\text{individual output}(n) = 5000 - 100(n-1), \qquad \text{team output}(n) = n\,\big(5100 - 100n\big)
$$

$$
\text{months to finish} = \frac{1\,000\,000}{\text{team output}(n)}
$$

| Designers $n$ | ≈5 | ≈10 | ≈15 | ≈20 | ≈25 | ≈30 | ≈35 | ≈40 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Months to finish | 43 | 24 | 19 | 16 | 15 | 16 | 18 | 23 |

![The mythical man-month](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/05A_Project_Management/mythical-man-month.svg)

*Team output peaks at about 25 designers and then falls, so completion time rises again; individual output falls steadily.*

### Supporting Plans And Management Activities

| Plan | Content |
|---|---|
| **Human resource plan** | Key staff and organisations: skills, roles and responsibilities; number and type of people; start dates, durations and how to get them |
| **Communications plan** | Who must be kept updated and how: weekly review meetings, progress reports and demos, revised schedules |
| **Risk management plan** | Identify as many risks as possible and treat each by **ARTA**: Accept, Reduce, Transfer or Avoid |

Management activities cover the four Ps:

| Area | Concerns |
|---|---|
| **People** | Project managers, team leaders, software team, customers, end users |
| **Product** | Scope, requirements, decomposition |
| **Process** | Start with a skateboard and refine iteratively; SDLC, Unified Process |
| **Project** | Resources, scheduling, risks, tracking |
| **Price** | Budget, cost control, bookkeeping |

### Milestones And Deliverables

#### Definitions

> **Activity**: A task that takes time, with a duration and a due date.

> **Precursor**: An activity that others depend on, so it must precede them.

> **Milestone**: The completion of an activity, with a recognisable end product (e.g. handing over a system for testing). It needs a formal, measurable output — "coding 80% complete" is not adequate.

> **Deliverable**: A project result that is delivered to the customer.

### DORA Metrics

#### Definitions

> **DORA metrics**: Four delivery-performance metrics from the DevOps Research and Assessment programme.

| Metric | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **Change lead time** | Time to implement, test and deliver code for a feature, from first commit to deployment |
| **Deployment frequency** | Number of deployments in a given period |
| **Change failure rate** | Percentage of changes that fail, out of all changes |
| **Mean time to recovery (MTTR)** | Time to restore service after a production failure |

Criticisms:

- They can't be used to compare teams: complexity, expertise and subject matter differ.
- They don't address business risk. You must do a risk analysis (hard), track risk indicators, and reduce risk through measurement-based actions.

DORA culture recommendations:

- support a **generative culture**;
- encourage and support learning;
- support collaboration among teams;
- provide resources and tools that make work meaningful;
- support or embody transformational leadership.

### KPIs, Productivity And Code Quality Metrics

#### Definitions

> **KPI (key performance indicator)**: A measurable value showing how effectively a team is achieving its objectives.

| Category | Metric | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| KPIs | Throughput | Tasks or user stories completed per sprint |
| | Cycle time | Average time from issue creation to resolution |
| | Bug fix rate | How quickly identified defects are resolved |
| | Release frequency | Cadence of production deployments |
| | Customer-reported defects | Post-release issues, showing testing effectiveness |
| Productivity and velocity | Velocity | Story points completed per sprint, showing consistency |
| | Burndown charts | Visual progress toward sprint goals |
| | Pull request metrics | Time to review, merge and deploy changes |
| | Commit frequency | Commits per developer — use carefully to assess engagement |
| | Rework percentage | Share of time spent fixing or redoing earlier work |
| Code quality | Code coverage | Percentage of code tested by automated tests |
| | Cyclomatic complexity | How complex the logic paths are |
| | Defect density | Defects per thousand lines of code (KLOC) |
| | Code churn | Frequency of code changes — often a sign of instability or unclear requirements |
| | Technical debt | The cost of delayed refactoring or poor design choices |

### Metric Pitfalls And SMART Metrics

| Pitfall | Description |
|---|---|
| Non-metrics | Lines of code, commit counts, hours worked |
| Over-measurement | Poor signal-to-noise ratio; bureaucratic overhead |
| Stack ranking | Fire the bottom $x\%$ on metric $y$, promote the top $x\%$, hire to fill the gap |
| Ignoring context | Complex work takes longer; quantification problems |

- **Many metrics are relative**: absolute values may be meaningless. What matters is improvement over time — as long as we're improving, we're winning.
- **Gaming the system** happens. A **moral hazard** arises when the wrong incentives create harmful outcomes.

Metrics should also be SMART:

| Letter | Question |
|---|---|
| Specific | What exactly is an indicator of success? |
| Measurable | Can you physically track the KPI? |
| Achievable | Are the KPIs realistic? |
| Relevant | Do the KPIs reflect the business's overall goals? |
| Time-framed | When should the KPIs be met? |

SMART metrics take the form of counts, percentages, sums or totals, averages, and ratios.

---

# Data-Oriented Design

**Subject**: CSC3003S Advanced Programming — Advanced Software Development (ASD)
**Source**: [ASD05 Data oriented design.pdf](../../Sources/CSC3003S%20-%20Advanced%20Programming/ASD/ASD05%20Data%20oriented%20design.pdf) (identical to [ASD05 Data oriented design - Copy.pdf](../../Sources/CSC3003S%20-%20Advanced%20Programming/ASD/ASD05%20Data%20oriented%20design%20-%20Copy.pdf) apart from slide dates), Johan Bontes, University of Cape Town, pages 1–56
**Generated**: 2026-11-06

> [!info] <span style="color:#66b3ff">Lecture reference</span>
> Why object-oriented code is slow on modern CPUs (memory bandwidth, caches, heap scattering, virtual calls), the three steps of data-oriented design shown on a Pacman example, and how CPU pipelining, prediction, superscalar and out-of-order execution interact with code structure.

---

## Core Content

### What Data-Oriented Design Is

#### Definitions

> **Data-oriented design (DOD)**: A technique for speeding up data processing by organising code around how data flows through the CPU, rather than around real-world objects. It requires thinking like a CPU: what slows it down, and what speeds it up?

**Rule 1: if it ain't broke, don't fix it.**

- DOD is all about data — **lots** of data. No lots of data, no DOD.
- Consider using a database instead.

Where DOD is used — anywhere with lots of uniform data to process, when a database can't be used:

- inside database engines,
- games,
- numerical simulation (number crunching),
- solvers.

Database techniques are moving into game engines (e.g. SpacetimeDB).

### The Pacman Case Study

The lecture compares two Pacman implementations.

![Pacman: OOP versus DOD](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/05B_Data_Oriented_Design/pacman-oop-vs-dod.svg)

*The OOP design scatters each doll's data and behaviour across a class hierarchy of heap objects; the DOD design keeps the hot data in a few flat arrays processed by static code.*

- The traditional OOP design is about **20 000× slower than needed** in its hot path.
- Parts of the OOP design that are not "hot" are not a problem.

### Why OOP Mapping Is Slow

Traditional OOP courses say:

- map real-world entities onto objects, and
- solve issues with more objects.

The problems:

| Issue | Consequence |
|---|---|
| The real world is complex, so 1-to-1 mapping creates **blob objects** | Large, unfocused objects |
| "Correct" OOP mapping uses lots of **indirection** | Extra work for the CPU: slow |
| Objects live on the **heap** | Scattered in memory |
| Objects mix **hot and cold** data | Cold data pollutes the cache |
| Objects mix **read-only and read-write** data | Harder to cache and parallelise |

### Feeding The CPU: Memory Bandwidth

A CPU is a **Von Neumann machine**: code and data are fetched from memory into the processor.

A 5 GHz core can process about

$$
5\text{G} \times 32 \times 4 \approx 600\ \text{GiB/s per core}
$$

| Source | Bandwidth | Relative to CPU demand |
|---|---|---|
| CPU core demand | ~600 GiB/s | — |
| DDR5-5600, sequential | ~70 GiB/s | ~10× slower |
| DDR5-5600, random access | ~400 MiB/s | ~1500× slower |
| 8-core CPU demand | $600 \times 8 = 4.8$ TiB/s | — |

Memory cannot keep the CPU fed, especially with random access.

### The Cache Hierarchy

#### Definitions

> **Hot data**: A small amount of data used over and over — lookup tables, a database index, sprite coordinates, local variables on the stack, scratchpads, data inside a loop.

> **Cache**: Small, fast memory close to the CPU that holds the hottest data.

Most time is spent on hot data, so the hottest data is kept closest to the CPU (figures for AMD Zen 5):

| Level | Size | Bandwidth |
|---|---|---|
| "L0": register file + micro-op cache | 16 KiB / ~24 KiB | ~600 GiB/s |
| L1 data / L1 code | 48 KiB / 32 KiB | ~200 GiB/s |
| L2 (per core, data + code) | 1 MiB | ~20 GiB/s |
| L3 (shared) | 32 MiB | ~2 GiB/s |
| RAM (shared) | Big | ~0.4 GiB/s (best case, random) |

![Cache hierarchy](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/05B_Data_Oriented_Design/cache-hierarchy.svg)

*Each level is larger and slower than the one above; the CPU only works on what reaches the register file and micro-op cache.*

The CPU has a small "notepad" for data — the register file, about 500 variables — and a small "screen" for code — about 6000 micro-ops.

### Cache Rules And Eviction

**The CPU cannot use RAM directly.**

- Data can be used only once it is in the register file; code only once it is in the micro-op cache.
- New data in means old data out.
- Data moves in and out of the CPU via L1, into L1 via L2, into L2 via L3.
- The closer to the CPU, the hotter the data. More re-used and more recent data is hotter.

#### Definitions

> **Eviction scheme**: The rule for which cached data to discard when new data arrives.

| Era | Scheme |
|---|---|
| 2000s | **LRU** — least recently used (delete the oldest) |
| 2010s | **LFU** — least frequently used (delete the coldest) |
| Modern | A tiny neural network mixing LRU and LFU |

Modern CPUs also **predict** which data and code will be needed next and fetch it speculatively (**speculative prefetching**, which has security implications). Tip: make your data use **predictable**.

### Access Patterns: Sequential Versus Random

```java
final int GB = 1024 * 1024 * 1024;
int[] array = new int[1 * GB];

// A: sequential
for (var a : array) {
    doStuff(a);
}

// B: random
var r = new Random();
for (var i = 0; i < GB; i++) {
    var i2 = r.nextInt(GB);
    var a = array[i2];
    doStuff(a);
}
```

Sequential access lets the prefetcher stream data; random access pays the full RAM latency on almost every read.

Java objects add a twist:

```java
int[]     array  = new int[1 * GB];      // 4 GiB
Integer[] array2 = new Integer[1 * GB];  // ~48 GiB after initialisation
for (var a : array2) {                   // effectively random access!
    doStuff(a);
}
```

- `Integer[]` holds **pointers**; every `Integer` is a separate heap object with a header and padding, which is why it balloons to about 48 GiB.
- Looping over it is effectively random access, because each element must be fetched from wherever its object landed on the heap.

### Anatomy Of An Object And The Heap

```java
ObjectX a;          // local variable: on the stack (always in L1)
a = new ObjectX();  // the ObjectX itself is created on the heap
```

- The local variable `a` is a **link (pointer)** of 8 bytes to the heap object.
- The heap is the object store in main memory.
- An object is data plus code:

```text
ObjectX = struct {
    VMT:     'this' pointer to the virtual method table (list of code)
    Data:    data members
    padding: empty space to make the size a multiple of 8 bytes
}
```

#### Definitions

> **Heap**: The memory area for dynamically allocated objects. It has four lists (tiny, small, medium and large objects); allocation is first come, first served, and destruction happens at random times (garbage collection).

> **Virtual method table (VMT)**: A per-class table of pointers to the class's (overridable) methods, reached through a pointer in each object.

Because objects are allocated in arrival order and freed at random times, they end up **randomly distributed** through memory. Heap bookkeeping also takes space.

### Object Problem 1: Living On The Heap

- An object's data and code are spread randomly over memory.
- Related objects are not stored sequentially, and the longer the program runs, the more randomly spaced they become.
- This adds about a **1500-cycle start-up penalty** to each object access — time in which the core could have performed about 6000 calculations.
- Delays are much worse in multi-threaded code.

### Object Problem 2: Virtual Methods

```java
Sprite[] dolls = new Sprite[5];
for (var i = 1; i <= 4; i++) { dolls[i] = new Ghost(); }
dolls[0] = new Pacman();
// ... game loop
dolls[i].moveto(x, y);
```

1. Look up the correct doll on the heap.
2. `moveto = doll.VMT[42]` — a pointer to code.
3. Call `moveto` — the call **cannot be predicted**: it might be a ghost, Pacman or a zombie, which all move differently.

A deeper chain:

```java
public void Ghost.draw() {
    bitmap = getScaryBitmap();
    super.draw();
}
public void MovingSprite.draw() {
    location = point(x, y);
    super.draw();
}
public void Sprite.draw() {
    display.draw(bitmap, location);
}
```

`dolls[i].draw()` then performs:

1. Look up the doll on the heap.
2. `draw = doll.VMT[41]`; call `Ghost.draw()` (unpredictable).
3. `parent = doll.VMT[0]`; `draw = parent.VMT[41]`; call `MovingSprite.draw()` (maybe predicted).
4. `grandparent = parent.VMT[0]`; `draw = grandparent.VMT[41]`; call `Sprite.draw()` (maybe predicted).
5. Look up the `display` object on the heap; `draw = display.VMT[125]`; call `Display.draw(bitmap, location)` (maybe predicted).
6. Look up the `bitmap` object on the heap.

![Virtual call chain](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/05B_Data_Oriented_Design/virtual-dispatch.svg)

*One `draw()` call chases pointers through several objects and VMTs, each a potential cache miss.*

Every lookup can evict useful data from L1 (**data cache eviction**, problem 2a), and every indirect call can evict code from the L1 code / micro-op cache (**code cache eviction**, problem 2b).

### Why Cache Lines Cause Eviction

- The minimum load size for data is 32 bytes on AMD/Intel. Even if you ask for 1 byte, 32 bytes are fetched, so 32 bytes of other data are evicted.
- Why? The hardware can fetch 256 bits in parallel, 32× faster than one byte at a time.
- Every CPU speed hack has a drawback elsewhere.

### Stack Versus Heap And Polymorphism

In Java, everything created with `new` lives on the heap, giving layers of indirection:

1. `ClassX myobject = new ClassX();` — `myobject` is a link to heap data.
2. All non-static methods are accessed indirectly (through the VMT).
3. Object fields such as `Bear bear;` are links to further heap objects.

| | Stack | Heap |
|---|---|---|
| Access | Direct | Indirect |
| Contents | Local variables and links to objects | Object data |
| Speed | Fast (effectively 0 time) | Slow: up to 1500 cycles |
| Size | Tiny | Huge |
| Typing | Literal | Polymorphic |

#### Definitions

> **Run-time polymorphism (late binding)**: The method called is chosen at run time via the VMT. Very flexible, but up to 1500 cycles per access, and the CPU can't predict it.

> **Compile-time polymorphism (early binding)**: The method called is fixed at compile time (e.g. C++ templates). Almost as flexible, but up to 1000× faster; not easy to use (better in C++23).

Java only has run-time polymorphism; C++ has both.

### DOD Step 1: Find The Hot Code

Split the program into hot and cold code:

| | Hot code | Cold code |
|---|---|---|
| Where | Always in a loop | Rarely executed |
| Frequency | Many times per second | Occasionally |
| Effect of more code/objects | Slower program | No measurable effect |

Unless you are 100% sure, **measure** where the bottlenecks are:

- **Instrumenting profilers** — Java: Java Flight Recorder, VisualVM, Async Profiler; C++: various (hackingcpp.com lists them); Python: pyinstrument.
- A simple **timer**:

```text
start = hiresTimer.microsecs();
// dostuff()
end = hiresTimer.microsecs();
duration: uint64 += end - start;
```

### DOD Step 2: Analyse The Data Flow

Pacman's hot loop runs on a timer 60 times per second:

1. Find where the dolls (Pacman and ghosts) are.
2. Check collisions between Pacman and ghosts.
3. Check collisions between Pacman and pills/walls.
4. Try to move the dolls one space.
5. Animate the dolls and draw the grid.

The data involved: positions, bitmaps and the grid. With thousands of ghosts, you might pull the AI into the hot path too.

### DOD Step 3: Data That Flows Together Stays Together

This is a creative process.

- **Arrays are your friend.** In Java use arrays of simple types (`char`, `int`, `float`) and fewer objects (`Integer`, `String`, `ObjectX`). Abusing a `String` as an array (as in the Pacman demo) is fine.
- **Use only static methods**: pass the data as the first parameter, or make the data static (arrays of stuff).
- **C++**: use classes but avoid `virtual`; templates give compile-time polymorphism.
- **Python**: use lists of simple types; avoid sets and dictionaries.

```java
public class GameBoard {
    // everything static, few Objects
    public static final int PM_id = 0;
    public static final int GCOUNT = 4;
    private static int  position[GCOUNT+1][2];       // 0 = Pacman, 1..4 = ghosts; x,y
    private static char grid[GRID_SX][GRID_SY];
    private static char bitmaps[GCOUNT+1][BITMAP_SIZE];
    private static char doll_state[GCOUNT+1];
    private static int  pillsLeft;
    private static int  PM_PowerUpTimeLeft;
    private static AI   GameAI;                       // access to normal objects
    public static void moveto(int doll_id, int x, int y);
    public static void draw(int doll_id);
}
```

Put dynamic data in another object, not inside the DOD object.

**What's in a Java object?** Mostly links, not much data.

| Directly included | Indirectly linked |
|---|---|
| Simple types (`int`, `double`, …) | Any `Object` |
| Arrays of simple types | Non-static (virtual) code |
| `enum` | |
| Static code | |

This isn't very flexible when different dolls behave differently. Solutions:

| Language | Technique |
|---|---|
| C++ | Variant objects: `class Doll { boolean isGhost; /* data */ }`, or `std::variant` (fully polymorphic) |
| Java | Tag bits in an index: `isGhost = dollIdx & 1; dollId = dollIdx >> 1;` |

### DOD Speed-Ups And Benefits

Static methods avoid indirect code lookup:

$$
20 \times 1500 = 30\text{K cycles} \times 5 \text{ dolls} = 150\text{K cycles}, \qquad \frac{150\text{K}}{5\text{ GHz}} \approx 30\ \mu\text{s per frame}
$$

In practice more like ~300 µs per frame because of other slowdowns. That's not worth it for Pacman as such, but with many ghosts the frame rate could drop below playable.

| Technique | Speed-up |
|---|---|
| DOD | up to 1 000 000× |
| Data parallelism | 1 000× |
| Combined | up to 1 000 000 000× |

Other benefits:

- Data flow is much easier to follow; code and its data flow are in one place.
- You can still use sub-methods; static calls are effectively free.
- The CPU doesn't pull in unrelated data.

With traditional OOP, Pacman/ghost code is spread over many objects, and all member data of every accessed object is pulled in even if never used, causing more cache eviction.

### CPU Code Flow: Pipelining And Prediction

#### Definitions

> **Pipelining**: Overlapping the stages (fetch, decode, execute, …) of successive instructions, so linear code completes one instruction every cycle.

> **Branch prediction**: The CPU guesses which way a branch goes and speculatively fetches and executes code as if the guess were right.

![Pipelining](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/05B_Data_Oriented_Design/pipeline.svg)

*Linear code keeps the pipeline full; a jump to unpredicted code empties it and stalls for the pipeline's length.*

| Code shape | Effect |
|---|---|
| Linear | One instruction per cycle |
| Jump (mispredicted) | Pipeline empties; stall for length(pipeline) cycles |

Speculation:

- **Correct guess**: commit the work.
- **Wrong guess**: roll back, empty the pipeline, fetch the other code.
- The CPU also prefetches data speculatively. If wrong, it may evict the wrong data, have to re-fetch evicted data, and wait for the correct data.

OOP is a poor fit:

| OOP feature | CPU effect |
|---|---|
| Virtual methods | Essentially random to the CPU; can't be predicted; often not in L1 code cache; costly waits |
| Heap-based objects | Chaotic random locations; the CPU can't predict data flow; very costly L1/L2/L3 misses |

### Superscalar And Out-Of-Order Execution

#### Definitions

> **Superscalar execution**: The CPU executes independent instructions at the same time.

> **Out-of-order (OoO) execution**: The CPU analyses code with a dependency graph and reorders independent instructions to keep execution units busy.

```c
a = b + 10;   // a depends on b
c = d * 11;   // c depends on d, not on a or b
```

These two lines can run at the same time (superscalar), though both use the same pipeline.

Out-of-order example:

```c
1: a = b + 10;  // depends on b
2: a += d;      // must wait for line 1
3: c = d * 11;  // depends on d only
4: c += b;      // must wait for line 3
```

The CPU reorders this to 1, 3, 2, 4. A 2-way superscalar CPU executes 1+3 together, then 2+4.

![Dependency graph](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/05B_Data_Oriented_Design/dependency-graph.svg)

*Lines 1 and 3 are independent and can run together; lines 2 and 4 each wait for their own predecessor.*

### The Stack Engine And The Compiler

**Stack engine** (since Intel P6b, 2003): modern CPUs simulate the stack in CPU registers.

| | Old CPUs | New CPUs |
|---|---|---|
| Stack bookkeeping | Has overhead | Takes zero time |
| Local variables | Some cost | Zero access time |

C++ objects live on the stack by default; for smallish objects (< 8 KB) access takes zero time, even random access.

**What about the compiler?**

| CPU | Compiler |
|---|---|
| Sees instructions **and** current data | Sees only a static picture of the code |
| Builds a better local dependency graph | Can't make assumptions, or subtle bugs would appear |
| Uses a neural network for prediction, trained as the program runs | Skips many seemingly obvious optimisations |
| Knows things the compiler cannot | Can't see data; global dependency tracking is NP-hard |

---

# Software Testing

**Subject**: CSC3003S Advanced Programming — Advanced Software Development (ASD)
**Source**: [ASD08 Software Testing.pdf](../../Sources/CSC3003S%20-%20Advanced%20Programming/ASD/ASD08%20Software%20Testing.pdf), Johan Bontes (with credit to Sarina Till), University of Cape Town, pages 1–45
**Generated**: 2026-11-06

> [!info] <span style="color:#66b3ff">Lecture reference</span>
> Defensive programming (const correctness, validation, assertions, error handling, resource management), automated testing (static analysis, sanitizers, fuzzing), unit tests, TDD and mocking, integration, regression and user acceptance testing, and security testing.

---

## Core Content

### Types Of Software Testing

There is no silver bullet: no single kind of testing catches everything.

![Types of software testing](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/08_Software_Testing/testing-types.svg)

*Functional testing checks what the system does; non-functional testing checks how well it does it.*

| Functional testing | Non-functional testing |
|---|---|
| Unit testing | Documentation testing |
| Integration testing | Installation testing |
