| Interface testing | Performance testing — load, stress, scalability, volume |
| System testing | Reliability testing |
| Regression testing | Security testing |
| User acceptance testing | |

### Defensive Programming Overview

#### Definitions

> **Defensive programming**: Writing code that protects itself against misuse and bad input, so errors are caught early and close to their cause.

| Technique | Example |
|---|---|
| Const correctness | `void setName(const string& newName); string getName() const;` |
| Input validation | `if (uid >= size(Users)) {...}`, `if (newName == "") {...}` |
| Assertions | `assert(Users); assert(isValid(Users));` |
| Error handling | `try { internalSetName(uid, newName); } catch ...` |
| Resource management | `finally { file.close(); }` or RAII |

### Const Correctness

#### Definitions

> **Const correctness**: Marking what must not change (methods, objects, parameters) as constant so the compiler enforces it.

| Language | Mechanism |
|---|---|
| C++, Rust, etc. | `const` methods can't change their object; `const` objects can't be changed; `const` parameters are fixed in the body |
| Java | Annotations from JSR 305/308 with the Checker Framework: `@ReadOnly`, `@Immutable`, `@Mutable` |

```cpp
string Person::getName() const { return name; }

const auto person = new Person();
person.getName();        // succeeds
person.setName("test");  // does not compile

void doStuff(const int uid) {
    uid++;                  // does not compile
    auto nextId = uid + 1;  // fine
}
```

```java
public String getName(@ReadOnly Person this) { return name; }

@ReadOnly Person person = new Person();
person.getName();        // succeeds
person.setName("test");  // does not compile
```

### Input Validation

Input validation **must run in release code** — unlike assertions.

| Check | Example |
|---|---|
| Input within bounds | `if (id < size(P)) {...}` |
| Object valid | `if (P && P.isOpen()) {...}` |
| Container big enough | `if (size(A) >= size(input)) {...}` |
| No SQL injection | `sqlSafe = sanitize(sql);` — better, use **parameterised queries** |
| No HTML injection | `displayTxt = sanitize(userTxt);` |

- Never display raw user input; always sanitise display text.
- Escaping is hard: never roll your own; use standard solutions.

### Assertions And Design By Contract

#### Definitions

> **Assertion**: A check for a condition that should **never** occur in production code. It runs only in debug builds and can be as slow as you like.

> **Design by contract**: Specifying each routine's pre-conditions and post-conditions as checked assertions (Meyer, 1997; contracts are coming to C++26).

- Assertions are removed in release builds, so **never put action code in an assertion**: `assert(DoAction());` would stop calling `DoAction` in release.
- Use them for pre-conditions and post-conditions:

```text
func Velocity(lat, lon, height: float): float;
    assert(-90 <= lat and lat <= 90);          // pre
    assert(0 <= lon and lon < 360);
    assert(-500 <= height and height <= 75000);
    ...
    assert(0 < result and result <= 600);      // post
    return result;
```

A failing assertion can abort and lose the debug state. If so, wrap it in the same condition so a breakpoint preserves program state:

```text
#ifdef _DEBUG
if (not(-90 <= lat and lat <= 90)) {   // exactly the same condition
    assert(-90 <= lat and lat <= 90);   // breakpoint here keeps state
}
```

| | Input validation | Assertions |
|---|---|---|
| Runs in release? | Yes | No (debug only) |
| Guards against | Bad external input | Programmer errors — "impossible" states |
| Cost | Must be cheap | Can be slow |

### Error Handling And Exceptions

There are "2½ schools of thought":

| Approach | Happy path | Error path | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| Exceptions | Zero overhead | Lots of overhead | — |
| Error codes | Little overhead | Little overhead | Much harder to get right |
| Handle errors on the spot | — | — | Needs a crystal ball |

```java
try {
    a = file.getData();
    ...
} catch (SomeException e) {
    // never silently drop errors ("ignore it")
    // do something useful:
    editBox.textColor = clRed;
} finally {
    file.close();
}
```

- Never silently swallow errors.
- Don't leak exceptions to the user. Alan Cooper (*About Face*): error messages are idiotic and should never be used. Instead, **prevent users from making errors** in the first place — a slider cannot be in error, but a text box can.

### Resource Management And Compiler Warnings

Resources must be released even when errors occur:

```java
String readFile(String name) {
    File file = new File(name);
    try { /* do stuff with file */ }
    finally { file.close(); }
}
```

```cpp
void rF(const string& name) {
    auto file = ifstream(name);
    // do stuff with file
}   // RAII: ifstream destroyed, file closed automatically
```

#### Definitions

> **RAII (Resource Acquisition Is Initialisation)**: Tying a resource's lifetime to an object's scope, so the destructor releases it automatically.

**Bonus check: compiler warnings are your friend.** Enable all warnings:

| Language | Setting |
|---|---|
| C++ (MSVC) | `/W4` |
| C++ (Clang) | `-Wall -Wextra -Wpedantic -Weverything` |
| C++ (GCC) | `-Wall -Wextra` |
| Python | `import warnings; warnings.simplefilter('always')` |
| Java | IDE-specific, e.g. NetBeans: Project properties → Build → Compiling → Show warnings; Tools → Options → Java → Compiler → Warnings = All |

### Static Analysis

#### Definitions

> **Static analysis**: Automatically examining source code without running it.

> **Code smell**: A sign of bad programming practice (a known antipattern), e.g. duplicated or complex code, poor naming, global variables (Fowler, *Refactoring*, 1999).

**Lint** (S. Johnson, 1978) is the classic tool, and is especially useful for Python. Static analysers check for:

- **smells** — known antipatterns;
- **bugs** — code matching known bug patterns, such as infinite loops or division by zero.

Formal proofs of correctness are NP-hard, so they are rarely feasible.

### Code Sanitizers

#### Definitions

> **Sanitizer**: A run-time checker that instruments source code (or injects into compiled code) to detect classes of bugs as the program runs. Introduced by Google in 2012 with AddressSanitizer.

| Sanitizer | Detects |
|---|---|
| AddressSanitizer | Dangling pointers, reading past the end, use after free |
| MemorySanitizer | Uninitialised memory |
| UBSan | Undefined behaviour |
| LeakSanitizer | Memory allocated but never freed |
| ThreadSanitizer | Deadlocks, data races |

### Fuzzing

#### Definitions

> **Fuzzing**: Feeding randomised input into a program to check its error handling, see whether it misbehaves, and find security problems (Miller et al., 1990).

- Can be **black-box** (no knowledge of internals) or **white-box** (uses code structure).
- Tools exist for most languages: Python — `atheris`; Java — Jazzer; C++ — libFuzzer.

### Unit Tests

#### Definitions

> **Unit test**: A test of a small section of code — often a class or function — in isolation.

> **Test coverage**: How much is exercised by tests: all code (measurable automatically) or all possible inputs (hard).

- Frameworks: Java — JUnit; Python — `unittest`; C++ — Catch2.
- Tests live in a separate module, typically one test file per file under test.
- The goal is **100% test coverage**. Python's `coverage` tool measures it.

```cpp
#include <catch2/catch_test_macros.hpp>

unsigned factorial(const unsigned n) {
    assert(n < 13);                // 13! overflows a 32-bit unsigned
    if (n <= 1) { return n; }      // bug: 0! should be 1
    else return factorial(n) * n;  // code smell — should be factorial(n-1)
}

TEST_CASE("Factorials are computed", "[factorial]") {
    REQUIRE(factorial(1)  == 1);
    REQUIRE(factorial(2)  == 2);
    REQUIRE(factorial(3)  == 6);
    REQUIRE(factorial(10) == 3'628'800);
    REQUIRE(factorial(13) == 6'227'020'800);  // fails
}
```

A failed test reports the expression and its expansion:

```text
test.cpp:18: FAILED:
  REQUIRE( binary_search(arr, 176) == 0 )
with expansion:
  -1 == 0
test cases: 1 | 1 failed
assertions: 5 | 4 passed | 1 failed
```

### Test-Driven Development

#### Definitions

> **Test-driven development (TDD)**: Writing tests **before** the code they test, then writing just enough code to make them pass.

![TDD cycle](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/08_Software_Testing/tdd-cycle.svg)

*Stub the code, write a failing test, implement until it passes, then add tests before each new piece of code.*

1. Write the code's interface with no implementation:
```cpp
unsigned factorial(const unsigned n) { return n; }
```
2. Write a test for it — it fails:
```cpp
TEST_CASE("Do factorials", "[factorial]") {
    REQUIRE(factorial(1)  == 1);
    REQUIRE(factorial(2)  == 2);
    REQUIRE(factorial(3)  == 6);
    REQUIRE(factorial(10) == 3'628'800);
}
```
3. Implement the code so (more) tests pass:
```cpp
unsigned factorial(const unsigned n) {
    auto f = n;                              // as written this gives n * n!;
    for (auto i = 1; i <= n; i++) f *= i;    // starting from f = 1 is correct
    return f;
}
```
4. Before adding code, add tests — they fail:
```cpp
REQUIRE(factorial(13) == 6'227'020'800);
REQUIRE(factorial(21) == {});               // 21! overflows 64 bits
```
5. Add code so the tests pass:
```cpp
optional<uint64_t> factorial(const uint64_t n) {
    if (n > 20) { return {}; }   // empty result
    assert(n <= 20);             // defensive programming
    ...
}
```
6. Ask "do we have full coverage?" and make the tests comprehensive:
```cpp
for (unsigned i = 21; i < UINT32_MAX; i *= 2) {
    REQUIRE(factorial(i) == {});
}
```

### Mock Objects

#### Definitions

> **Mock object**: A stand-in for a component that isn't implemented yet (or is expensive), such as a dummy database or API, that records how it is called and returns programmed values.

Tools: Java — Mockito; Python — `unittest.mock`.

```python
from unittest.mock import Mock

json = Mock()
json.loads('{"key": "value"}')     # will magically work
json.loads.assert_called()
json.loads.assert_called_once()
json.loads.assert_called_with('{"key": "value"}')
json.loads.assert_called_once_with('{"key": "value"}')
```

Mocks make non-deterministic code testable:

```python
from datetime import datetime
from unittest.mock import Mock

datetime = Mock()

def is_weekday(d):
    today = d.today()
    # Python's datetime treats Monday as 0 and Sunday as 6
    return (0 <= today.weekday() < 5)

datetime.today.return_value = wednesday   # mock today() to return Wednesday
assert is_weekday(datetime)
datetime.today.return_value = sunday
assert not is_weekday(datetime)
```

### Selecting Test Cases

| Strategy | Approach |
|---|---|
| **Partition testing** | Group possible inputs and outputs into categories (partitions) and look for edge cases at their boundaries |
| **Guideline testing** | Choose test cases from known failure points (experience of where programs commonly fail) |

### Integration Testing

#### Definitions

> **Integration testing**: Testing the interactions between modules and classes — like unit testing, but at a higher level. Unit testing checks a class or function in isolation.

![Integration approaches](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/08_Software_Testing/integration-approaches.svg)

*Big-bang integrates everything at once; incremental approaches integrate in phases from the top, the bottom, or both.*

| Approach | How |
|---|---|
| **Big-bang** | Everything at once |
| **Incremental** | Tested in phases: |
| — Top-down | Higher-level modules first (lower ones stubbed) |
| — Bottom-up | Lower-level modules first (higher ones driven by test harnesses) |
| — Sandwich | Higher and lower levels together |

### Regression Testing

#### Definitions

> **Regression testing**: Re-running old tests after changes to make sure existing behaviour hasn't broken.

When you add new code:

1. Rerun all your old tests.
2. Fix any breakage.
3. Add new tests as needed.

![Types of regression testing](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/08_Software_Testing/regression-types.svg)

*Regression testing ranges from rerunning everything to selecting only the tests the change can affect.*

| Type | Scope |
|---|---|
| Corrective | Reuse existing tests when the specification hasn't changed |
| Retest-all | Rerun every test |
| Selective | Rerun only tests affected by the change |
| Progressive | New tests for changed specifications |
| Complete | Full retest after major changes |
| Partial | Test the changed part and its interactions |
| Unit | Rerun the unit tests of the changed unit only |

### User Acceptance Testing

#### Definitions

> **User acceptance testing (UAT)**: The testing phase that ensures software is ready for production — that it is what the user wanted, and is usable and user-friendly.

| Aspect | Check |
|---|---|
| Functional | Matches the user stories (Agile/XP) |
| Operational | The system can be used and maintained |

Alpha and beta tests are late, usable prototypes:

| | Alpha testing | Beta testing |
|---|---|---|
| Where / who | In the software house, or with "super users" | With end users of the system |

**Usability testing versus UAT**:

| | Usability testing | User acceptance testing |
|---|---|---|
| Purpose | Measure ease of access | Validate the requirements document from a real user's perspective |
| When | Middle to end of the SDLC | End of the SDLC |
| Focus | Behaviour, experience and goals | Functionality |
| Cost | Can be expensive | Not that expensive |
| Measures | Satisfaction, memorability, desirability, credibility | Compatibility, roadmap validation, test support processes |
| Shared | Errors, efficiency, learnability | (same) |

### Security Testing

Security must be built in from the start, for example:

- parameterised queries, not cut-and-paste SQL;
- never displaying unescaped HTML;
- salted password hashes, with a different salt per user ID;
- strong cryptographic functions (e.g. post-quantum Kyber rather than RSA);
- not keeping passwords in memory.

**OWASP Top 10 (2021):**

| Rank | Vulnerability (change) | Fix |
|---|---|---|
| A01 | Broken access control (↑5) | Deny by default |
| A02 | Cryptographic failures (=2) | Encrypt data in transit and at rest |
| A03 | Injection (↓1) | Safe APIs; whitelist input validation |
| A04 | Insecure design (new) | "Shift left" |
| A05 | Security misconfiguration (↑6) | Secure installation process |
| A06 | Vulnerable and outdated components (↑9) | Patch management; signed components |
| A07 | Identification and authentication failures (↓2) | Multi-factor authentication; no back doors |
| A08 | Software and data integrity failures (new) | Signed software |
| A09 | Security logging and monitoring failures (↑10) | Log everything and keep the logs |
| A10 | Server-side request forgery (new) | Sanitise and whitelist |

#### Definitions

> **Shift left**: Moving testing, security and quality earlier in the process — into design before code, and into code before test.

- Unit tests should also test for security and quality issues.
- Run sanitizers regularly (AddressSan, UBSan, MemSan, …) — cheap and easy.
- Security in particular is nearly impossible to fix in late stages.

> **Penetration testing (pen testing)**: Simulating attempts to breach security to discover vulnerabilities. MobSF is an open-source tool supporting it.

Security audits report vulnerabilities systematically. In continuous integration, GitHub integrates easily with **CodeQL** to analyse code for security flaws.

### Traps And Pitfalls

> "It is not enough to be busy; the question is, what are we busy about?" — Henry David Thoreau

More traps don't help if they target the wrong thing (a mouse that doesn't like cheese): test what actually matters.

> "Absence of proof is not proof of absence." — William Cowper

Passing tests don't prove there are no bugs — only that the tests didn't find any.

---

# Dependable Systems And Software Quality

**Subject**: CSC3003S Advanced Programming — Advanced Software Development (ASD)
**Source**: [ASD10 Dependable Systems - Copy.pdf](../../Sources/CSC3003S%20-%20Advanced%20Programming/ASD/ASD10%20Dependable%20Systems%20-%20Copy.pdf), Johan Bontes, University of Cape Town, pages 1–30
**Generated**: 2026-11-06

> [!info] <span style="color:#66b3ff">Lecture reference</span>
> Software quality according to ISO 25010 plus information quality, functional and non-functional characteristics (reliability and the "nines", security risk, maintainability, flexibility, safety, compatibility), quality in use, quality control versus assurance, and the Boeing 737 MAX MCAS as a case of bad design.

---

## Core Content

### Quality Has Two Meanings

The lecture opens with *Stairway to Heaven*: "'Cause you know sometimes words have two meanings." "Quality" is such a word — the programmer and the user mean different things by it, so it must be broken into well-defined characteristics.

### ISO 25010 Software Quality Model

#### Definitions

> **ISO 25010 (2011)**: The international standard model of software product quality, splitting quality into characteristics and sub-characteristics.

![ISO 25010 quality characteristics](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/10_Dependable_Systems/iso25010.svg)

*Functional suitability is the functional characteristic; the rest are non-functional. The lecturer adds information quality, and treats security as the most important.*

| Characteristic | Sub-characteristics |
|---|---|
| Functionally suitable | Complete, correct, appropriate |
| Performance efficient | Time, resources, capacity |
| Compatible | Co-existence, interoperable |
| Usable | Recognisable, learnable, operable, user-error protection, engagement (aesthetics), inclusivity (accessibility), user assistance, self-describing |
| Reliable | Fault-free, availability, fault tolerant, recoverability |
| Secure | Confidential, integrity, non-repudiation, accountability, authenticity, resistance |
| Maintainable | Modular, reusable, analysable, modifiable, testable |
| Flexible | Adaptable, scalable, installable, replaceable |
| Safe | Operations, identifying risks, fail-safe, hazard warning, safe integration |
| **+ Information quality** (lecturer's addition) | Accurate, usable, secure |

(Sub-characteristics follow Lew et al., 2010, and the updated ISO 25010.)

### Information Quality

The lecturer adds **information quality** — whether the information the software handles is fit for purpose.

| Group | Aspect | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| Accurate | Complete | Not too little — "the whole truth" |
| | Correct | Not too much — "and nothing but the truth" |
| | Timely | Correct timestamp (no "robbing Peter to pay Paul") |
| | Mathematically accurate | Correct calculations (Excel sheets!) |
| Usable | Legible | Can be read/deciphered (e.g. binary file formats can't) |
| | Relevant | Fits the information demand; not outdated |
| | Understandable | Including its assumptions |
| | Comparable | Over time and against other data |
| Secure | Verified | Has it been, or can it be, checked? |
| | Trustworthy | Believable — what is the source? |
| | Legally compliant | Violates no laws (e.g. VAT calculations) |
| | Ethical | Privacy, medical data |
| | Authorised | Is the user allowed access? |

### Standards Are Made By People

| ISO 25010 (engineers) | Information quality (accountants) |
|---|---|
| Focus on models and processes | Focus on information flows and procedures |
| Software as a machine | Software as an administrative system |

IT means *information* technology, yet information quality is only implied in quality standards, not explicit — much as security was a few years ago.

### Functional Suitability

| Sub-characteristic | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Complete | All needed features |
| Correct | All features implemented correctly |
| Appropriate | No unneeded or harmful features |

### Performance Efficiency

| Aspect | Examples |
|---|---|
| Time | Run time, latency, solution quality |
| Resources | Data storage, network usage, CPU required |
| Capacity | How much data it can process |

### Usability

| Sub-characteristic | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Recognisable | Uses standard UI patterns |
| Learnable | Easy to learn |
| Operable | Easy to operate |
| Protection from user error | No data loss; no idiotic showstoppers (see Cooper, *About Face*, ch. 12) |
| UI aesthetics | Pleasant to use |
| Accessible | Works for users with disabilities |

### Reliability And Availability

| Sub-characteristic | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Available | Uptime |
| Faultless | No errors inside the software |
| Fault tolerant | Keeps working despite outside errors (hardware or other software failing) |
| Recoverable | If a fault occurs: how long recovery takes, and whether it fully recovers |

#### Definitions

> **Availability**: The probability that a system is "up". Uptime is quoted in "nines".

| Nines | Availability | Downtime per year |
|---|---|---|
| 2 | 99% | ~3½ days |
| 3 | 99.9% | ~8 hours |
| 4 | 99.99% | ~1 hour |
| 5 | 99.999% | ~5 minutes |
| 6 | 99.9999% | < 1 minute |

Downtime per year is $(1 - A) \times 365 \times 24$ hours, e.g. $(1 - 0.999) \times 8760 \approx 8.8$ hours.

> **MTTF (mean time to failure)**: Average time the system behaves correctly before failing.

> **MTTD (mean time to diagnose)**: Average time from failure until repair begins.

> **MTTR (mean time to repair)**: Average time to complete the repair.

> **MTBF (mean time between failures)**: Average time from one failure to the next.

$$
\text{MTBF} = \text{MTTD} + \text{MTTR} + \text{MTTF}, \qquad A = \frac{\text{MTTF}}{\text{MTBF}}
$$

![MTTF, MTTD, MTTR and MTBF](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/10_Dependable_Systems/mtbf-timeline.svg)

*After a failure the system is diagnosed and repaired, then runs correctly until the next failure; MTBF spans the whole cycle.*

### Functional Versus Non-Functional

The distinction is somewhat blurry:

| Functional | Non-functional |
|---|---|
| Directly affects users | Users may not notice, but it affects business risk |

### Security And The Risk Matrix

Security is the **most important** quality characteristic. Why? Because

$$
\text{Risk} = \text{cost} \times \text{likelihood}
$$

and insecure software is both likely to be attacked and expensive when it is.

| | Cheap | Expensive |
|---|---|---|
| **Unlikely** | Accept | Transfer (insure) |
| **Likely** | Reduce | Avoid |

![Risk matrix](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/10_Dependable_Systems/risk-matrix.svg)

*Each risk is treated according to its cost and likelihood: accept, reduce, transfer or avoid (ARTA).*

### Maintainability, Flexibility, Safety And Compatibility

**Maintainability** — for programmers:

- modular, with few connections (low fan-out);
- reusable;
- analysable;
- modifiable;
- testable.

**Flexibility** — for implementers:

- adaptable (portable);
- scalable;
- installable;
- replaceable.

**Safety** — not harmful:

| Sub-characteristic | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Operations | Doesn't hurt people or the organisation |
| Identify risks | Risks of harm are known |
| Fail-safe | "The plane does not crash" |
| Hazard warning | "Format disk: are you sure?" |
| Safe integration | Integrates with other systems without creating hazards |

**Compatibility**:

| Sub-characteristic | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Co-existence | Doesn't misbehave alongside other software |
| Interoperable | Can exchange information with other software |

### Quality In Use

| Perspective | Whose view |
|---|---|
| Inside quality | The programmer's view (the product's internal and external characteristics) |
| Outside quality (quality in use) | The user's view (how well it serves them in practice) |

### Quality Control Versus Quality Assurance

#### Definitions

> **Quality control**: A **reactive** approach that tries to detect quality problems, mainly by testing.

> **Quality assurance**: A **preventive** approach that puts procedures in place to assure quality (e.g. the ISO 9000 series) and documents the work done.

| | Quality control | Quality assurance |
|---|---|---|
| Nature | Reactive: detect problems | Preventive: procedures |
| Methods | Unit tests, automated tests, functionality tests, stress tests, alpha/beta tests | Documentation of work, checklists, an audit trail (who, what, why, where, when) |
| Prescribes procedures? | No | Yes |
| Certification | Not by itself | Useful for quality certification |

Formal assurance assumes a **waterfall** model: quality is checked against documentation, especially requirements and analysis, and each stage has its own documents. Pure Agile assumes the design grows organically, so it is hard to certify because of minimal documentation.

### Dromley's Quality Model

Every quality model (including Dromley's) breaks quality into different sub-components; the course sticks with ISO 25010's breakdown but uses Dromley's 5-step process:

1. Pick the high-level quality metrics to evaluate.
2. List the components in the system.
3. Identify the quality-carrying properties of each component.
4. Determine how each property affects the quality metric.
5. Evaluate, identify weaknesses, and use a risk matrix to mitigate.

### The Cost Of Poor Quality

The cost of poor quality is **exponential**. Sources include:

- errors in specification and development (communication and testing);
- verification and validation issues;
- availability problems — the system goes down;
- a buggy system;
- security problems — costly risks and cyber-attacks.

Design for recovery from failures and attacks **without loss of data**.

### Case Study: Boeing 737 MAX MCAS

The **MCAS** (Manoeuvring Characteristics Augmentation System) shows how bad design kills.

| Design flaw | Consequence |
|---|---|
| Solved a trivial problem | Added risk for little benefit |
| No training, just a bulletin | Pilots didn't know it existed |
| Pilot has 3 seconds to respond | Little margin for error |
| Has a hidden "cheat code" (pilot trim interrupts it) but restarts every 5 seconds | Repeated activation |
| Not on the runaway-stabiliser checklist | Pilots followed the wrong procedure |
| Depends on a **single** angle-of-attack (AoA) sensor | One faulty sensor triggers it — no redundancy |
| Bigger correction on every restart, up to 90° nose down | Escalating danger |
| Uses the tail, a huge lever | Pilot must exert tens of kg, then tons, to overcome it |
| Can only be switched off manually, and then the stabilisers are stuck | The fallback is a slow, awkward manual trim wheel |

MCAS drove the trim at increasing speed, while the pilot's only alternative was a slow, awkward manual trim wheel. The force needed on the control yoke was around 25–50 kg. It violates the safety characteristics above: no fail-safe, no hazard warning, unidentified risks, and unsafe integration.

---

# Software Architecture And Architectural Patterns

**Subject**: CSC3003S Advanced Programming — Advanced Software Development (ASD)
**Source**: [ASD11-Architecture.pdf](../../Sources/CSC3003S%20-%20Advanced%20Programming/ASD/ASD11-Architecture.pdf), Johan Bontes (slides modified from Prof. Edwin Blake), University of Cape Town, pages 1–48
**Generated**: 2026-11-06

> [!info] <span style="color:#66b3ff">Lecture reference</span>
> The architecture-centred approach, why patterns encapsulate experience, the key architectural patterns (layered, repository, client–server, pipe and filter, MVC), multi-tier layered architectures, realising non-functional requirements, and generic application architectures.

---

## Core Content

### The Architecture-Centred Approach

- This approach emphasises **design**, which pervades engineering activities and is an important part of computer science.
- Misreading Agile's lean documentation leads to **inadequate architectural design information**.
- **Architectural decisions are system-wide and strategic**: use object orientation, choose the overall architectural pattern, choose APIs.
- **Tactical decisions are local and detailed**: use of design patterns, refactoring decisions, etc.

Requirements and analysis lead to design, in a loop:

1. We are investigators learning about a problem (analysis: use cases, system sequence diagrams, domain model).
2. We change roles and become designers making a working solution (design: architecture, interaction diagrams, class diagrams).
3. Go to 1.

### What Software Architecture Is

#### Definitions

> **Software architecture**: The large-scale, big ideas of a system — its motivations, constraints, organisation, patterns, responsibilities and connections.

Architecture is very concerned with **non-functional requirements**: ensuring they can be met, or, if not, suggesting trade-offs (e.g. trends, cost, maintainability).

### Patterns Are Encapsulated Experience

#### Definitions

> **Architectural pattern**: A reusable template for the overall structure of a system that encapsulates the basic approach to a class of similar problems.

- Much of your programming style is the way you **structure** solutions, not the minute details of code. Reusing structure across projects gives all the benefits of reuse.
- Architecture never starts from an empty slate: new designs build on previous similar ones. The IT community has many past solutions, but has made little progress documenting the experience — architectural templates describe it best.
- Like accountancy: every factory follows the same procedures, the same blueprint.

Aims:

- Enhance the reusability of object-oriented code — well-structured OO systems have recurring patterns of classes and objects.
- Knowing what worked before makes designers more productive and designs more flexible and reusable.

Benefits of architectural patterns:

| Benefit |
|---|
| Capture expertise and communicate it to non-experts |
| A common language among developers |
| Better design understandability |
| Easier reuse of good designs; avoid non-reusable alternatives |
| Easier design modification |
| Better documentation — start system documentation with a UML design pattern |

### Layered Architecture Pattern

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| **Problem** | Building new facilities on top of existing systems; development spread across teams (one team per layer); multi-level security needed |
| **Solution** | Organise the system into layers of related functionality. Each layer provides services to the layer above. The lowest layers are core services used throughout the system |
| **Pros** | A layer can be swapped for another with the same interface. Redundant facilities (e.g. authentication) can be provided in each layer for dependability |
| **Cons** | Clean separation between layers is hard in practice; high layers may need to talk directly to low ones. Multiple levels can make it slow |

![Layered architecture](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/11_Architecture/layered.svg)

*UI (Swing, Web) sits on the domain (Sales, Payments, Taxes), which sits on technical services (Persistence, Logging, RulesEngine).*

### Repository Pattern

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| **Problem** | Large volumes of information must be stored for a long time; or a data-driven system where adding data triggers an action or tool |
| **Solution** | Store all data in a central repository accessible to all components; components interact **only** through the repository |
| **Pros** | Components are independent. A change by one component propagates to all. All data is managed in one place (e.g. one backup) |
| **Cons** | The repository is a **single point of failure**. Routing all traffic through it may be slow. Distributing it across machines is complex |

![Repository pattern](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/11_Architecture/repository.svg)

*Clients' business logic persists to and queries a shared repository rather than talking to each other.*

### Client–Server Pattern

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| **Problem** | Shared-database data must be accessed from many locations; servers must be replicable; load is variable |
| **Solution** | Organise functionality into services, each on a separate server. Clients use the services |
| **Pros** | Servers can be distributed across a network and added or upgraded with minimal disruption. General functionality (e.g. printing) is available to all clients, which don't implement it |
| **Cons** | Each service is a single point of failure (denial-of-service or server failure). Performance may be unpredictable (network). Management problems if servers belong to different organisations |

![Client-server film library](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/11_Architecture/client-server.svg)

*Clients 1…n reach catalogue, video, picture and web servers over the Internet; each server owns its store.*

### Pipe And Filter Pattern

#### Definitions

> **Filter**: A discrete processing component that performs one type of data transformation.

> **Pipe**: The connection through which data flows from one filter to the next.

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| **Problem** | Data processing (batch or transaction) where inputs are processed in stages to produce related outputs |
| **Solution** | Each filter performs one transformation; data flows through pipes from component to component |
| **Pros** | Easy to understand; supports transformation reuse; workflow style matches many business processes; adding transformations is simple; can be sequential or concurrent |
| **Cons** | The data format between transformations must be standardised, so each filter decodes input and encodes output — extra overhead. Transformations with incompatible data structures can't be reused |

![Pipe and filter](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/11_Architecture/pipe-filter.svg)

*Source → Filter 1 → Filter 2 → Sink, e.g. Document (XML) → Enrich (internal XML) → Generate (HTML) → Display.*

### Cohesion And Coupling

#### Definitions

> **Cohesion**: Communication **within** a module — how strongly its parts belong together.

> **Coupling**: Communication **between** modules — how strongly they depend on each other.

The key aim of all these patterns: **minimise coupling and maximise cohesion**.

### Model-View-Controller

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| **Problem** | Multiple ways to view and interact with data; or future interaction and presentation are unknown |
| **Solution** | Separate presentation and interaction from the data, using three interacting components |
| **Pros** | Decouples data from its representation; the same data can be shown in different ways |
| **Cons** | Extra code and complexity when the data model and interactions are simple |

| Component | Role |
|---|---|
| **Model** | Manages the system data and operations — the representation of the application |
| **View** | Defines and manages presentation — how information is displayed |
| **Controller** | Manages user interaction (key presses, mouse clicks) — maps user actions to operations on the model, passing them to the view and model |

![Model-View-Controller](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/11_Architecture/mvc.svg)

*The controller initiates changes in the model; the model notifies its subscribed views, which redraw by querying it.*

MVC is a special case of the repository architecture: the model implements the central data store, and the controller explicitly dictates control flow.

### MVC Components In Detail

**Model**:

- Carries out some task, with no concern for "look and feel".
- Has a purely functional public interface: **query methods** (get current state) and **mutator methods** (modify state).
- Lets views **register** and **notifies** registered views when its state changes. In Java: a class extending `java.util.Observable`, whose superclass provides register/notify.

**View**:

- Provides the GUI for a model and displays it by querying the model.
- When the user manipulates the view, it informs the controller.
- In Java: built with AWT or Swing; implements `java.util.Observer`.

**Controller**:

- Views link to (possibly multiple) controllers.
- When the user interacts with a linked view, the controller calls the model's mutator methods.
- The model then notifies **all** registered views, which update the display.
- In Java: controllers are event listeners.

Sequence for one user action:

1. The user manipulates a view.
2. The view informs its controller.
3. The controller calls a model mutator.
4. The model notifies all registered views.
5. Each view queries the model and redraws.

### Multi-Tier Layered Architectures

Advice: separate presentation from application logic and other concerns.

#### Definitions

> **Layer**: A coarse-grained group of classes, subsystems or packages with cohesive responsibility for a major aspect of the system. Higher layers call the services of lower layers.

> **Strict layered architecture**: Each layer uses only the layer directly below it.

> **Relaxed layered architecture**: A layer may skip layers below it.

Typical layers, from most application-specific to most general (lower layers are wider, i.e. more widely applicable):

| Layer | Also called | Contents |
|---|---|---|
| UI | Presentation, View | GUI windows, reports, speech interface; HTML, XML, XSLT, JSP, JavaScript |
| Application | Workflow, Process, Mediation, Controller | Handles presentation requests; workflow; session state; window/page transitions; consolidating data for presentation |
| Domain | Business, Application logic, Model | Handles application requests; domain rules; domain services (POS, inventory), possibly shared by several applications |
| Business infrastructure | Low-level business services | Very general business services used in many domains, e.g. `CurrencyConverter` |
| Technical services | Technical infrastructure, high-level technical services | Relatively high-level services and frameworks: persistence, security |
| Foundation | Core/base services, low-level infrastructure | Data structures, threads, maths, file, DB and network I/O |

![Typical layers](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/11_Architecture/layer-stack.svg)

*Dependencies point downward; lower layers are more general and more reusable.*

In UML, logical partitioning is shown with **package diagrams**, e.g. a UI package (Summaries, Editors), a Domain package (Core, Evaluation, Policies) and a Services package (Persistence, Logging, Reporting, Security). Packages can group classes, other packages, use cases, etc.

### Application Coordination And Model-View Separation

**Application coordination layer**: consider a layer whose objects represent **use cases** (e.g. `RentingVideoUCHandler`, `PayingFinesUCHandler`) and may hold session state. It sits between the UI and the domain.

#### Definitions

> **Model-view separation principle**: Model (domain-layer) objects must not know about view (UI-layer) objects, and domain responsibilities must not be given to view objects.

Benefits:

- better separation of concerns and lower coupling;
- the domain layer can be reused with another UI;
- multiple views of one model become easy (e.g. desktop and web UIs for the same application).

### Benefits Of Layering And Real-World Stacks

- The system is easier to comprehend: each layer has a specific purpose.
- High cohesion within layers, low coupling between them.
- Source changes don't ripple through the system; they stay within a layer.
- Low coupling makes it easy to swap a lower-layer service for another technology — e.g. replace the database without rewriting application logic.
- Lower layers contain more reusable functionality.

**Internet stack (TCP/IP model)**:

| Layer | Examples |
|---|---|
| Application (incl. presentation, session) | HTTP, FTP, DNS, SMTP, SSH, TLS/SSL |
| Transport | TCP, UDP |
| Network | IP (IPv4, IPv6) |
| (Data) link | PPP, MAC, FDDI |
| Physical | 10BASE-T, POTS, 802.11a/b/g/n |

RFC 1958 (1996): over 25 years the Internet grew by factors of 1000 (backbone speed) to 1 000 000 (hosts), so "the principle of constant change is perhaps the only principle of the Internet that should survive indefinitely."

**Web 2.0 reference architecture**:

| Tier | Role | Examples |
|---|---|---|
| Client application tier | User's view of services | Web browsers, Flash, Acrobat, iTunes |
| Connectivity | Means of reaching services | XML over HTTP |
| Service tier | Packages resources as services and controls what goes in and out | Application servers: SOAP, EJB, PHP, Rails, ASP |
| Resource tier | Back-end data or processing capabilities | Databases, back-end systems |
| Design, development and governance tools | Building the web apps | Dreamweaver, Xcode, DashCode, IDEs |

### Realising Non-Functional Requirements

| Requirement | Architectural approach |
|---|---|
| **Performance** | Localise critical operations and minimise communication; use large rather than fine-grained components; allow for concurrency |
| **Security** | Use a layered architecture with critical assets in the inner layers |
| **Safety** | Localise safety-critical features in a small number of subsystems |
| **Availability** | Include redundant components and fault-tolerance mechanisms; allow components to be replaced without stopping the system |
| **Maintainability** | Use fine-grained, replaceable components; avoid global/shared data structures |

Note the conflicts: performance wants large components, maintainability wants fine-grained ones.

Architecture also serves **beforehand**:

- as a design plan for negotiating system requirements;
- to structure discussions with clients, developers and managers;
- as an essential complexity-management tool that hides details so designers can focus on key abstractions.

### Application Architectures

So far architecture was viewed through control, distribution and structure. An alternative is the **application perspective**: generic models of application types help us understand and compare applications.

![Application architectures](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/11_Architecture/application-architectures.svg)

*Four generic application types, each with a characteristic structure.*

| Type | Description | Examples |
|---|---|---|
| **Data processing** | Data-driven; processes data in **batches** without user intervention | Billing, payroll |
| **Transaction processing** | Data-centred; processes user requests and updates a database | E-commerce, reservations |
| **Event processing** | Actions depend on interpreting events from the environment | Games, word processors, real-time systems |
| **Language processing** | User intentions are written in a formal language that the system processes and interprets | Compilers, command interpreters |

Real applications may combine types.

**Data processing (input–process–output)**:

1. **Input** reads data from a file or database, checks validity and queues valid data.
2. **Process** takes a transaction from the queue, computes, and creates a new result record.
3. **Output** reads the records, formats them and writes them to the database or a printer.

**Transaction processing**: processes user requests for information from, or updates to, a database. To a user, a transaction is any coherent sequence of operations that satisfies a goal (e.g. "find flight times from London to Paris"). Users make asynchronous requests that a **transaction manager** processes. Information can be accessed and modified remotely by many users.

**Event processing — editing systems** are naturally object-oriented:

| Component | Role |
|---|---|
| Screen | Monitors screen memory and detects events |
| Event | Recognises events and passes them on for processing |
| Command | Executes a user command |
| Editor data | Manages the editor data structure |
| Ancillary data | Manages other data, such as styles and preferences |
| File system | Manages file I/O |
| Display | Updates the screen display |

User-interface events are detected and an in-memory data structure is modified.

**Language processing — compilers** translate source code into another language (e.g. assembler) and may interpret the instructions:

1. **Lexical analysis** (lexical analyser) → tokens, filling the **symbol table**.
2. **Syntactic analysis** (syntax analyser) → an **abstract syntax tree (AST)**.
3. **Semantic analysis** (semantic analyser), using the symbol table and syntax tree.
4. **Code generation** (code generator).

---

# Antipatterns

**Subject**: CSC3003S Advanced Programming — Advanced Software Development (ASD)
**Source**: [ASD12 Antipatterns.pdf](../../Sources/CSC3003S%20-%20Advanced%20Programming/ASD/ASD12%20Antipatterns.pdf), Johan Bontes, University of Cape Town, pages 1–28
**Generated**: 2026-11-06

> [!info] <span style="color:#66b3ff">Lecture reference</span>
> Common counterproductive "solutions", grouped as generic software, object-oriented, programming and methodological antipatterns — each with its problem, the wrong solution, the result and the fix.

---

## Core Content

### What An Antipattern Is

#### Definitions

> **Antipattern** (Andrew Koenig, 1995): A common solution to a problem that is likely to be counterproductive. The name is a pun on the "Gang of Four" design patterns book.

Each antipattern below follows the same shape: a real **problem**, a tempting **wrong solution**, its **result**, and the **fix**.

### Generic Software Antipatterns

#### Abstraction inversion

| | |
|---|---|
| Problem | Users of a construct need its private functions |
| Wrong solution | Users re-implement the private functions in terms of the public interface — which itself uses (other) private functions |
| Result | Lower-level features depend on higher-level ones: the abstraction is "inverted" |
| Fix | Dependencies must form an acyclic graph (ideally a simple one) |

#### Big ball of mud

#### Definitions

> **Big ball of mud** (spaghetti code, rat's nest): A program with no discernible structure.

Dijkstra's "Go To Statement Considered Harmful": `goto` jumps put all code on a single level with no structure. That critique led to **structured programming** and the waterfall model.

| | |
|---|---|
| Problem | How to structure a program? |
| Wrong solution 1 | Just get started and see where the flow takes us |
| Wrong solution 2 | Use a system you don't understand |
| Result | Code understandable only as one complex unit; it can't be decomposed into independent items; hard to understand and untestable |
| Fix | Use design and architecture patterns plus decomposition |

#### Inner-platform effect

| | |
|---|---|
| Problem | Programmers want to make the application customisable |
| Wrong solution | Turn the application into a programming language |
| Result | A complex solution to a perhaps simple problem (an add-in would do); security becomes very hard |
| Fix | Sandbox the inner language — or just don't: it's a security nightmare |
| Examples | Plug-in based software (Firefox), MS Office (macros) |

#### Stovepipe system

#### Definitions

> **Stovepipe system**: A system procured and developed to solve one specific problem, with limited focus and functionality, whose data cannot easily be shared with other systems.

| | |
|---|---|
| Problem | No architecture up front; the app was built to do one thing, then features were added |
| Wrong solution | Reinvent the wheel for this one app; don't use shared functionality from other systems |
| Result | No code reuse; one-off systems are hard to test fully |
| Fix | Use standard components for extra functionality (databases, etc.) |

#### Data races

#### Definitions

> **Data race**: Two threads access the same memory concurrently, at least one writes, and there is no synchronisation.

| | |
|---|---|
| Problem | Task-parallel (multi-threaded) code is hard |
| Wrong solution | Not realising it's hard; not using a known good solution |
| Result | Sequencing problems in Java (data corruption); in C++ additionally **undefined behaviour** (bizarre side effects) |
| Fix | Simplify the threading model; use MPI; use known good components (hard!) |

### Object-Oriented Antipatterns

| Antipattern | Problem | Wrong solution | Result | Fix |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Circular dependencies** | The real-world model has a circular dependency, and OOP models the real world | Copy the cycle into the classes | SOLID violations, especially tight coupling | Design patterns, e.g. Observer |
| **Circle–ellipse problem** | Looks like an IS-A relationship, but the invariants differ (a circle can't stretch one axis) | Inheritance | Liskov substitution violated | Never violate Liskov. Bad: weaken the restrictions. Better: use a **mixin**, not inheritance. Some languages allow partial inheritance (e.g. Lisp) |
| **God object** | Communication between objects is complex | Put everything in one class so communication is "easy" | The OOP equivalent of spaghetti code; classes hard to debug | Create a proper hierarchy; use patterns to keep communication simple |
| **Sequential (temporal) coupling** | Some steps must happen in order (e.g. initialisation) | `init()` or numbered methods the caller must call in order | Code misbehaves if earlier steps didn't happen first | Automatically call `init`/step 1 when step 2 needs it; use the **Template Method** pattern |
| **Call super** | The superclass method has part of the needed code and the subclass the rest, but calling `super` is optional | Write "call super needed" in a comment | Code breaks when someone forgets | **Template Method** pattern; Lisp has `before`/`after`/`around` methods |
| **Object orgy** (unrestricted access) | Access to private members is needed | Make everything public | Encapsulation broken, no information hiding, huge public interfaces (violates the I in SOLID) | Design the interface properly; break the API if needed |

See [SOLID](#SOLID%20Design%20Principles) and [Design Patterns I](#Design%20Patterns%20I%3A%20GoF%20Patterns%20And%20Lambdas) for the principles and patterns behind these fixes.

### Programming Antipatterns

#### Cargo cult programming

| | |
|---|---|
| Problem | Paradigms or patterns used without understanding |
| Wrong solution | More patterns |
| Result | Code that doesn't make sense and is hard to understand |
| Fix | Take this course (CSC3003S); add an experienced programmer; pair programming; code review |

#### Coding by exception

| | |
|---|---|
| Problem | Program flow is awkward |
| Wrong solution | Throw an exception to jump into a section of **non-exceptional** code — equivalent to a `goto` |
| Result | Poor performance; hard-to-understand code; errors accidentally hidden by handled exceptions |
| Fix | Read "Goto Considered Harmful"; use a nested function or `case` statement, redesign the logic — or even an honest `goto` |

#### Repeated code

Violates **DRY** (don't repeat yourself).

#### Shotgun surgery

| | |
|---|---|
| Problem | A small change is needed in many places |
| Wrong solution | Make lots of quick-and-dirty changes all over |
| Result | Unit tests and docs out of date; non-DRY code |
| Fix | Aspect-oriented programming (see [AOP](#Aspect-Oriented%20Programming%20And%20Design%20Patterns%20Recap)); refactor or rewrite |

#### Error hiding

| | |
|---|---|
| Problem | Exceptions break your code |
| Wrong solution | Silently swallow the exception |
| Result | Bugs persist but become undetectable |
| Fix | Find and fix the source of the error. Only handle exceptions that are well understood or due to outside factors |

### Methodological Antipatterns

#### Copy-paste programming

| | |
|---|---|
| Problem | The answer was on Stack Overflow — or worse, elsewhere in the code base — and "dunno why, but it seems to work" |
| Wrong solution | Copy-paste it as is, preferably in many places |
| Result | Non-DRY, undocumented code without unit tests; the pasted code carries hidden assumptions that differ from yours |
| Fix | DRY; make sure you understand your code and the assumptions in the Stack Overflow code |

**Variant** — repetitive tasks, each slightly different:

| | |
|---|---|
| Wrong solution | Copy the code for task 1 many times and tweak each copy |
| Result | Non-DRY, hard-to-read code; poor performance from overusing the L1 code cache |
| Fix | Isolate the basic task in a function; in C++ templates are your friend; design patterns |

#### Vibe coding

Generating code with an LLM without understanding it: "copy-paste programming ×2".

#### Premature optimisation

| | |
|---|---|
| Problem | Code needs to be fast, and it may or may not be |
| Wrong solution | Design overly clever, fast-looking solutions |
| Result | Unneeded complexity in the wrong places |
| Fix | Pick a good algorithm (with the right asymptotic run time); start with straightforward code; profile every iteration and optimise only where needed |

> "We should forget about small efficiencies, say about 97% of the time: premature optimization is the root of all evil. Yet we should not pass up our opportunities in that critical 3%." — Donald Knuth

#### Reinventing the (square) wheel

| | |
|---|---|
| Problem | You have a problem nobody else can solve — you're the A-Team |
| Wrong solution | Assume the problem is novel (it likely isn't) and invent a clever, novel solution |
| Result | Wasted time, possibly on a suboptimal solution |
| Fix | Most problems already have solutions: use the standard one, and check its quality |

---

# SOLID Design Principles

**Subject**: CSC3003S Advanced Programming — Advanced Software Development (ASD)
**Source**: [ASD13 SOLID.pdf](../../Sources/CSC3003S%20-%20Advanced%20Programming/ASD/ASD13%20SOLID.pdf), Johan Bontes, University of Cape Town, pages 1–45
**Generated**: 2026-11-06

> [!info] <span style="color:#66b3ff">Lecture reference</span>
> The five SOLID object-oriented design principles — Single responsibility, Open–closed, Liskov substitution, Interface segregation and Dependency inversion — with their classic examples and design by contract.

---

## Core Content

### SOLID Overview

| Letter | Principle | One-line summary |
|---|---|---|
| S | Single responsibility | A class has one reason to change |
| O | Open–closed | Open for extension, closed for modification |
| L | Liskov substitution | Subtypes must be usable wherever their base type is |
| I | Interface segregation | No client depends on methods it doesn't use |
| D | Dependency inversion | Depend on abstractions, not details |

### Single Responsibility Principle

#### Definitions

> **Single responsibility principle (SRP)**: A class (or function or module) must do one thing and do it well. "There should never be more than one reason for a class to change." — Robert C. Martin, *The Principles of OOD*, ~1998.

**Violation**: a `Rectangle` class does both drawing (`draw()`) and area calculation (`area()`).

- It entangles the computational geometry application and the graphical application.
- Really, `Rectangle` is a compound object, with a hidden cross-link between the graphics app and geometry, and high fan-out.
- Changes to the drawing code can break the area calculation.

**Fix**: split it. `GeometricRect` provides `area()` for the geometry app; `Rectangle` provides `draw()` for the graphics app and uses `GeometricRect`.

- The hidden cross-link is removed and fan-out is low.
- Changes to drawing code can't affect geometry.

![SRP violation and fix](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/13_SOLID/srp.svg)

*Left: one Rectangle with two reasons to change. Right: geometry and drawing in separate classes.*

### What Counts As A Responsibility

#### Definitions

> **Responsibility**: A reason for change. If a class has two reasons to change, it violates SRP.

The pitfall: humans naturally cluster responsibilities into groups. This modem interface looks cohesive:

```java
interface Modem {
    public void dial(String phonenr);
    public void hangup();
    public void send(char c);
    public char receive();
}
```

But it holds two responsibilities that change for different reasons:

| Responsibility | Methods |
|---|---|
| **Connection control** — the lifetime of the connection | `dial`, `hangup` |
| **Data control** — sending and receiving characters | `send`, `receive` |

![Modem responsibilities separated](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/13_SOLID/modem.svg)

*Separate `Connection` and `DataChannel` interfaces; one modem implementation can still implement both.*

SRP is really a collation of four older principles:

| Principle | Source |
|---|---|
| Decomposition | Parnas, "On the Criteria to Be Used in Decomposing Systems into Modules", 1972 |
| Separation of concerns | Dijkstra, "On the role of scientific thought", 1974 |
| Coupling and cohesion | Constantine, *Structured Design*, 1975 |

> Gather together the things that change for the same reasons. Separate the things that change for different reasons.

### Open–Closed Principle

#### Definitions

> **Open–closed principle (OCP)**: "Classes, modules, functions should be open for extension, but closed for modification." — Meyer, *Object-Oriented Software Construction*, 1988.

| | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **Open for extension** | A new module can extend the old one |
| **Closed for modification** | No source changes to the old module — no new features in old source code. (Bug fixes are not modification.) |

The workflow: start with the existing code, clean it up (hopefully), then **add** the new feature in new code rather than editing the old.

### Ways To Extend Without Modifying

| Mechanism | Example languages |
|---|---|
| Class inheritance (non-virtual) | C++ (impossible in Java) |
| Interface inheritance (virtual methods) | Java |
| Message handlers | Smalltalk, Objective-C, Windows API |

**Non-virtual class inheritance (C++)**:

```cpp
class Parent {
    void hi() { printf("hi"); }
};
class Child : private Parent {
    void hi() { Parent::hi(); printf("C\n"); }
};  // warning: child method hides parent
```

**Virtual (polymorphic) inheritance**:

```cpp
class Parent { virtual void hi() { ... } };
class Child : private Parent {
    override void hi() { Parent::hi(); printf("Child\n"); }
};  // polymorphic inheritance is an antipattern
```

**Pure interface** — better:

```cpp
class IGreet { virtual void hi() = 0; };   // abstract
class Child : public IGreet {
    override void hi() { printf("hi Child\n"); }
};  // better to use interfaces
```

C++ also has compile-time polymorphism (CRTP, "deducing this" in C++23; mixins in Rust) — out of scope for ASD. Operator or function overloading is **not** OOP polymorphism.

**Message passing** (Windows API, Smalltalk, Objective-C): each message has an ID and a data record; a handler is bound to a message ID. If the handler returns false, the parent's handler is called.

```pascal
const WM_MouseMove := 200;  // message id
record TWMMouseMove;
begin                       // message data
    MsgId: int := WM_MouseMove;
    keys: set of [ctrl, alt, shift, caps, win, menu];
    buttons: set of [bL, bR, bM, b4, b5, b6, b7, b8];
    pos: TPoint;
end;

function TWindow.MouseMove(Msg: TWMMouseMove) message WM_MouseMove: bool;
begin
    result := (bL in Msg.buttons) and (Msg.keys = []);
    if result then begin
        DrawLine(prev, Msg.pos);
        prev := Msg.pos;
    end;
end;  // result = false? call parent's handler

SendMessage(Window1, MouseData);  // WinAPI sends this when the mouse moves
```

(Petzold, *Programming Windows*, 5th ed., 1998.)

### Liskov Substitution Principle

#### Definitions

> **Liskov substitution principle (LSP)** (Barbara Liskov, 1988): "If for each object $o_1$ of type $S$ there is an object $o_2$ of type $T$ such that for all programs $P$ defined in terms of $T$, the behaviour of $P$ is unchanged when $o_1$ is substituted for $o_2$, then $S$ is a subtype of $T$." In short: functions that use references to base classes must be able to use derived objects without knowing it.

**Blatant violation**:

```cpp
// intentionally bad code
void DrawShape(Shape& s) {
    if (typeid(s) == typeid(Rect)) DrawR((Rect&)s);
    else if (typeid(s) == typeid(Circ)) DrawC((Circ&)s);
}
```

- `DrawShape` must know every `Shape` subtype's details.
- It breaks when new types are added (also violating OCP).
- It breaks if RTTI (run-time type information) is disabled.

### The Square–Rectangle Violation

Is a square a rectangle? Mathematically yes — but not behaviourally.

```cpp
struct Rectangle {
    void setWidth(const int w)  { width = w; }
    void setHeight(const int h) { height = h; }
    double getHeight() const { return height; }
    double getWidth()  const { return width; }
private:
    int width, height;
};

struct Square : public Rectangle {
    void setWidth(const int w)  { width = height = w; }
    void setHeight(const int h) { setWidth(h); }
};

void f(Rectangle& r) { r.setWidth(32); }   // breaks if r is a Square
```

Because `setWidth`/`setHeight` aren't virtual, `f` calls `Rectangle::setWidth` on a square and breaks its invariant (width = height).

"Fix" it by making everything virtual — and it still breaks:

```cpp
void g(Rectangle& r) {
    r.setWidth(2);
    r.setHeight(3);
    assert(r.getWidth() * r.getHeight() == 6);  // fails if r is a Square
}
```

`g` relies on Rectangle's behaviour (setting height leaves width alone), which Square doesn't honour.

- Only use virtual inheritance for **pure IS-A relationships**. Outside of UIs, these are rare.
- In Java all methods are virtual by default. You should almost never use inheritance:
  - use **interfaces** instead;
  - use inheritance only if you're sure of an IS-A relationship;
  - use **encapsulation** (composition) for HAS-A relationships.

```java
interface IPart extends IDraw, IMore {}

public class Whole implements IDraw {
    private IPart part;
    public void draw() {
        this.draw_background();
        part.draw();
    }
}
```

### Design By Contract

To honour LSP, methods should declare **pre-conditions** and **post-conditions** (Meyer, 1988). A subtype may weaken pre-conditions and strengthen post-conditions, but never the reverse.

**C++26 contracts**:

```cpp
bool canbe_normal(float3 v) { return v.x * v.y * v.z != 0.0; }

constexpr float3 normalize(float3 v) noexcept
    pre(canbe_normal(v))      // prevents division by 0, hence noexcept
    post(v: is_normal(v))
{
    const auto norm = std::hypot(v.x, v.y, v.z);
    contract_assert(norm != 0.0);
    v.x /= norm; v.y /= norm; v.z /= norm;
    return v;
}

int main() {
    const auto v = normalize({0.3, 0.4, 0.5});  // succeeds
    const auto w = normalize({0, 0, 0});        // fails pre- and post-condition
}
```

Older languages use assertions for the same checks.

**Ariane 5 flight V88 (rocket 501)**: an integer overflow (not a problem in itself) raised an exception, which crashed the guidance system and destroyed the rocket. Check your pre- and post-conditions.

| | Contracts | Errors |
|---|---|---|
| Violation | Cannot be recovered from | Can be accommodated in code with fallback options |
| Tooling | Supported by tools | — |

| Contract part | Checks |
|---|---|
| Pre-condition | Input parameters and the object's starting state |
| Post-condition | Output and the object's end state |
| Invariant ("middle condition") | Assertions in the middle |

Contract assertions catch only a subset of violations and can't prove full correctness: program correctness is **undecidable** (Turing), and language features are not yet comprehensive.

### No God Classes

![God class detection](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/13_SOLID/god-class.svg)

*A class is a god class when it directly uses many attributes of other classes AND has very high functional complexity AND low cohesion.*

A god class concentrates many responsibilities, violating SRP (see [Antipatterns](#Object-Oriented%20Antipatterns)).

### Interface Segregation Principle

#### Definitions

> **Interface segregation principle (ISP)**: Don't use fat interfaces. "Clients should not be forced to depend upon (parts of) interfaces that they do not use."

### The Timed Door

```java
public interface IDoor {
    public void lock();
    public void unlock();
    public boolean isDoorOpen();
}

public interface ITimerClient {
    public void TimeOut();
}

public class Timer {
    public void register(int timeout, TimerClient client);
}
```

How do we make a door that sounds an alarm if left open too long?

**Bad**: `public class Door implements IDoor, ITimerClient;` and `public class TimedDoor extends Door;`. Now every `Door` depends on `ITimerClient`. When `ITimerClient` changes, `Door` must change too, even though plain doors don't use timers. (Java has only single class inheritance.)

**Solution — separate using delegation (Adapter pattern)**:

```java
public class TimerDoor implements IDoor {
    public DTAdaptor dta;
    public void regTimer(int end) {
        Time t = toTime(end);
        dta.register(t);
    }
}
```

`TimedDoor` *is an* `IDoor` and *has an* adapter that *is an* `ITimerClient`; the adapter calls back into the door on `TimeOut`.

![Timed door via adapter](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/13_SOLID/timed-door.svg)

*With single inheritance, TimedDoor implements IDoor and delegates timer callbacks to a DoorTimerAdapter.*

Alternatives:

| Approach | Code | Note |
|---|---|---|
| Implement multiple interfaces | `class TimerDoor implements IDoor, ITimerClient { DTAdaptor dta; void register(Time t) { dta.register(t); } }` | You still implement or embed the relevant objects |
| C++ multiple inheritance | `class TimerDoor : public Door, public TimerClient { /* inherits register */ };` | Rarely used because complex, except for abstract classes (= interfaces) |

### The ATM Example

An ATM has deposit, withdrawal and transfer transactions.

- **Bad**: one fat `UI` interface used by every transaction. Each transaction uses parts no other needs.
- **Good**: split it into `DepositUI`, `WithdrawalUI` and `TransferUI`. Each transaction depends only on its own UI interface; internally the UIs can still use the overall UI.

Benefits: transactions decouple into separate modules, and errors in deposit can't lead to accidental withdrawals.

![ATM interface segregation](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/13_SOLID/atm-isp.svg)

*Before: every transaction depends on the whole UI. After: each depends only on its own segregated interface.*

### Dependency Inversion Principle

#### Definitions

> **Dependency inversion principle (DIP)**: (A) High-level modules should not depend on low-level modules; both should depend on abstractions. (B) Abstractions should not depend on details; details should depend on abstractions.

```c
// bad code
void Copy() {
    char c;
    while ((c = readKey()) != EOF) {
        print(c);
    }
}
```

Problem: `Copy` can't be reused in other contexts. A bad fix adds a switch:

```c
// bad fix
enum OutputDevice { printer, disk };   // more devices...
void Copy(OutputDevice dev) {
    int c;
    while ((c = ReadKeyboard()) != EOF) {
        if (dev == printer) { WritePrinter(c); }
        else { WriteDisk(c); }
    }
}
```

Every new device means modifying `Copy` (violating OCP).

Solution: make `Copy` depend on reader and writer **abstractions**:

```cpp
interface Reader { char Read(); }
interface Writer { void Write(char c); }

void Copy(const Reader& r, Writer& w) {
    char c;
    while ((c = r.Read()) != EOF) {
        w.Write(c);
    }
}
```

`Copy` now supports any device.

![Dependency inversion for Copy](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/13_SOLID/dip-copy.svg)

*Traditional: Copy depends on concrete keyboard and printer code. Inverted: Copy and the concrete devices both depend on Reader and Writer.*

### Why "Inversion"?

| Traditional structured design (1990s) | Dependency inversion |
|---|---|
| High-level modules depend on low-level modules | Both depend on abstractions |
| Abstractions depend on details | Details depend on abstractions |
| High-level modules can't be reused | High-level modules are easy to reuse |

High-level modules contain the logic and policy; low-level changes must not break them, and we want to reuse them. Abstractions hide the low-level details. The dependency arrow now points **up** from details to abstractions — inverted.

> "All problems in computer science can be solved by another layer of indirection." — Butler Lampson
>
> "…except the problem of too many layers of indirection" / "…but that usually will create another problem." — David Wheeler

---

# Design Patterns I: GoF Patterns And Lambdas

**Subject**: CSC3003S Advanced Programming — Advanced Software Development (ASD)
**Source**: [ASD14-Design Patterns.pdf](../../Sources/CSC3003S%20-%20Advanced%20Programming/ASD/ASD14-Design%20Patterns.pdf) (61 pages) and [ASD15-Design Patterns.pdf](../../Sources/CSC3003S%20-%20Advanced%20Programming/ASD/ASD15-Design%20Patterns.pdf) (66 pages), Johan Bontes, University of Cape Town. ASD15 is a revised version of ASD14 (same content plus demo links and an updated chain-of-responsibility example), so both are covered in this one note. Content adapted from *Dive Into Design Patterns* (refactoring.guru).
**Generated**: 2026-11-06

> [!info] <span style="color:#66b3ff">Lecture reference</span>
> What design patterns are, how lambdas made many obsolete, the three GoF pattern classes, and the creational, structural and behavioural patterns covered in the course.

---

## Core Content

### What Design Patterns Are

#### Definitions

> **Design pattern**: A reusable solution to a general problem that software developers face. "Patterns … are ways to describe best practices, good designs, and capture experience in a way that it is possible for others to reuse this experience." — hillside.net

> **GoF patterns**: The patterns catalogued by the "Gang of Four" (Gamma, Helm, Johnson, Vlissides) in *Design Patterns* (1994).

New language features — especially **lambdas** — have made many patterns obsolete.

### Lambdas

#### Definitions

> **Lambda**: An anonymous function that can **capture** variables from its surrounding scope. Because it is a value, it can be stored, passed and called later.

| Language | Lambdas since |
|---|---|
| Lisp | 1960 |
| Python | 2.2 (2002); a broken version already in 1994 |
| C++ | C++11 |
| Java | Java SE 8 (2014) |

A Von Neumann computer treats code and data the same. OOP separates them: in a class, the methods (code) are fixed and the members (data) vary. In functional code, **data can also be a function** — and that is what lambdas bring to OOP languages.

### Anatomy Of A Lambda And Captures

```cpp
int i;
const auto X = [=/&, ...] (params) const/mutable {
    i++;            // error in Java
    int a = i + 2;  // valid in Java
};
```

| Part | Meaning |
|---|---|
| `const auto X =` | A named lambda; it has no dedicated type (two identical lambdas have different types) |
| `[=]` / `[&]` | Capture list: `[=]` captures by value, `[&]` by reference |
| `(params)` | Optional parameters |
| `const` / `mutable` | Whether by-value captures may be modified |
| `{ ... }` | Body; the outside `i` has been captured |

| | Java lambdas | Lambdas in other languages (e.g. C++) |
|---|---|---|
| Captures | Always effectively `const` ("somewhat broken") | Can capture by reference |
| Power | No more powerful than named functions | Changes inside the lambda change the outside value — very powerful (e.g. sorting with a counting comparator) |

**Lambdas are data**: you can put them in a collection of "workers" and call each one:

```cpp
for (auto a : WorkList) { a(); }   // C++
```
```java
for (var a : WorkList) { a(); }    // Java
```

**What can be captured?** Anything in scope when the lambda was defined. When in doubt, pass items as parameters instead:

```cpp
int i1;                              // C++ can pass anything by reference
for (auto a : WorkList) { a(i1); }
```
```java
MyInteger i2;                        // an Object is passed by reference
for (var a : WorkList) { a(i2); }
```

**Internally**, each capture becomes a member field of a hidden class:

```cpp
int i = 0;
int r = 10;
auto lambda = [&]() { i++; r++; };

// is compiled roughly as:
class lambda {
    int& i;
    int& r;
    lambda(int& i, int& r) : i(i), r(r) {}
    void dostuff() { i++; r++; }
};
```

### The Three Pattern Classes

| Class | Purpose | Patterns covered |
|---|---|---|
| **Creational** | Create objects without having to know all the details | Factory method, abstract factory, builder, singleton, prototype |
| **Structural** | Assemble objects into larger structures that are flexible and efficient | Adapter, bridge, composite, decorator, facade, flyweight, proxy |
| **Behavioural** | Communication between objects | Chain of responsibility, command, iterator, mediator, memento, observer, state, strategy, template method, visitor |

![GoF pattern catalogue](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/14_15_Design_Patterns_I/catalogue.svg)

*The GoF catalogue grouped into creational, structural and behavioural patterns.*

Creational summary:

| Pattern | In a phrase |
|---|---|
| Factory method | Polymorphic construction |
| Abstract factory | More complex construction (families of objects) |
| Builder | Build in stages |
| Singleton | "There can be only one" |
| Prototype | Java's `object.clone()` |

### Factory Method

#### Definitions

> **Factory method**: Keeps object-construction details in one place, so code can create the right subclass without naming it — "polymorphic construction".

Polymorphism needs virtual methods, but Java and C++ have **no virtual constructors**. Some languages do, e.g. Pascal (Delphi), using class references:

```pascal
type TParentClass = class of TParent;   // class type: describes the family

constructor TParent.Create() virtual;
begin Writeln('Parent.Create'); end;

constructor TChild.Create() override;
begin
    // inherited Create();
    Writeln('Child.Create');
end;

var firstClass: TParentClass; first: TParent;
begin
    firstClass := TChild;
    first := firstClass.Create();   // creates a TChild polymorphically
end.
```

Why not in C++/Java? In C++ the order of construction would be wrong (the child would be created first); Pascal constructs the parent first (it got objects later, with the flaw fixed).

**Java problem**: recreate objects from a text file of serialised object data.

- You can't restrict a constructor's signature, and constructors aren't virtual.
- You can't call a `Parent.MakeChild` method without naming the child.
- There's no `Object.fromString()` that returns a new object.

**Solution**: keep the constructor details in one place — a factory.

```java
public Shape shapeFactory(ShapeId shapeId, String params)
        throws UnknownShapeException {
    switch (shapeId) {
        case ID_oval: return new RoundRect(params, 100);
        case ID_rect: return new RoundRect(params, 0);
        case ID_blob: return new Blob(params);
        // shift left: never use default!
    }
    throw new UnknownShapeException(shapeId, params);
}

public class Circle extends Shape {
    public Circle(String params) { ... }
}
```

Leaving out `default` means the compiler can warn about unhandled enum values, catching mistakes early (shift left).

### Prototype

#### Definitions

> **Prototype**: Create new objects by cloning an existing one.

- **Java**: a single object hierarchy plus virtual methods makes it polymorphic: `object.clone()`.
- **C++**: no virtual methods needed — use duck typing and restrict the method signature with a concept:

```cpp
template <typename T>
concept Clonable = requires (const T t) {
    { t.clone() } -> same_as<T&>;
};

// clone factory method
auto clone(const Clonable auto& object) {
    return object.clone();
}
```

### Singleton

#### Definitions

> **Singleton**: A class that allows only one instance, with a global access point to it.

```java
public /*final*/ class Singleton {
    private static boolean isInitDone = false;   // lazy init
    private static final Singleton instance = new Singleton();
    // private constructor: clients cannot create more
    private Singleton() {}
    public static Singleton getInstance() {
        if (!isInitDone) { /* do initialisation */ }
        return instance;
    }
}
```

Pattern or antipattern?

| For | Against |
|---|---|
| Memory use, performance, consistency | When the GoF book appeared, it was used far too much |
| A global shared resource accessible everywhere | Breaks if more than one is actually needed |
| Used correctly for logging and database connections | Misused, it creates hidden dependencies and tight coupling |
| | It lets code **lie about its dependencies**, and you can't trust other programmers not to add mutable state to a previously immutable singleton |
| | Global state is bad and too tempting; the "CEO of the application" making all decisions becomes a god object |
| | Hard to test code without including the singleton (fix by mocking it) |

### Adapter

#### Definitions

> **Adapter**: A wrapper that lets objects with incompatible interfaces work together by converting one interface into another.

Example: an app produces XML, but the database only accepts JSON.

```java
public class Adapter implements IXML_er, IJSON_er {
    public void writeToDB(XML xdata) {
        JSON jdata = toJSON(xdata);
        json_db.write(jdata);
    }
}
```

![Adapter](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/14_15_Design_Patterns_I/adapter.svg)

*The client talks XML to the adapter, which converts to JSON for the database.*

### Bridge

#### Definitions

> **Bridge**: "Separates abstraction from implementation" (GoF) — use **composition instead of inheritance** to avoid a combinatorial explosion of subclasses.

1. Use inheritance only for IS-A relationships.
2. These are rare — very rare outside UIs.
3. Bridge uses composition instead, which lets you add new dimensions to the model.

Example: `Shape` × `Color` by inheritance needs `RedCircle`, `BlueCircle`, `RedSquare`, `BlueSquare`, … Instead, `Shape` **has a** `Color`. Similarly, for a cross-platform GUI, don't build every control for every OS: create a display layer (the bridge) that abstracts each OS's display implementation.

![Bridge](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/14_15_Design_Patterns_I/bridge.svg)

*Two independent hierarchies joined by a has-a link, so they vary separately instead of multiplying.*

### Decorator

#### Definitions

> **Decorator**: A wrapper that adds new behaviour to an object by wrapping it in an object with the same interface; wrappers can be stacked.

Inheritance is rarely the best solution. To combine traits (e.g. a notifier that sends email **and** SMS **and** Slack messages), use aggregation: wrap the base object in decorators rather than creating a subclass for every combination.

![Decorator](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/14_15_Design_Patterns_I/decorator.svg)

*Each decorator wraps another component with the same interface and adds one trait before or after delegating.*

### Facade

#### Definitions

> **Facade**: A simple interface in front of a complex subsystem.

A webshop: as a client you order a product, from a vendor, to a location, by a shipping company, with VAT payable to SARS, paid online by credit card via your bank. None of those entities is your problem — you fill in a few form fields. The form is the facade.

![Facade](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/14_15_Design_Patterns_I/facade.svg)

*The client calls one simple facade, which coordinates the many subsystems behind it.*

### Composite

#### Definitions
