> **Composite**: Composes objects into tree structures so that a container and its parts can be treated uniformly.

- The composite class is a container — a list or a tree — that may contain other containers.
- Operations iterate **recursively** over everything inside the container (e.g. the total price of a box of boxes of products).

![Composite](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/14_15_Design_Patterns_I/composite.svg)

*Leaves and containers share one interface; a container forwards each operation to its children recursively.*

### Flyweight

#### Definitions

> **Flyweight**: Shares common (intrinsic) data between many objects instead of duplicating it, to save memory.

- **Problem**: many objects contain duplicate information (e.g. every particle in a game storing its own colour and sprite), consuming too much memory.
- **Solution**: move the shared data into a shared flyweight object that many objects reference.

![Flyweight](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/14_15_Design_Patterns_I/flyweight.svg)

*Many lightweight objects keep only their unique state and point to one shared object holding the repeated data.*

But games don't actually use flyweight — they use **data-oriented design** (see [DOD](#Data-Oriented%20Design)):

- Iterating 1 000 000 objects is too slow.
- Flyweight is an **AoS** (array of structures) solution: pointer chasing.
- Games use **SoA** (structure of arrays), giving linear access.

### Proxy

#### Definitions

> **Proxy**: A stand-in that controls access to another object — a gatekeeper — often a cache between a program and a slow resource.

Examples: web proxies (Nginx, Squid), database proxies, disk proxies.

```java
interface IDisk {
    List<String> getFileList(String path);
    Vector<byte> readData(String file);
}

class DiskProxy implements IDisk {
    List<Vector<byte>> Cache;
    Disk hd;
    Vector<byte> readData(String file) {
        if (file in Cache) { return Cache.getFile(file); }
        else {
            data = hd.getFile(file);
            Cache.Add(data);
            return data;
        }
    }
}
```

Structural summary:

| Pattern | In a phrase |
|---|---|
| Adapter | Match objects with different interfaces |
| Bridge | Prevent combinatorial explosion |
| Composite | Split a big object into parts |
| Decorator | Wrapper to add new functionality |
| Facade | Simplified interface around an object |
| Flyweight | Cache redundant data to save space |
| Proxy | Gatekeeper restricting access to a class |

### Chain Of Responsibility

#### Definitions

> **Chain of responsibility**: A request passes along a chain of handlers; each one receives the request, processes it, then passes it on or drops it.

Behavioural summary:

| Pattern | In a phrase |
|---|---|
| Chain of responsibility | Message passing |
| Command | Action objects |
| Iterator | Visit every element |
| Mediator | Reduce cross-talk |
| Memento | Snapshot / undo |
| Observer | Subscribe to events |
| State | Finite state machine |
| Strategy | Object per algorithm |
| Template method | Algorithm blueprint |
| Visitor | Command++ (see [Clean Code And Visitor](#Design%20Patterns%20II%3A%20Clean%20Code%20And%20The%20Visitor%20Pattern)) |

**WinAPI mouse messages** (a window `Form1` containing `Panel1` containing `Button1`):

| Option | How the chain works |
|---|---|
| 1. Every link knows the next | Msg 1: `Button1.OnMouseOver` draws a glow and drops the message. Msg 2: `Panel1.OnMouseOver` does nothing and calls its owner's `OnMouseOver`; `Form1.OnMouseOver` flashes the caption; no owner, so drop |
| 2. Links don't know each other | WinAPI iterates (iterator pattern) over controls top-most to bottom-most: traverse the tree doing hit tests, descending into children until no more hits (the top-most control). Then visit the hit controls in reverse order; each handler returns `true` to pass the message on or `false` to stop |

ASD15 replaces this with a "clean text" demo: each handler in the chain cleans one aspect of a text (e.g. trimming, normalising) before passing it on.

![Chain of responsibility](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/14_15_Design_Patterns_I/chain.svg)

*Each handler either handles the request and stops, or passes it to the next handler.*

### Command

#### Definitions

> **Command**: Turns an action into an object (or lambda) so it can be stored, passed and executed separately from the UI that triggers it.

Problem: is `OKButton` IS-A `Button`? Yikes — subclassing every button for its action causes:

| Without Command | With Command |
|---|---|
| Confusion of property and class | No code duplication |
| Explosion of classes | Loose coupling between UI and logic |
| Business logic everywhere | |
| Duplicated code | |
| Tight UI–logic coupling | |

With lambdas, no OOP workaround is needed:

```text
type Command: [] {} -> bool;                  // lambda returning bool
const emptyCommand: Command = [&]{ return false; };

class UIControl : public InvisibleControl {
public:
    action: Command = emptyCommand;           // do nothing
    bool execute() final { return action(); }
}

//                             +--- [&] = capture context
form1.Actions.Add(ID_SaveAs, [&]{
    if (savedialog1.execute()) {              // user pressed OK
        auto filename = savedialog1.filename;
        editor1.SaveToFile(filename);
    }
});
```

![Command](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/14_15_Design_Patterns_I/command.svg)

*UI controls hold a command; executing the control runs the command, keeping business logic out of the UI classes.*

### Iterator

#### Definitions

> **Iterator**: Walks through every item of a collection without exposing its internal structure.

A collection provides `first`, `next` and `current` methods. A `for (a in Collection) { a.dostuff(); }` loop runs:

```text
c.First();            // current = c[-1]
while (c.next()) {    // current = c[0], c[1], c[2], ...
    a = c.current();
    a.dostuff();
}
```

Design custom collections with `first`, `next` and `current`. Every modern language has `for-in` loops, so no OOP workaround is needed.

### Mediator

#### Definitions

> **Mediator**: A central hub that connects multiple components so they don't talk to each other directly — reducing cross-talk. A fancy version of the command pattern.

### Memento

#### Definitions

> **Memento**: A snapshot of an object's state, created by the object itself, so state can be restored later without exposing private details.

Problem: a program must save the state of one or more objects, but

- must not access their private details, and
- cloning isn't an option (unsafe, because of links to other objects).

Solution:

| Role | Responsibility |
|---|---|
| **Originator** (the object) | Creates a snapshot of its own state, and restores from one |
| **Memento** | The snapshot, plus metadata (date, command) |
| **Caretaker** | Stores and manages snapshots |

```java
class Editor implements IUndo {
    String text;     // gets saved
    Window owner;    // not saved
    EditorSnapshot makeSnapshot();                          // IUndo
    void restoreSnapshot(EditorSnapshot restorePoint);      // IUndo
}

class EditorSnapshot implements IMemento immutable { String text; }

interface IMemento immutable {
    property int id;
    property IUndo source;
}

interface IMementoList immutable {   // if several objects must be restored
    property int id;
    property Datetime timestamp;
    property CommandId reason;
    property List<IMemento> sources;
    void restore() { for (s in sources) { s.source.restoreSnapshot(s); } }
}

class Caretaker {                    // manages snapshots
    List<IMementoList> snapshots;
    void add(IMementoList item);
    void restore(int id) {
        restorepoint = getById(snapshots, id);
        restorepoint.restore();
    }
}
```

![Memento](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/14_15_Design_Patterns_I/memento.svg)

*The editor creates and restores its own snapshots; the caretaker only stores them.*

Uses: undo (revert to a save point), transactions (commit/rollback), saving to file.

| Pro | Con |
|---|---|
| Saves state without knowing objects' internal details | Can use a lot of RAM with many snapshots |
| The caretaker tracks snapshots | May need to track originals (if not permanent) to destroy obsolete snapshots |
| | Needs immutable objects |

### Observer

#### Definitions

> **Observer** (publish–subscribe): Interested objects subscribe to a publisher, which notifies all subscribers when its state changes.

Problem: you're waiting for the iPhone 35. You could check in at the store every day (polling), or the store could email everyone. Better: interested clients **subscribe** to updates.

```java
interface IObserver {
    void update(msg);
}

interface IPublisher {
    void subscribe(IObserver client);
    void unsubscribe(IObserver client);
}

class Publisher implements IPublisher {
    List<IObserver> clients;
    private void update(msg) {
        for (c in clients) { c.update(msg); }
    }
}
```

![Observer](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/14_15_Design_Patterns_I/observer.svg)

*Subscribers register with the publisher; one state change notifies every subscriber.*

### State

#### Definitions

> **State**: Implements a finite state machine with one class per state; the master object delegates behaviour to its current state object, which switches states.

- **Problem**: a complex `case` statement controls the state.
- **Solution**: an `IState` interface; one class per state; the master object switches state and calls the state object to do the state's work.

**Music player**:

```java
interface IPlayState {
    property AudioPlayer player;
    void pressPlay();   // play/pause
}

class PlayState implements IPlayState {
    void pressPlay() {                  // play -> pause
        player.setState(new PauseState());
        player.pauseMusic();
    }
}

class PauseState implements IPlayState {
    void pressPlay() {                  // pause -> play
        player.setState(new PlayState());
        player.playMusic();
    }
}

class MusicPlayer {
    IPlayState state = new PauseState();
    Button playButton(state.pressPlay);
}
```

![State pattern music player](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/14_15_Design_Patterns_I/state.svg)

*Pressing play in PauseState switches to PlayState and vice versa; the player just forwards the button press.*

States with shared code can inherit from, or contain, shared classes.

| Pro | Con |
|---|---|
| Single responsibility: one state per class | Overkill with only a few simple states |
| Open–closed: change state details without changing the player class | |
| Eliminates complex state-machine conditionals; the master can only be in one state | |

### Strategy

#### Definitions

> **Strategy**: One object per interchangeable algorithm, selected at run time — a more complex version of State. Out of scope for this course.

### Template Method

#### Definitions

> **Template method**: Defines the skeleton of an algorithm in a base class, leaving the detailed steps to subclasses.

- **Problem**: different versions of an algorithm repeat the same code in each version, violating DRY.
- **Solution**: put the steps (skeleton) in class A and the details in class B.

```java
class BrewMaster {
    void boilWater()     { print("boil water"); }
    void addPlants()     { /* do nothing */ }
    void removePlants()  { /* do nothing */ }
    void addCondiments() { /* do nothing */ }
}

class BrewTea extends BrewMaster {
    void addPlants()     { /* hang tea bag */ }
    void removePlants()  { /* remove tea bag */ }
    void addCondiments() { /* just sugar, no milk */ }
}

class BrewCoffee extends BrewMaster {
    void addPlants() { /* add coffee */ }
    // no sugar, no milk (duh)
}
```

![Template method](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/14_15_Design_Patterns_I/template.svg)

*The base class fixes the order of steps; subclasses override only the steps that differ.*

Pro: clients can override only certain parts of a large algorithm, so they are less affected by changes to other parts. Template method is also the fix for the sequential-coupling and call-super antipatterns (see [Antipatterns](#Object-Oriented%20Antipatterns)).

---

# Security

**Subject**: CSC3003S Advanced Programming — Advanced Software Development (ASD)
**Source**: [ASD16 Security.pdf](../../Sources/CSC3003S%20-%20Advanced%20Programming/ASD/ASD16%20Security.pdf), Johan Bontes, University of Cape Town, pages 1–29
**Generated**: 2026-11-06

> [!info] <span style="color:#66b3ff">Lecture reference</span>
> Security as a built-in quality, the three main security problem areas — memory safety, code injection (SQL, XSS, command) and authentication (password storage, sessions, access control) — and their standard fixes.

---

## Core Content

### Security As A Quality And A Risk

Security is one of the ISO 25010 quality characteristics (confidentiality, integrity, non-repudiation, accountability, authenticity, resistance) — see [Dependable Systems](#Dependable%20Systems%20And%20Software%20Quality).

$$
\text{Risk} = \text{cost} \times \text{likelihood}
$$

| | Cheap | Expensive |
|---|---|---|
| **Unlikely** | Accept | Transfer (insure) |
| **Likely** | Reduce | Avoid |

Security breaches are likely and expensive, so they must be avoided.

### Security Must Be Built In

Security must be built in **from the start**:

- It is complex and subtle.
- You cannot roll your own; you must use standard solutions.
- In a hodge-podge system, the **weakest link** determines the security.
- Have a security architecture and follow the standard.
- It is a **moving target**: solutions secure in 2023 are not in 2025 (e.g. RSA public-key encryption is no longer considered secure).
- Secure software design patterns exist but are out of scope for ASD.

### The Three Main Security Problems

| Area | Threats |
|---|---|
| **Memory safety** | Reading or writing forbidden data; rights escalation; code execution |
| **Code injection** | SQL injection; command injection (and XSS) |
| **Authentication** | Passwords; session management |

### Never Roll Your Own

- Always use a known good security pattern.
- Every security problem has its own very specific fix. SQL injection and XSS are both code injection, but their fixes are completely different, with zero overlap.
- Don't use outdated fixes. SQL injection **cannot be escaped**, no matter what your 2001 website says.
- 80% secure is **not** secure.

### Memory Safety

#### Definitions

> **Buffer overrun (out-of-bounds read/write)**: Accessing memory past either end of an intended buffer.

> **Invalid buffer operation**: e.g. using a read-only pointer to write, or overwriting neighbouring data.

![Buffer overrun](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/16_Security/buffer-overrun.svg)

*Reading or writing past a buffer, or writing through a read-only pointer, can crash, give unexpected results, leak data or execute arbitrary code.*

Mitigations:

| Mitigation | Detail |
|---|---|
| Secure languages | Rust, Java |
| Modern techniques in insecure languages | C++: use the 2023 standard — "constexpr all the things". C: stop using it; it is and always will be a 1970s language |
| Compiler hardening flags | Stack canaries, etc. |
| Code sanitizers | AddressSanitizer and friends (see [Software Testing](#Code%20Sanitizers)) |

### Stack And Heap Attacks

| | Stack | Heap |
|---|---|---|
| Contents | Local data **and** function return addresses | Objects (code + data) |
| Attack | A buffer overrun changes state; overwriting the **return address** allows arbitrary code execution | Object data is nearly always read-write with no protection; overwriting a **VMT pointer** allows arbitrary code execution |
| Detection | — | Many objects sit side by side in the same data segment, so out-of-bounds writes are unlikely to trigger an exception |

### Code Injection

#### Definitions

> **Code injection**: An attack in which input is interpreted as code.

- If code is text, and input is text, then **input can be code** — never trust input.
- If output is text, then **output can be code** — never trust output.

### Security Cast Of Characters

| Name | Role |
|---|---|
| Alice, Bob, Charlie | Normal users, assumed to be "noobs" |
| Eve | Eavesdropper (read-only); a man-in-the-middle if she can also write |
| Mallory | Malicious actor, often a bot |
| Trudy | Thief, trying to steal money or credentials |

### SQL Injection

Trudy types this into a product search box:

```sql
' union select username, password from users --
```

The site pastes it into its query:

```sql
select * from products where name like ''
union select username, password from users -- '
```

The `'` closes the string, `union` appends a second query returning every username and password, and `--` comments out the rest.

![SQL injection](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/16_Security/sql-injection.svg)

*Trudy's search text becomes part of the SQL, turning a product search into a password dump.*

**There is only one fix: parameterised queries.**

- You cannot sanitise or escape SQL — it's impossible.
- Never copy-paste (string-build) SQL.
- If you must build SQL dynamically (e.g. dynamic table names), use a **whitelist**.

```python
# safe
safe_query = "SELECT * FROM users WHERE username = ?"

# impossible to fix (e.g. W3Schools-style code)
unsafe_query = f"SELECT * FROM users WHERE username = '{user_input}'"
```

### Cross-Site Scripting (XSS)

#### Definitions

> **Cross-site scripting (XSS)**: An attack where stored or reflected input is output to other users' browsers, where it runs as code.

Mallory posts a message containing `<script>BOMB</script>`. The website stores it and later shows Alice "Hello Alice! Mallory posted: <script>…", and Alice's browser runs the script.

![Cross-site scripting](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/16_Security/xss.svg)

*Input stored by the site is replayed to another user as executable code.*

Mitigated by:

- **HTML sanitation**;
- **HTML whitelisting** — only allow a safe subset.

### Whitelists Versus Blacklists

| | Whitelist (allow list) | Blacklist (deny list) |
|---|---|---|
| Approach | Only allow a known safe subset | Block known bad inputs |
| Safety | Safe (though still non-trivial) | Fundamentally broken — you can't be complete in an infinite universe |

### OS And Command Injection

A script runs `LIST /home/ + userName`. Trudy enters the user name `";DELETE ALL FILES"`, so the command becomes `LIST /home/;DELETE ALL FILES`.

![Command injection](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/16_Security/command-injection.svg)

*Input pasted into a shell command adds a second command; echoed error messages help the attacker refine it.*

Echoing verbose error messages back to Trudy is very helpful to an attacker.

Mitigated by:

- using parameterised calls;
- escaping and sanitising all input;
- never copy-pasting input into commands;
- never echoing error messages to users.

### Storing Passwords

**Never store passwords in a database.** Store a salted one-way hash instead:

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| `user_id` | e.g. 007 |
| `username` | e.g. "Bont, Johan" |
| `salt` | `strong_random_number(user_id)` — random, different per user |
| `passhash` | `crypto_one_way_hash(password + salt)` |

![Salted password hashing](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/16_Security/password-hash.svg)

*The password plus a per-user random salt goes through a slow one-way hash; only the salt and hash are stored.*

#### Definitions

> **Rainbow table**: A precomputed table of hashes of common passwords, used to reverse unsalted hashes quickly.

> **Salt**: Random data added to each password before hashing, so identical passwords hash differently and precomputed tables are useless.

- A per-user salt defeats rainbow tables.
- **Slower hashes are better**: they make brute force expensive. Strengthen further with a slower/stronger hash and a different number of rounds depending on the salt.
- Even so, individual (weak) passwords are still very likely to be cracked.

### Session Management

HTTP is **stateless**, so tracking an authenticated user is non-trivial. There are exactly three standard methods — pick one and implement it **exactly as prescribed**, using a known good library.

Python (Flask):

```python
from flask_session import Session
app = Flask(__name__)
app.config["SESSION_PERMANENT"] = False     # sessions expire when the browser closes
app.config["SESSION_TYPE"] = "filesystem"   # store session data on the filesystem
Session(app)
```

Java (Jakarta servlets):

```java
import jakarta.servlet.http.*;   // known good solution

if (user + password + 2FA == OK) {
    // erase any existing session
    HttpSession oldSession = request.getSession(false);
    if (oldSession != null) { oldSession.invalidate(); }
    // generate a clean, secure new session
    HttpSession newSession = request.getSession(true);
    newSession.setAttribute("user", "role");   // store user state on the server
    response.sendRedirect("page.html");
} else {
    response.sendRedirect("login.html?error=invalid");
}
```

Invalidating the old session on login prevents **session fixation** (an attacker planting a known session ID before the victim logs in).

### Improper, Missing Or Hard-Coded Authentication

| Problem | Mitigation |
|---|---|
| Weak, missing or hard-coded passwords, or a user ID in the URL | Force a change of the default password after installation; never put in back doors; two-factor authentication; use a standard (known secure) authentication scheme |
| All users are superusers (often combined with injection) | "Switch on brain — 1960 is no more"; always disable guest users; sandbox untrusted visitors |

---

# Design Patterns II: Clean Code And The Visitor Pattern

**Subject**: CSC3003S Advanced Programming — Advanced Software Development (ASD)
**Source**: [ASD17-Design Patterns II.pdf](../../Sources/CSC3003S%20-%20Advanced%20Programming/ASD/ASD17-Design%20Patterns%20II.pdf), Johan Bontes, University of Cape Town, pages 1–29
**Generated**: 2026-11-06

> [!info] <span style="color:#66b3ff">Lecture reference</span>
> Clean-coding principles (DRY, one task, meaningful names, no littering, no magic constants, const correctness), then the Visitor pattern and its alternatives: procedural switch, `std::variant` + `std::visit`, and the acyclic visitor. Iterator and Memento are recapped from [Design Patterns I](#Design%20Patterns%20I%3A%20GoF%20Patterns%20And%20Lambdas).

---

## Core Content

### Clean Coding Principles

| Principle | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **DRY** | Don't repeat yourself |
| **OneTask** | Do only one thing in a function or class |
| **Meaningful names** | Names that tell the reader what things are and do |
| **No littering** | No stale comments or commented-out code |
| **No magic constants** | Name your numbers and strings |
| **Const correctness** | Mark what must not change |

### DRY: Don't Repeat Yourself

If two functions both contain steps X, Y (and Z), extract each step into a function and call it:

```text
void doThis() {          void doThis2() {
    X - - -                  doX();
    Y - - -       →          doY();
    Z - - -                  doZ();
}                        }

void doThat() {          void doThat2() {
    X - - -       →          doX();
    Y - - -                  doY();
}                        }
```

### OneTask: Do One Thing

A function doing X, Y and Z with five parameters becomes a coordinator of three single-purpose functions, each taking only what it needs:

```text
void doThis(a, b, c, d, e) {      void doThis2(a, b, c) {
    X - - -                           doX(a, b);
    Y - - -              →            doY(b);
    Z - - -                           doZ(c);
}                                 }
```

This is the single responsibility principle at function level (see [SOLID](#SOLID%20Design%20Principles)).

### Meaningful Names

Code is written for humans first, computers second.

| Rule | Example |
|---|---|
| Functions are `verbNoun` | `readFile`, `computeTax` |
| Variables are two or more nouns | `fuelLevel`, `retryCount` |
| Include the unit of measure | `fuelLiters`, `timeoutSecs`, `massKg` |
| Long scope → long name; short scope → short name | `for (i …)` only within a loop; more descriptive names for longer scopes |
| Limit the lifetime (scope) of variables | Declare close to use |
| No Hungarian notation | Not `szFilename`, `ui32_counter` — use strong types instead |
| Consistent names | `begin`/`end`, `isDone`, … |

### No Littering

- Code is always up to date; comments are not.
- Put more of the description into the code and less into comments.
- Remove commented-out code — use the version control system (git) instead.
- Use comments to explain **why**; let the code explain **how/what**.

### No Magic Constants

#### Definitions

> **Magic constant**: A naked number or string in code with no explanation.

```c
a += 100;                 // why 100, not 101? what is 100?
if ('zorg' == name) {...} // hidden back door?
```

Name constants instead:

```c
const int LevelOffset = 100;    // levels are 100 units apart
const auto superuser = 'zorg';  // definitely a back door
```

Benefits:

- the intention is clear;
- each constant is defined in one place (DRY), so it's easy to update;
- you can document named constants.

### Const Correctness

| Language | Mechanism |
|---|---|
| Java | `@ReadOnly`, `final` |
| C++, Rust | `const` |
| Python | Not supported |

Benefits:

- Enables **pure functions** with no side effects.
- `const`/`final` variables keep their initial value, so they're easy to reason about and simplify debugging.
- Compiler optimisation can fold away extra variables.

### Behavioural Patterns Recap And Examples

The behavioural patterns (chain of responsibility, command, iterator, mediator, memento, observer, state, strategy, template method, visitor) and the Iterator and Memento details are as in [Design Patterns I](#Iterator).

Code examples for builder, adapter, decorator, iterator, visitor, state, chain of responsibility, strategy and observer are provided as Compiler Explorer (godbolt.org) links in the slides.

### The Visitor Pattern

#### Definitions

> **Visitor**: Adds new operations to a type hierarchy without changing the types. Each operation is a visitor class with one `visit` method per type; each type has an `accept(visitor)` method that calls back `visitor.visit(this)` ("double dispatch").

In OOP it's easy to add **types** (just add a class) but hard to add **operations**: operations are a second dimension, while a class hierarchy is one-dimensional. Visitor turns operations into their own hierarchy.

![GoF visitor](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/17_Clean_Code_And_Visitor/gof-visitor.svg)

*Shapes accept a ShapeVisitor; Rotate and Draw implement a visit method per shape. The visitor names every shape and every shape names the visitor — a cycle.*

1. `Shape` declares `accept(ShapeVisitor v)`.
2. `Circle::accept(v)` calls `v.visit(this)`, selecting `visit(Circle)`.
3. Each operation (`Rotate`, `Draw`) is a `ShapeVisitor` with `visit(Circle)` and `visit(Square)`.
4. A new operation is a new visitor class; a new shape means editing every visitor.

### Visitor Pros And Cons

| Pros | Cons |
|---|---|
| Operations can be added easily (OCP) | Hard to add new types (shapes) |
| Hides operations' implementation details (SRP) | Operations are limited to the types' public interfaces |
| | Requires a base class (risk of a god object) |
| | Needs heap allocation and memory management |
| | Requires virtual functions (extra indirection) |
| | **Has a cyclic dependency** — `ShapeVisitor` depends on every shape, and every shape depends on `ShapeVisitor` |

### Procedural Alternative

A procedural solution — an enum type tag plus a `switch` in each operation:

- needs heap-allocated objects;
- relies on `switch` statements;
- is impossible to maintain (every new type means editing every switch).

### std::variant And std::visit

A modern C++ solution:

```cpp
class Circle { /* ... */ };
class Square { /* ... */ };
using Shape = std::variant<Circle, Square>;

struct Draw {
    void operator()(const Circle&) const { /* ... */ }
    void operator()(const Square&) const { /* ... */ }
};

std::vector<Shape> shapes = { Circle{1.0}, Square{2.0} };
for (const auto& s : shapes) std::visit(Draw{}, s);
```

#### Definitions

> **`std::variant`**: A type-safe sum type (tagged union) holding exactly one of several alternative types, stored by value.

> **`std::visit`**: Calls the overload of a function object matching the type currently held by a variant.

![std::variant and std::visit](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/17_Clean_Code_And_Visitor/variant-visit.svg)

*Shapes and operations are independent; the variant ties them together, so dependencies point one way across the architectural boundary.*

No cycles; easy to add operations **and** types; satisfies OCP and SRP.

| Pros | Cons |
|---|---|
| Operations can be added easily (OCP) | Mixing big and small types carries large overhead (the variant is as big as its largest alternative) — fix with the Proxy pattern |
| Hides operations' implementation details (SRP) | `std::variant` is bad at hiding implementation details (information hiding) |
| Easy to add types | |
| Operations can use all type details | |
| No manual heap allocation (automatic destruction) | |
| No virtual functions; no cyclic dependency | |
| Faster than the OOP visitor; static polymorphism is faster and easier to optimise | |

### Performance Comparison

![Visitor performance](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/17_Clean_Code_And_Visitor/visitor-performance.svg)

*Approximate run times under GCC 11.2 and Clang 12.0.1: the enum and OO solutions and classic visitor are slowest; `std::variant` and `mpark::variant` are fastest.*

### The Acyclic Visitor

#### Definitions

> **Acyclic visitor**: A visitor variant that breaks the dependency cycle by splitting the visitor interface into an empty base `AbstractVisitor` and one small `Visitor<T>` interface per type.

```cpp
class AbstractVisitor {                 // high level
public:
    virtual ~AbstractVisitor() = default;
};

template <typename T>
class Visitor {                         // high level
public:
    virtual ~Visitor() = default;
    virtual void visit(const T&) const = 0;
};

class Shape {                           // high level
public:
    virtual ~Shape() = default;
    virtual void accept(const AbstractVisitor&) = 0;
};

// ---------------- architectural boundary ----------------

class Circle : public Shape { public: Circle(double rad); /* ... */ };
class Square : public Shape { public: Square(double side); /* ... */ };

class Draw : public AbstractVisitor,
             public Visitor<Circle>,
             public Visitor<Square> {
public:
    void visit(const Circle& circle) const override;
    void visit(const Square& square) const override;
};
```

Each shape's `accept` checks (via `dynamic_cast`) whether the visitor implements `Visitor<ItsOwnType>`. All dependencies point from low-level classes up to high-level abstractions, so there is no cycle. **Drawback: it is slow — very slow** (run-time type checks on every visit).

### Visitor Recap

| Approach | Good for | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| Classic visitor | Adding operations | Bad for adding types; cyclic |
| `std::variant` + `std::visit` | Adding operations and types | **Preferred in most scenarios** |
| Acyclic visitor | Also adding types | Slow — use if overhead isn't an issue. In Java you have no choice |

---

# Aspect-Oriented Programming And Design Patterns Recap

**Subject**: CSC3003S Advanced Programming — Advanced Software Development (ASD)
**Source**: [ASD18 Design patterns + AOP [Auto-saved].pdf](../../Sources/CSC3003S%20-%20Advanced%20Programming/ASD/ASD18%20Design%20patterns%20%2B%20AOP%20%5BAuto-saved%5D.pdf), Johan Bontes, University of Cape Town, pages 1–20
**Generated**: 2026-11-06

> [!info] <span style="color:#66b3ff">Lecture reference</span>
> Why aspect-oriented programming exists, crosscutting concerns, advice, pointcuts and aspects, how weaving works via reflection, AspectJ, AOP's pros and cons, and a summary table of every design pattern covered.

---

## Core Content

### Why AOP?

Recall the [Visitor pattern](#The%20Visitor%20Pattern): it makes it easy to add **operations** to a type hierarchy — but only if you designed it in from the start.

Design patterns need to be put in at the start. But what if you have an existing application that:

- wasn't designed with the right patterns, and
- just grew "organically-ish, sort of"?

**Example — Capislack Bank**: a banking app that grew organically over 40 years now needs security added.

- Adding features means adding new **operations**.
- Adding operations to an existing OOP app is hard.
- If only we could add the visitor pattern somehow… That is what AOP provides.

### Crosscutting Concerns

#### Definitions

> **Crosscutting concern**: A requirement that applies across many unrelated classes and methods — the same intervention needed in different parts of the application.

> **Aspect-oriented programming (AOP)**: A technique for adding crosscutting behaviour to existing code by injecting it at specified locations, without editing each location.

Characteristics:

- Different classes and interfaces need the same intervention.
- Scattered methods have the same requirements.
- The same concerns appear in different parts of the application, cutting across many classes.

Bank examples:

- new transactions only with approved clients;
- new clients only if their IDs are proper and they are not banned.

![Crosscutting security aspect](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/18_Aspect_Oriented_Programming/crosscutting.svg)

*One security check cuts across many classes of the bank app; AOP applies it everywhere from one place.*

Without AOP, this would be **shotgun surgery** (see [Antipatterns](#Shotgun%20surgery)).

### Advice, Pointcuts And Aspects

#### Definitions

> **Advice**: A piece of code injected into one or more methods of your application.

> **Pointcut**: The location(s) where an advice is injected — before a method starts, after it ends, or around it.

> **Aspect**: Advice + pointcut.

> **Join point**: A specific point in the program (e.g. a method execution) where advice can run; a pointcut selects a set of join points.

### Pointcut Expressions

Pointcuts are defined with a pattern (regular-expression-like) matching class names and function signatures.

| Pointcut | Matches |
|---|---|
| `[A-Z]*s.Add*(client_id, *)` | A class whose name starts with a capital and ends in `s`, with a method starting with `Add` whose first parameter is `client_id` |
| `[A-Z]*s.Add*(client_id,*) \| *.New*(*name, *)` | The same, **or** any method starting with `New` whose first parameter name ends in `name` |

Matches can be **AND**ed (must match all) or **OR**ed (may match any).

### Kinds Of Advice

| Advice | Runs |
|---|---|
| **Before** | Before the method starts |
| **After** | After the method finishes, regardless of exceptions |
| **Around** | Part before, then the method, then part after |
| **After exception** | Only when an exception happens |
| **After return** | After the method, only if no exception |

![Advice kinds](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/18_Aspect_Oriented_Programming/advice.svg)

*Advice can be woven in before, after or around a method, or only on normal return or on exception.*

### An Aspect In Java

```java
@Aspect
public class CheckNewTransactions {
    private Security sec;
    public CheckNewTransactions(Security sec) { this.sec = sec; }

    @Before("execution(* [A-Z]*s.Add*(client_id,..))")   // pointcut
    public void beforeMethodExecution(JoinPoint joinPoint) { // advice
        Object[] args = joinPoint.getArgs();
        if (args[0] instanceof Integer) {
            final var client_id = (int) args[0];
            sec.check(is_customer(client_id));
            sec.check(!is_criminal(client_id));
            sec.check(!is_bankrupt(client_id));
        }
    }
}
```

Before every matching `Add…(client_id, …)` method runs, the client is checked — without touching any of those methods.

### How It Works: Reflection And Weaving

#### Definitions

> **Reflection**: The ability of a process to examine, introspect and modify its own structure and behaviour.

| | Dynamic reflection | Static reflection |
|---|---|---|
| When | At run time | At compile time |
| How | Uses RTTI (run-time type information) | The compiler does "search and replace" on the source code |
| Also called | Injection | Instrumentation |

#### Definitions

> **Weaving**: Inserting aspects into the program at their pointcuts.

**AspectJ** (Java AOP, since 2001) instruments source code with an extra compile step, using the AspectJ **weaving compiler**.

### AOP Pros And Cons

| Pros | Cons |
|---|---|
| **Modularity**: crosscutting operations are easy to add | **Debugging**: class methods contain only part of the code; crosscuts can happen at any time |
| **Code reusability**: makes DRY code easy (one aspect applied in many places) | **Complexity**: easy to weave very complex crosscuts — it adds an extra dimension to your hierarchy |
| **Maintainability**: easy to patch existing code, even with a sub-optimal design | **Tooling**: the IDE may make crosscuts hard to see |
| **SOLID**: reduces dependencies; separates crosscutting concerns from main logic | **Portability**: not portable across languages; Java has the best support, C++ not yet (maybe C++29) |
| **Scalability**: AOP crosscuts are lightweight | |
| **Testability**: simpler testing because the code is DRYer | |

### Design Patterns Recap: Creational

The three categories:

| Category | Role |
|---|---|
| Creational | Object-creation workarounds |
| Structural | Mid-level architecture |
| Behavioural | Low-level design |

| Pattern | Does what? | Why? |
|---|---|---|
| Factory | Polymorphic construction of a single object | No virtual constructors |
| Abstract factory | Factory for multiple related objects | Same — no virtual constructors |
| Builder | Constructs a complex object in parts | Composition |
| Singleton | A single, globally available instance | Database wrappers, logging |
| Prototype | Clone an object (`Object.clone()`) | `a = b` only does a shallow copy |

### Design Patterns Recap: Structural

| Pattern | Does what? | Why? |
|---|---|---|
| Adapter | Connects two mismatching interfaces | Fix a mismatching API |
| Proxy | Caches a slow resource / encapsulates a big object | Speed |
| Flyweight | Obsolete | Use DOD instead |
| Composite | Recursive container (tree and/or jagged array) | A complex "list" |
| Facade | Simple interface around a complex object | Limit functionality |
| Bridge | Prevents combinatorial explosion | $10 \times 10 = 100$ classes (bad) vs $10 + 10 = 20$ (good) |
| Decorator | Adds new properties | e.g. add colour to shapes |

### Design Patterns Recap: Behavioural (Non-Lambda)

| Pattern | Does what? | Why? / notes |
|---|---|---|
| Memento | Undo / snapshot | Avoids full cloning |
| Iterator | `for-in` loop | The magic behind the scenes that makes `for-in` work |
| Visitor | Makes adding behaviour easy | An abstract pattern: 3 variants plus 1 to avoid |
| — Cyclic (classic) visitor | Easy to add behaviour, hard to add objects | The pure OOP solution |
| — Acyclic visitor | Easy to add objects and behaviour, but slow | `dynamic_cast` is slow because it walks up and down the inheritance tree |
| — Variant visitor | Discriminated / tagged-union visitor: easy to add objects and behaviour, fully decoupled | "Variant = proper 1956 OOP done right" |
| — Procedural visitor | Do not use | Not maintainable |

### Design Patterns Recap: Behavioural (Lambda For The Win)

With lambdas, most behavioural patterns reduce to "a lambda per …":

| Pattern | Does what? | Lambda version |
|---|---|---|
| Command | e.g. File → Save via menu, button and Ctrl+S | Link a lambda to an action |
| Strategy | Changes the behaviour of an algorithm (e.g. sort) | A lambda steers the algorithm |
| State | Finite state machine | Use a Java `enum` class instead |
| Template method | Multi-step algorithm | A lambda per step |
| Observer | Subscribe to events; notifies subscribers of changes | A lambda per subscription |
| Chain of responsibility | Pipe-and-filter / message passing (MPI: message passing interface, a 1970s OOP paradigm) | A lambda per message handler |
| Mediator | Central node of communication | A lambda per communication node; also MPI |

The mediator reduces the number of links between $n$ components from all pairs to one link each to the hub:

$$
\frac{n^2 - n}{2} \text{ links} \quad\longrightarrow\quad n + 1 \text{ links}
$$

---

# Artificial Intelligence In Software Development

**Subject**: CSC3003S Advanced Programming — Advanced Software Development (ASD)
**Source**: [ASD20 Artificial intelligence - Copy.pdf](../../Sources/CSC3003S%20-%20Advanced%20Programming/ASD/ASD20%20Artificial%20intelligence%20-%20Copy.pdf), Johan Bontes, University of Cape Town, pages 1–38
**Generated**: 2026-11-06

> [!info] <span style="color:#66b3ff">Lecture reference</span>
> What "AI" means (knowledge representation, reasoning, perception), how neural networks run on hardware, why scaling shows diminishing returns, the hype cycle, developer attitudes (Stack Overflow 2025), UCT's generative-AI policy, AI and learning, and the security of AI-generated code.

---

## Core Content

### What Is AI?

Is "AI" a meaningless marketing term? The EU definition is broad: essentially anything you can do with a computer. The lecture groups AI into three families:

| Family | Kind | Examples |
|---|---|---|
| **Knowledge representation** | Exact | Knowledge bases (expert systems), ontologies |
| **Reasoning** | Exact | Boolean logic (including defeasible reasoning), optimisation (genetic algorithms) |
| **Perception** | Fuzzy | Neural networks — all of generative AI |

![Kinds of AI](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/20A_Artificial_Intelligence/ai-families.svg)

*Knowledge representation is exact but hand-built; reasoning is exact but NP-hard; perception is fuzzy pattern matching on GPUs.*

### Knowledge Representation

#### Definitions

> **Knowledge base (expert system)**: A curated store of facts and rules used to answer questions.

> **Ontology**: A human-curated representation of concepts and the relations between them.

- Strives to be exact, but real-world concepts are inherently fuzzy.
- Doesn't scale, because it must be built by hand.
- Effectively dead — killed off by perception AI.

### Reasoning

#### Definitions

> **Defeasible reasoning**: Exact logic plus "maybe" — conclusions that can be withdrawn when new information arrives.

Boolean-logic reasoning is either:

- **exact** — a mathematically ideal model of a real-world problem; or
- **defeasible** — exact + maybe.

Exact Boolean logic models a problem mathematically as an **NP-hard or NP-complete** representation, e.g.:

- integer linear programming (the high-school simplex method);
- Boolean satisfiability (SAT);
- knapsack, travelling salesman, bin packing.

### Perception And Neural Networks

All of generative AI is perception, built with neural networks:

- Layers are encoded as matrices of floats.
- Transformations are matrix multiply + add — the fused multiply-add (**FMA**) operation.

For an $n \times n$ matrix multiplication:

| Measure | Scaling | Example ($n = 100$) |
|---|---|---|
| Memory | $O(n^2)$ | $100^2 = 10^4$ values |
| Work | $O(n^3)$ | $100^3 = 10^6$ operations |

Work grows faster than memory and the operations are independent, so the problem is almost **embarrassingly parallel** and runs on GPUs.

### AlexNet And Hardware Progress

**AlexNet (2012)** was the watershed moment:

- ran on the GPU, massively parallel;
- 5 convolutional layers and 3 fully connected layers;
- much better than shallow neural networks.

Neural nets run on matrix multiplication, using **tensor cores** on GPUs (and NPUs).

Speed-ups in TPUs/NPUs have come from:

- bigger TPUs/NPUs;
- more GPUs and bigger models;
- **smaller floating-point numbers** (float32 → fp16 → fp8 → fp4).

Smaller formats lose precision. NVIDIA claims int4 is as good as fp32, which is hard to believe: 4 bits give only 16 values versus about 4 billion for 32 bits. Each move to smaller units needs new hardware — lots of money for NVIDIA.

### AI Scaling: Diminishing Returns

On a log–log plot of model quality against compute, the observed frontier is **not straight** — it isn't a power law, and it keeps flattening out: diminishing returns, terrible scaling.

![Diminishing returns of scaling](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/20A_Artificial_Intelligence/scaling.svg)

*Each tenfold increase in compute buys a smaller improvement (Toby Ord, "The Scaling Paradox").*

| Hype | Reality |
|---|---|
| "Scaling Laws for Neural Language Models" (OpenAI) | Terrible return on investment: minimal improvement for 10× compute (Toby Ord) |

Recap (from Toby Ord):

- AI progress as a function of **time** is impressive.
- AI progress as a function of **resources** is not.
- Smooth, long-lasting scaling laws are proof of **poor but predictable** scaling, not impressive scaling.
- AI quality metrics scale poorly with resources; real-world impacts may (?) scale better.

**The "circle r" test**: image generators asked to draw something like a circled "r" fail abjectly, because picture AI doesn't understand the concept of a word.

**The future?** The current approach scales poorly. Companies can still throw money at it — another 10× scale-up of data centres is possible (ouch!) — but the human brain scales much better. Perhaps a new algorithm will fix this: we're waiting for the next AlexNet-style discovery.

### Combining Perception And Reasoning

Humans use **perception + reasoning**, but no known algorithm combines both:

| Perception | Reasoning |
|---|---|
| Its knowledge representation is unknown (unknowable?) | Rigid facts and hard mathematical models (defeasible reasoning research works on soft "maybe" logic) |
| Everything fuzzy | Everything absolute |

How do you merge the two? Current "reasoning AI" is just a marketing term for perception — it has no understanding and no way to model concepts.

### Problems With Matrix-Multiply Pattern Matching

| Property | Effect |
|---|---|
| $x \times 0 = 0$ | Zeroed items are gone for good — partly good, as it limits scope to the question asked |
| $x \times \infty = \infty$ | Infinite values can't be recovered — the lecturer suspects this causes confabulation |
| Perception = pattern matching | Extreme patterns get magnified (e.g. LLMs and Nazi ideology); subtle patterns vanish into the noise (e.g. LLMs and nuance) |

Social networks optimise for engagement the same way: anger is good, nuance is bad.

### The Hype Cycle

#### Definitions

> **Gartner hype cycle**: The typical path of public expectations for a new technology over time.

![Gartner hype cycle](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/20A_Artificial_Intelligence/hype-cycle.svg)

*Visibility rises from a technology trigger to a peak of inflated expectations, falls into a trough of disillusionment, then climbs a slope of enlightenment to a plateau of productivity.*

1. Technology trigger
2. Peak of inflated expectations
3. Trough of disillusionment
4. Slope of enlightenment
5. Plateau of productivity

Gartner's 2025 hype cycle for generative AI places many GenAI technologies around the peak and heading into the trough.

### Developers And AI: Stack Overflow 2025

![Stack Overflow survey 2025](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/20A_Artificial_Intelligence/so-survey.svg)

*Most developers use AI tools, but few trust their accuracy or their handling of complex tasks.*

| Using AI tools? (84% use or plan to) | Share |
|---|---|
| Yes, daily | 47.1% |
| Yes, weekly | 17.7% |
| Yes, monthly | 13.7% |
| No, but planning to | 5.3% |
| No, and don't plan to | 16.2% |

| Trust the accuracy of AI output? | Share |
|---|---|
| Highly trust | 3.1% |
| Somewhat trust | 29.6% |
| Somewhat distrust | 26.1% |
| Highly distrust | 19.6% |

| How well does AI handle complex tasks? (professional devs) | Share |
|---|---|
| Very well | 3.9% |
| Good, but not great | 25.2% |
| Neither good nor bad | 14.2% |
| Bad | 22.8% |
| Very poor | 18.6% |
| Don't use / don't know | 15.3% |

| Biggest frustrations | Share |
|---|---|
| AI solutions that are almost right, but not quite | 66% |
| Debugging AI-generated code takes longer | 45.2% |
| Don't use AI tools regularly | 23.5% |
| Less confident in own problem-solving | 20% |
| Hard to understand how/why the code works | 16.3% |
| Other | 11.6% |
| No problems | 4% |

**Vibe coding at work?** No: 72.2%; no, emphatically: 5.3%; yes: 11.9%; yes, somewhat: 2.8%; yes, emphatically: 0.4%.

### UCT CS Generative AI Policy

| Rule | Consequence |
|---|---|
| Undeclared generative AI use | Plagiarism: zero for the course and a mark on your degree record |
| A vibe-coded assignment (no human effort) | 1% mark for the course |
| First-year CS | No generative AI allowed |
| Second and third year | Allowed, but must be declared, and the work must be mostly human |
| Explicitly allowed by a supervisor | May be used to expand the scope of an assignment (more interesting prototypes) |
| Science Dean | 80% of the mark must come from examinations in a controlled environment (no internet) |

If a student shows insufficient understanding or knowledge of work they claim to have done themselves, the grade may be lowered.

### AI And Student Learning

Research on AI's effect on learning is **inconclusive** and inconsistent. It:

- supports the creation of ideas;
- aids interaction;
- **hinders critical thinking and analytical skills**.

#### Definitions

> **Bloom's taxonomy**: A hierarchy of learning goals from lower-order to higher-order thinking.

![Bloom's taxonomy](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/20A_Artificial_Intelligence/blooms.svg)

*Learning goals from lower order (remember, understand, apply) to higher order (analyse, evaluate, create).*

| Level | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Create | Produce new or original work |
| Evaluate | Justify a stand or decision |
| Analyse | Draw connections among ideas |
| Apply | Use information in new situations |
| Understand | Explain ideas or concepts |
| Remember | Recall facts and basic concepts |

A meta-analysis ("The impact of generative AI on university students' learning outcomes via Bloom's taxonomy") of 24 studies was mostly positive:

| Outcome | Pooled effect (Hedges' $g$) | Result |
|---|---|---|
| Lower-order learning | ≈ 0.93 ($p < 0.001$) | Helps |
| Higher-order learning | ≈ 0.64 ($p \approx 0.001$) | Helps |
| Non-cognitive skills (motivation, self-efficacy, enjoyment) | ≈ 0.55 ($p \approx 0.004$) | Helps |

But treat this with caution:

- Studies were performed in controlled environments — does that translate to messy real-world universities?
- Studies with non-significant or negative results are less likely to be published (**publication bias**), and there may be funding bias (big tech).
- Only short-term effects were measured.
- GenAI is a social experiment that will take decades to play out.

### Security Of AI-Generated Code

The Veracode 2025 GenAI Code Security Report found that **45% of generated code contains a known security flaw** (only 55% of tasks produced secure code).

![Secure pass rates of AI-generated code](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/20A_Artificial_Intelligence/genai-security.svg)

*Security varies hugely by weakness type and somewhat by language, and has not improved with newer or larger models.*

| Language | Secure pass rate |
|---|---|
| Python | 61.69% |
| JavaScript | 57.34% |
| C# | 55.27% |
| Java | 28.5% |

| Weakness (CWE) | Secure pass rate |
|---|---|
| CWE-327 bad cryptography | 85.61% |
| CWE-89 SQL injection | 80.44% |
| CWE-80 XSS | 13.53% |
| CWE-117 log injection | 12.03% |

- Better models? **Syntax** has improved; **security** has not.
- Larger models are not significantly better.

Is insecure GenAI code a proxy for logical bugs too? Likely, because GenAI lacks understanding. (An interesting research question: GenAI code versus bugs found by sanitizers.)

GenAI shifts effort from writing functionality to finding and fixing bugs. Writing code is about 5% of effort, while finding and fixing bugs is about 50% — so this doesn't look like progress.

# Professional Conduct And Ethics

**Subject**: CSC3003S Advanced Programming — Advanced Software Development (ASD)
**Source**: [ASD20-Ethics.pdf](../../Sources/CSC3003S%20-%20Advanced%20Programming/ASD/ASD20-Ethics.pdf), Johan Bontes, University of Cape Town, pages 1–22
**Generated**: 2026-11-06

> [!info] <span style="color:#66b3ff">Lecture reference</span>
> How software development compares with regulated professions, the ACM and IEEE codes of ethics, principle-based versus rule-based ethics, licensing, the Boeing MCAS and Meta LLM cases, intellectual property and copyleft, legal versus ethical, software licences, and plagiarism.

---

## Core Content

### Regulated Professions Versus Software Developers

Chartered accountants (requirements vary by country; the lecturer's example):

- 4 years undergraduate;
- 3 years postgraduate (comparable to a master's);
- 3 years apprenticeship under a chartered accountant (concurrent with postgrad);
- 7 years of university-level study in total;
- compulsory membership of a professional oversight body;
- permanent continuing-education requirements;
- disciplinary hearings for violations of the code of ethics;
- subject to legal requirements codified in law.

The same holds for medical doctors, architects and lawyers. For software developers: nothing — a "nullptr exception".

| Requirement | Chartered accountant | Software developer |
|---|---|---|
| University-level education | 7 years (4 UG + 3 PG) | None required |
| Apprenticeship | 3 years | None |
| Certification / licensing | Yes | None |
| Compulsory professional body | Yes | None |
| Continuing education | Yes | None |
| Disciplinary mechanism | Yes | None |
| Code of ethics | Enforced in law | Voluntary only |

![Regulated professions versus developers](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/ASD/20B_Professional_Conduct_And_Ethics/professions.svg)

*Every safeguard regulated professions have is absent for software developers.*

### ACM Code Of Ethics (2018)

**1. General ethical principles**

1. Contribute to society and to human well-being, acknowledging that all people are stakeholders in computing.
2. Avoid harm.
3. Be honest and trustworthy.
4. Be fair and take action not to discriminate.
5. Respect the work required to produce new ideas, inventions, creative works and computing artefacts.
6. Respect privacy.
7. Honour confidentiality.

**2. Professional responsibilities**

1. Strive for high quality in both the processes and products of professional work.
2. Maintain high standards of professional competence, conduct and ethical practice.
3. Know and respect existing rules pertaining to professional work.
4. Accept and provide appropriate professional review.
5. Give comprehensive and thorough evaluations of computer systems and their impacts, including analysis of possible risks.
6. Perform work only in areas of competence.
7. Foster public awareness and understanding of computing, related technologies and their consequences.
8. Access computing and communication resources only when authorised or when compelled by the public good.
9. Design and implement systems that are robustly and usably secure.

**3. Professional leadership principles**

1. Ensure the public good is the central concern during all professional computing work.
2. Articulate, encourage acceptance of, and evaluate fulfilment of social responsibilities by members of the organisation or group.
3. Manage personnel and resources to enhance the quality of working life.
4. Articulate, apply and support policies and processes that reflect the principles of the Code.
5. Create opportunities for members of the organisation or group to grow as professionals.
6. Use care when modifying or retiring systems.
7. Recognise and take special care of systems that become integrated into the infrastructure of society.

**4. Compliance with the Code**

1. Uphold, promote and respect the principles of the Code.
2. Treat violations of the Code as inconsistent with membership in the ACM.

### IEEE Code Of Ethics (2020)

"To uphold the highest standards of integrity, responsible behaviour and ethical conduct in professional activities":

1. Hold paramount the safety, health and welfare of the public; strive to comply with ethical design and sustainable development practices; protect others' privacy; promptly disclose factors that might endanger the public or the environment.
2. Improve individual and societal understanding of the capabilities and societal implications of conventional and emerging technologies, including intelligent systems.
3. Avoid real or perceived conflicts of interest whenever possible, and disclose them to affected parties when they exist.
4. Avoid unlawful conduct in professional activities, and reject bribery in all its forms.
5. Seek, accept and offer honest criticism of technical work; acknowledge and correct errors; be honest and realistic in stating claims or estimates based on available data; credit properly the contributions of others.
6. Maintain and improve technical competence, and undertake technological tasks for others only if qualified by training or experience, or after full disclosure of pertinent limitations.
7. Treat all persons fairly and with respect, without discrimination based on race, religion, gender, disability, age, national origin, sexual orientation, gender identity or gender expression.
8. Do not engage in harassment of any kind, including sexual harassment or bullying.
9. Avoid injuring others, their property, reputation or employment by false or malicious actions, rumours or any other verbal or physical abuse.
10. Support colleagues in following this code, strive to ensure it is upheld, and do not retaliate against individuals reporting a violation.

### Don't Lawyerise The Code Of Ethics

| American legal system | European legal system |
|---|---|
| Exceptionally detailed; every eventuality captured in clauses | Mostly derived from Roman law (449 BC – 526 AD) |
| Feels secure, but encourages **gaming the system**: "if it's not explicitly forbidden, it must be allowed" | **Principle-based** |
| Forces an ever-increasing level of detail | ***Fraus legis***: applying the letter of the law fraudulently is itself illegal — acts are legal only if they comply with the **intent** of the law |

A code of ethics should be read like the European system: follow its intent, not search for loopholes.

### Should Software Developers Be Licensed?

The ACM opposes licensing. Its arguments and the lecturer's responses:

| ACM argument | Response |
|---|---|
| It is premature | Seems a weak argument |
| Computing pervades all aspects of modern life | — |
| Licensing would not be effective in improving software quality and reliability | Debatable: centres of excellence aside, a certified minimum level of training and experience would improve quality and reliability |

Licensing would also bring **enforceable** codes of ethics and professionalism, giving the public a means to hold professionals to account — though it obviously wouldn't stop all violations.

### Case: Boeing MCAS

Was the behaviour of the programmers who built the 737 MAX's MCAS ethical? (See [Dependable Systems](#Case%20Study%3A%20Boeing%20737%20MAX%20MCAS) for the design.) Questions to ask, from the codes:

| Code principle | Question |
|---|---|
| Avoid harm | Did the design avoid harm? |
| Competence | Was the work within their competence? |
| High quality | Was it high-quality work? |
| Honesty | Were risks honestly disclosed? |
| Lawful conduct | Was it FAA-compliant? |
| Care when modifying systems | Was enough care taken when modifying the aircraft's system? |

### Case: Generative AI And Meta's LLM

- Meta downloaded every book on the pirate site libgen.is, via BitTorrent, to train its LLM.
- It claimed this was "legal" because it didn't enable sharing of the torrented data (leeching only).
- This is a clear violation of the ACM and IEEE codes of ethics — but software developers have no legally enforceable code of ethics, unlike accountants, lawyers and architects.
- Generative AI threatens to put content creators out of work: artists, writers, film makers — and programmers too.

### Intellectual Property And Copyleft

#### Definitions

> **Intellectual property (IP)**: Legal rights over creations of the mind — copyright, patents and licensing terms — within an international framework. Not all countries respect IP (e.g. Russia disregards copyright entirely).

> **Copyleft (GPL)**: Using copyright law to **preserve** the right to use, modify and distribute free software, by requiring derivatives to carry the same freedoms.

**Richard Stallman** (Free Software Foundation):

- Created copyleft via the GPL licence.
- Argues copyright, patents and digital restrictions management (DRM) are misused to lock information away from users and programmers indefinitely ("Defective by Design" campaign).
- Advocates **boycott, not piracy**.

### Sci-Hub And Civil Disobedience

Pirate Bay, LibGen and **Sci-Hub** (Alexandra Elbakyan) — Sci-Hub has been paused since 2020 due to an Indian lawsuit.

- Goal: "to provide free and unrestricted access to all scientific knowledge."
- Elbakyan argues it is legal under the right to science and culture in Article 27 of the Universal Declaration of Human Rights.
- Others argue Sci-Hub is a moral imperative (**civil disobedience**), and that IP law should be changed rather than Sci-Hub banned.

### Legal Versus Ethical

Law is a **balance of interests**:

- That doesn't mean the balance is just or fair — "legal" does not mean "good".
- Laws can be captured by special interests.
- Apartheid laws were legal; the NSDAP (Nazi party) complied fully with German law.

The lecturer's take: if you join an organisation, either subscribe to its core tenets or resign — your power to change it from within is very limited.

### Common Software Licences

| Type | Examples |
|---|---|
| Open source | GPL, LGPL, Apache, MIT |
| Proprietary | Vendor-specific licences |

### Plagiarism And Authorship

| Why it's unethical for scholars | Why it's unethical for developers |
|---|---|
| Reputations are built on original research | Violates intellectual property |
| Undermines meritocracy | Makes it impossible to track the source of code |
| Dishonest | You can't research its limitations or issues |
| | Makes you look like a fraud |

As a risk: probability high and cost high, so **avoid** it (see the risk matrix in [Dependable Systems](#Security%20And%20The%20Risk%20Matrix)).
