# Advanced Typing And Functions

**Subject**: CSC3003S Advanced Programming — Functional Programming (FP)
**Source**: [FP 8 - Haskell 4 - advanced typing and functions-3.pdf](../../Sources/CSC3003S%20-%20Advanced%20Programming/FP/FP%208%20-%20Haskell%204%20-%20advanced%20typing%20and%20functions-3.pdf), Cael Marquard (adapted from Dr Sabre Didi), University of Cape Town, pages 1–31
**Generated**: 2026-11-06

> [!info] <span style="color:#66b3ff">Lecture reference</span>
> Polymorphism and generic functions, type constraints and typeclasses, implementing and deriving `Show`, generic data types and `Maybe` instead of null, type constructors as functions on types, and Hoogle.

---

## Core Content

### Why Polymorphism?

So far, functions have taken and returned **concrete types**. `add' :: Int -> Int -> Int` works only on `Int`s — not even on `Float`s:

```haskell
add' :: Int -> Int -> Int
add' a b = a + b

add' 1 2      -- 3
add' 1.0 2.0  -- ERROR: this function only takes Ints
```

Floats can be added perfectly well, so how do we write one function that accepts both?

#### Definitions

> **Polymorphism**: "Having many forms" (poly = many, morph = form). A polymorphic function or type works for many types.

> **Generic function**: A function that is parameterised over a type, like Java's `ArrayList<T>`, which is generic over type `T`.

### Declaring A Generic Function

Add a **type parameter** (type variable) to the signature:

```haskell
add :: Int -> Int -> Int
-- becomes
add :: a -> a -> a
```

Read it as: "`add` is a function with a type parameter `a` that takes two values of type `a` and returns a value of type `a`."

Writing the body fails:

```haskell
add' :: a -> a -> a
add' x y = x + y
```

```text
error: [GHC-39999]
  * No instance for `Num a' arising from a use of `+'
  * In the expression: x + y
```

The signature claims `add'` accepts **any** type, but not every type can be added with `+`.

### Type Constraints And Overloaded Functions

#### Definitions

> **Unconstrained type variable**: A type variable such as `a` in `a -> a -> a` that only enforces that all uses are the same concrete type; it says nothing about what operations that type supports.

> **Type constraint**: A restriction such as `Num a =>` limiting a type variable to types that belong to a typeclass.

> **Overloaded function**: A function with one or more constraints on its type parameters.

Restrict `a` to numbers:

```haskell
add' :: Num a => a -> a -> a   -- "Num a =>" is the constraint
add' x y = x + y

add' 1 2      -- 3
add' 1.0 2.4  -- 3.4
```

[AR: "Ad-hoc Polymorphism" on HaskellWiki]

The lecture exercise — a generic `sum` — uses the same idea:

```haskell
sum' :: Num a => [a] -> a
sum' xs = foldl (+) 0 xs
```

### Typeclasses

#### Definitions

> **Typeclass**: A named set of behaviours (functions) that a type can implement. It is not a Java/Python class; it is more like an **interface**, and almost exactly a Rust **trait**.

`Num` in `Num a =>` is a typeclass.

| Typeclass | OOP class |
|---|---|
| Specifies **behaviours** | Bundles data **and** logic |
| A type can implement as many as you like | Often you can inherit from only one |
| The instance can be defined with the typeclass **or** with the type, so you can implement it for foreign types | Inheritance is fixed at the type's definition; you can't inherit "for" a foreign type |

[AR: haskell.org tutorial, "Type Classes and Overloading"]

### The Num Typeclass

```haskell
class Num a where              -- a: the type that will implement Num
    (+), (-), (*)  :: a -> a -> a
    negate         :: a -> a
    abs            :: a -> a
    signum         :: a -> a
    fromInteger    :: Integer -> a
    x - y          = x + negate y   -- provided (default) definitions
    negate x       = 0 - x
```

| Part | Role |
|---|---|
| `class Num a where` | Declares the typeclass; `a` stands for the implementing type, so the signatures can mention it |
| Signatures (`(+)`, `negate`, …) | Member functions an instance must implement |
| Equations (`x - y = …`, `negate x = …`) | Member functions provided by default in terms of others |

### Implementing Show

One advantage of typeclasses is implementing them for **foreign types**: a typeclass defined in one package (e.g. `base`, the standard library) can be implemented in another.

`Show` (from `base`) is a common example. It specifies how to convert a type to a `String`, which is useful for debugging with `print :: Show a => a -> IO ()` (equivalent to `putStrLn . show`).

`Show` requires one function, `show :: a -> String`. There is a tacit contract that the output is itself a valid Haskell expression, so `show` is for debugging, not pretty-printing.

```haskell
-- The constructor may be named differently from the type
data StreetAddress = Addr {
    number :: Int,
    street :: String
}

instance Show StreetAddress where
    show addr =
        "Addr { number = " ++ (show $ number addr)
        ++ ", street = " ++ (show $ street addr)
        ++ "}"

x = Addr { number = 1, street = "Main Rd" }
show x   -- Addr { number = 1, street = "Main Rd" }
```

Adding more fields (`postcode`, `city`, `province`, …) makes the hand-written instance grow line by line — tedious, fast.

### Deriving Typeclasses

#### Definitions

> **Deriving**: Asking the compiler to generate a typeclass instance automatically with `deriving`.

```haskell
data StreetAddress = Addr
  { number   :: Int
  , street   :: String
  , postcode :: Int
  , province :: String
  , city     :: String
  , country  :: String
  } deriving Show

show $ Addr 1 "Main Rd" 7700 "Western Cape" "Cape Town" "South Africa"
-- "Addr {number = 1, street = \"Main Rd\", postcode = 7700,
--  province = \"Western Cape\", city = \"Cape Town\", country = \"South Africa\"}"
```

Deriving `Show` also works for generic types, e.g. `data Pair a = Pair a a deriving Show`.

### The Problem With Null-Like Values

Generic **data types** are useful too. Example: a social-media user profile where

- a user must always have a name,
- a user may have an age,
- a user may have a parent's email (e.g. for under-18s).

In SQL:

```sql
CREATE TABLE users (
    name        TEXT NOT NULL,
    age         INTEGER,
    parentEmail TEXT
);
```

**Haskell has no null values.** You can't assign "null" or "None" to an `Int` or `String`. [AR: "Null References: The Billion Dollar Mistake", Tony Hoare, the inventor of null]

A tempting workaround uses 0 for "no age" and `""` for "no email":

```haskell
data User = User
    { name        :: String
    , age         :: Int
    , parentEmail :: String
    }

users = [ User "Cael" 24 ""
        , User "Thabo" 16 "mama.ka.thabo@example.com"
        , User "Haskell Curry" 0 ""   -- he is dead
        ]
```

Now spam every user:

```haskell
sendEmail :: String -> String -> IO ()
-- sends the 1st string to the email address in the 2nd string

spamAll :: String -> [User] -> IO ()
-- mapM_ is like map for IO functions, discarding the () results
spamAll spam users = mapM_ (\user -> sendEmail spam $ email user) users

spamAll "We miss you!" users
```

Eventually this evaluates:

```haskell
(\user -> sendEmail spam $ email user) (User "Cael" 24 "")
= sendEmail "We miss you!" $ email (User "Cael" 24 "")
= sendEmail "We miss you!" ""   -- uh-oh! No email address!
```

This is fundamentally a **type error**: `sendEmail` expected a non-empty string, and we forgot to handle the empty case. Filtering fixes this one call site:

```haskell
mapM_ (\user -> sendEmail spam $ email user)
    $ filter (\user -> (email user) /= "") users
```

But you would need that filter everywhere the email is used — and if empty emails are introduced late in development, how do you find every place? A type system exists precisely to prevent this kind of error.

### The Maybe Type

#### Definitions

> **`Maybe a`**: A generic sum type representing an optional value: either `Nothing` or `Just` a value of type `a`.

```haskell
data Maybe a = Nothing | Just a
```

The type parameter `a` goes before the `=`; this is different syntax from functions.

```haskell
data User = User
    { name        :: String
    , age         :: Maybe Int
    , parentEmail :: Maybe String
    }
```

Now the buggy `spamAll` doesn't compile:

```text
error: [GHC-83865]
  * Couldn't match expected type: [Char]
                with actual type: Maybe String
  * In the second argument of `($)', namely `email user'
```

GHC catches the bug before it can happen. The fix uses `mapMaybe` from `Data.Maybe`, which applies a function returning `Maybe` and keeps only the `Just` results:

```haskell
import Data.Maybe

spamAll :: String -> [User] -> IO ()
spamAll spam users = mapM_
    (\usrEmail -> sendEmail spam usrEmail)
    $ mapMaybe email users
```

![mapMaybe](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/FP/08_Advanced_Typing_And_Functions/mapmaybe.svg)

*`mapMaybe` unwraps every `Just` value and drops every `Nothing`.*

The lecture exercise re-implements it:

```haskell
mapMaybe' :: (a -> Maybe b) -> [a] -> [b]
mapMaybe' _ [] = []
mapMaybe' f (x:xs) = case f x of
    Nothing -> mapMaybe' f xs
    Just y  -> y : mapMaybe' f xs
```

A loophole remains: you can still construct `Just ""`. Closing it is covered in [Making Illegal States Unrepresentable](#Making%20Illegal%20States%20Unrepresentable). [AR: "Make illegal states unrepresentable"] [AR: "Parse, don't validate", Alexis King]

### Type Constructors Are Functions On Types

#### Definitions

> **Type constructor**: A generic type such as `Maybe`, seen as a function that maps a type to another type: `Maybe` maps `Int` to `Maybe Int`.

> **Kind**: The "type of a type". `*` means a concrete type, so `* -> *` means "maps a type to a type".

Functions map between values: `show :: Show a => a -> String` maps a `Show` instance to a string. Generic types map between **types**.

In GHCi, `:kind` shows a type's kind, just as `:type` shows a value's type:

```text
ghci> :kind Int
Int :: *
ghci> :kind Maybe
Maybe :: * -> *
ghci> :kind Maybe Int
Maybe Int :: *
```

![Type constructors as functions on types](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/FP/08_Advanced_Typing_And_Functions/type-constructor.svg)

*`Maybe` takes the concrete type `Int` and produces the concrete type `Maybe Int`.*

[AR: "Type constructor" on HaskellWiki] [AR: "Kinds and Higher-Kinded Types in Haskell", Gints Dreimanis]

### Hoogle

Haskell's type system is expressive enough that Haskellers often search for functions by **type signature** rather than by name, using **Hoogle** (hoogle.haskell.org). For example, searching `Integer -> String` finds `show` and related functions.

Assigned reading (LYAH): *Types and Typeclasses*, *Syntax in Functions*, *Making Our Own Types and Typeclasses*; in preparation: "Foldl and foldr" (Alexis King) and the Stack Overflow question "Why does foldl seem to be harmful despite being tail recursive?".

---

# Recursion And Lazy Evaluation In Haskell

**Subject**: CSC3003S Advanced Programming — Functional Programming (FP)
**Source**: [FP 9 - Haskell 5 - Recursion and lazy evaluation-1.pdf](../../Sources/CSC3003S%20-%20Advanced%20Programming/FP/FP%209%20-%20Haskell%205%20-%20Recursion%20and%20lazy%20evaluation-1.pdf), Cael Marquard (adapted from Dr Sabre Didi), University of Cape Town, pages 1–35
**Generated**: 2026-11-06

> [!info] <span style="color:#66b3ff">Lecture reference</span>
> Recursion as while and for loops, naturally recursive functions (Fibonacci, quicksort), why `foldl` overflows and `foldl'` doesn't, thunks, and three ways to make Fibonacci fast.

---

## Core Content

### Loops, Folds And Recursion

Recap: `:kind Int` is `*`, and a typeclass encapsulates supported **behaviour** only (no data).

| Imperative construct | Haskell equivalent |
|---|---|
| `for` loop | `foldl` / `foldr` / `foldl'` |
| `while` loop (the "unbounded search operator" $\mu$) | Recursion |

Haskell has no `while` loops, but recursion can compute exactly the same things. Just as any `for` loop can become a fold, any `while` loop can become a recursive function — recursion can implement **any** kind of loop.

### Recursion As While Loops

Recursive functions needn't be complicated. Think of them as `while` loops where the state is passed forward in the arguments.

```python
def next_prime(start):
    x = start + 1
    while not isPrime(x):
        x += 1
    return x
```

```haskell
nextPrime :: Int -> Int
nextPrime start = searchPrime $ start + 1
  where
    searchPrime start
      | isPrime start = start
      | otherwise     = searchPrime (start + 1)
```

The helper `searchPrime` exists because the loop's state (`x`) starts at `start + 1`, not at `start`. The outer function sets up the initial state, and the helper carries it through the iterations.

**Keeping more state**: find the $k$th prime after `start`.

- `kth_next_prime(2, 4)` passes 3, 5 and 7, returning 11 (2 itself is not counted).
- `kth_next_prime(4, 4)` passes 5, 7 and 11, returning 13.

```python
def kth_next_prime(start, k):
    x = start
    while k > 0:
        x += 1
        if isPrime(x):
            k -= 1
    return x
```

```haskell
kthNextPrime :: Int -> Int -> Int
kthNextPrime start k = searchPrime (start + 1) (k - 1)
  where
    searchPrime start k
      | isPrime start && k == 0 = start
      | isPrime start           = searchPrime (start + 1) (k - 1)
      | otherwise               = searchPrime (start + 1) k
```

### Recursion As For Loops

Recursion also neatly expresses `for` loops — and therefore list combinators and comprehensions.

```haskell
sum' :: Num a => [a] -> a
sum' []     = 0
sum' (x:xs) = x + sum' xs

map' :: (a -> b) -> [a] -> [b]
map' _ []     = []
map' f (x:xs) = f x : map' f xs
```

The lecture exercise "implement `foldr` recursively":

```haskell
myfoldr :: (a -> b -> b) -> b -> [a] -> b
myfoldr _ acc []     = acc
myfoldr f acc (x:xs) = f x (myfoldr f acc xs)
```

### Naturally Recursive Functions And Their Structure

#### Definitions

> **Recurrence relation**: The part of a recursive function that calls itself.

> **Base case**: The part that defines the output for the simplest input and stops the recursion. Omit it only if you want an infinite loop.

Some functions are most natural recursively, like Fibonacci:

$$
\text{fib}(0) = 0, \qquad \text{fib}(1) = 1, \qquad \text{fib}(n) = \text{fib}(n-1) + \text{fib}(n-2)
$$

```haskell
fib :: Int -> Int
fib 0 = 0                          -- base case
fib 1 = 1                          -- base case
fib n = fib (n - 1) + fib (n - 2)  -- recurrence relation

fib 10 -- 55
```

### Quicksort

Many sorting algorithms are naturally recursive. Quicksort:

1. `[]` is already sorted.
2. A non-empty list is sorted by picking a pivot (here, simply the first element) and partitioning the rest:
   - elements $\le$ pivot,
   - elements $>$ pivot.
3. Quicksort both parts.
4. Return (sorted smaller part) ++ [pivot] ++ (sorted larger part).

```haskell
qsort :: Ord a => [a] -> [a]
qsort []     = []
qsort (x:xs) = qsort smaller ++ [pivot] ++ qsort larger
  where
    pivot   = x
    smaller = [i | i <- xs, i <= pivot]
    larger  = [i | i <- xs, i >  pivot]

qsort [2, 5, -10, 2]   -- [-10, 2, 2, 5]
```

The `Ord a` constraint is needed because elements are compared. (The source names the parts `tail` and `head`, which shadows the Prelude functions; they are renamed here for clarity.)

The lecture exercise "implement merge sort recursively":

```haskell
msort :: Ord a => [a] -> [a]
msort []  = []
msort [x] = [x]
msort xs  = merge (msort left) (msort right)
  where (left, right) = splitAt (length xs `div` 2) xs

merge :: Ord a => [a] -> [a] -> [a]
merge [] ys = ys
merge xs [] = xs
merge (x:xs) (y:ys)
  | x <= y    = x : merge xs (y:ys)
  | otherwise = y : merge (x:xs) ys
```

### Recursive Expansion

Substituting definitions shows how `fib 3` is evaluated:

```text
fib 3 = ( fib (3-1)              +  fib (3-2) )
      = ( fib (3-1)              +  fib 1     )
      = ( fib 2                  +  1         )
      = ( (fib (2-1) + fib (2-2)) + 1         )
      = ( (fib 1 + fib 0)        +  1         )
      = ( (1 + 0)                +  1         )
      = 1 + 1 = 2
```

`fib 1` is evaluated **twice**, and the duplication gets exponentially worse for larger $n$.

![Naive Fibonacci call tree](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/FP/09_Recursion_And_Lazy_Evaluation/fib-call-tree.svg)

*The call tree for `fib 4` repeats whole subtrees, which is why naive recursion is exponential.*

### Why foldl Overflows

`foldl` has a trap: stack overflows.

```haskell
ns = [1..300_000_000]
foldl (+) 0 ns
```

This allocates about 14.3 GiB of memory and may end with `*** Exception: stack overflow` — on a mere 300 million items.

#### Definitions

> **Thunk**: An unevaluated expression held in memory until its value is needed.

`foldl (+) 0 ns` computes $((((0 + 1) + 2) + 3) + 4) + \dots$. Because Haskell evaluates lazily and outermost-first, none of the additions happens until the very end. Memory fills with a chain of almost 300 million thunks:

$$
((\dots + 299999998) + 299999999) + 300000000
$$

The expression can't be reduced until memory has already run out. (The lecture's "lazy kitchen with only one bench" analogy: piling up unfinished dishes instead of finishing each as you go.)

`foldr` doesn't help. `foldr (+) 0 ns` computes $1 + (2 + (3 + \dots))$; before resolving $1 + (\dots)$ it must resolve $2 + (\dots)$, and so on. (And `foldr` is only interchangeable with `foldl` for associative operators — not for `-`.)

### foldl' And seq

#### Definitions

> **`seq`**: A built-in ("magic", not user-implementable) function `seq :: a -> b -> b` that strictly evaluates its first argument, then returns its second. It is useful when the second is defined in terms of the first, e.g. `seq arg (function arg)`.

> **`foldl'`**: A left fold that applies the operator strictly at each step.

The fix is to stop Haskell piling up thunks by forcing strict evaluation of the operator, so the accumulator collapses as we go:

```haskell
-- Lazy
foldl f acc []     = acc
foldl f acc (x:xs) = let acc' = f acc x
                     in foldl f acc' xs

-- Strict in the operator f
foldl' f acc []     = acc
foldl' f acc (x:xs) = let acc' = f acc x
                      in seq acc' $ foldl' f acc' xs
```

`foldl' (+) 0 [1..300_000_000]` works.

![foldl versus foldl'](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/FP/09_Recursion_And_Lazy_Evaluation/foldl-thunks.svg)

*`foldl` builds a growing chain of thunks; `foldl'` forces each partial sum immediately, keeping one value.*

| Fold | Associativity | Evaluation | Large sums |
|---|---|---|---|
| `foldl` | Left | Lazy — builds thunks | Overflows |
| `foldr` | Right | Lazy — nested pending additions | Overflows |
| `foldl'` | Left | Strict at each step | Constant memory |

### Think Of The Thunks

The most important thing about recursion in Haskell is to **think of the thunks**.

- Because Haskell is lazy, a notional call stack growing large doesn't matter much — Haskell doesn't really have one in the usual sense.
- What matters is keeping the number of **unevaluated thunks** down, by judiciously forcing strict evaluation when necessary, as with `foldl'`.

Technically Haskell is specified as **non-strict**, which is slightly different from lazy. [AR: "Lazy vs. non-strict", Haskell Wiki]

### Memoising Fibonacci: Lazy Dynamic Programming

#### Definitions

> **Memoisation**: Caching previously computed values of a function, conceptually like dynamic programming.

Following Tikhon Jelvis's "Lazy Dynamic Programming": define an array of computed values, as in DP, but build the array **recursively from itself** instead of iteratively.

```haskell
import Data.Array

fib' :: Int -> Integer
fib' n = go n
  where go 0 = 0
        go 1 = 1
        go i = fibs ! (i - 1) + fibs ! (i - 2)
        fibs = listArray (0, n) [go x | x <- [0..n]]
```

| Piece | Role |
|---|---|
| `Data.Array` | Like Java's `ArrayList`: $O(1)$ access; `!` is the index operator |
| `listArray (0, n) [...]` | Builds the array lazily from a range of indices |
| `go` | Conventional name for a recursing helper (like `i` in a `for` loop) |

Each array entry is a thunk that refers to earlier entries. Once forced, it stays computed, so each Fibonacci number is computed once. This is much faster and handles much larger $n$.

It still keeps every previous value, so it uses $O(n)$ space. Only the last two values are needed, so Fibonacci can run in $O(1)$ space. Laziness doesn't make Haskell immune to ordinary algorithmic optimisation.

### Tail Recursion And Constant-Space Fibonacci

#### Definitions

> **Tail recursion**: A function always returns either a simple value or a call to itself (a **tail call**). In many languages this allows $O(1)$ stack space.

In Haskell, tail recursion and tail-call elimination matter less because laziness is implemented with thunks [see "Tail recursion" on the Haskell Wiki], but it helps here.

**Tail-recursive version**:

```haskell
fib :: Int -> Integer
fib n = go 0 1 0
  where
    go n_2 n_1 cur_n
      | n == cur_n = n_2
      | otherwise  = go n_1 (n_2 + n_1) (cur_n + 1)
```

- Only the last two results are kept and passed to the next `go` call, so it uses $O(1)$ space.
- `+` is strict in both arguments, which forces evaluation and avoids a large stack of thunks.
- The source returns `n_1` in the base case, which yields $\text{fib}(n+1)$; returning `n_2` gives $\text{fib}(n)$ with $\text{fib}(0) = 0$.

**Fold version** — fold is the universal list combinator. Fold over $1..n$ carrying the pair $(\text{fib}(i), \text{fib}(i-1))$:

```haskell
import Data.List (foldl')

fib :: Int -> Integer
fib n = fst (foldl' go (0, 1) [1..n])
  where
    go (n_1, n_2) _ = (n_1 + n_2, n_1)
```

| Version | Time | Space |
|---|---|---|
| Naive recursion | Exponential | Large (repeated calls) |
| Lazy DP array | $O(n)$ | $O(n)$ |
| Tail recursion / `foldl'` | $O(n)$ | $O(1)$ — fast enough for `fib 1_000_000` |

### Recursion Or Combinator?

Many things can be written either way:

| Recursive | Combinator |
|---|---|
| `fib` with `go` | `fib` with `foldl'` |
| `transform (x:xs) = (x * 2) : transform xs` | `map (*2) xs` |

It is ultimately a matter of taste.

- Combinators each do one specific thing, so the intent is obvious — the `map` version is much clearer.
- Some say the more Haskell you know, the less recursion you write, because you learn the specific tool for each job.
- Code is read more often than it is written, so keep it clear.

### Guarded Recursion

#### Definitions

> **Guarded recursion**: Placing the recursive call inside a data constructor (such as cons `:`), so the rest of the structure is only computed when demanded.

```haskell
map f (x:xs) = (f x) : (map f xs)
```

Because Haskell is lazy, `map f xs` is only evaluated if and only if the rest of the list is needed. This lets `map` work on infinite lists and avoids building thunk chains. You don't need the theory, just the habit: make recursive calls happen inside a data constructor where possible.

Quiz answers: a base case is the condition for the simplest input, which stops the recursion; `foldl` differs from `foldr` in being left-associative; recursion can implement any kind of loop.

Further reading (only if folds confuse you): "Foldl and foldr" (blog.haskell.org), "Foldr Foldl Foldl'" and "Performance/Strictness" (Haskell Wiki); assigned: LYAH *Recursion*, "Fold" (Haskell Wiki), Jelvis's "Lazy Dynamic Programming", "Fibonacci in Haskell" (Michael Jones).

# Practical Preparation: Tree Traversal

**Subject**: CSC3003S Advanced Programming — Functional Programming (FP)
**Source**: [FP-10-Haskell 6 - Prac prep (tree traversal)-1.pdf](../../Sources/CSC3003S%20-%20Advanced%20Programming/FP/FP-10-Haskell%206%20-%20Prac%20prep%20%28tree%20traversal%29-1.pdf), Cael Marquard (adapted from Dr Sabre Didi), University of Cape Town, pages 1–39
**Generated**: 2026-11-06

> [!info] <span style="color:#66b3ff">Lecture reference</span>
> Function composition, then the "dog in the wizard's dungeon" problem: modelling a tree in Haskell, solving it by divide and conquer, ranking results with custom `Eq`/`Ord` instances, and the format of the practical.

---

## Core Content

### Engagement Warning

The test and practical are set at a fair level and won't be hard **if you engage and practise** — but you will certainly fail if you don't practise Haskell. Most students weren't doing the daily exercises or attending, and fewer than 300 of 429 had opened the first lecture PDF.

### Function Composition

#### Definitions

> **Function composition**: Defining a function as a combination of other functions, without mentioning the data it acts on.

> **Compose operator (`.`)**: $f . g = \lambda x \to f\,(g\,x)$, so $(f . g)\,x = f\,(g\,x)$.

Haskell's functions are first-class and its function definitions are expressive. Composition lets you think about the **mapping** you want rather than how data is shuffled around — about the functions themselves, not the values going in and out.

**Example**: the magnitude (length) of a vector, e.g. the distance from $(1, 2)$ to $(0, 0)$:

$$
\lVert \mathbf{x} \rVert = \sqrt{\sum_i x_i^2}
$$

```haskell
magnitude :: [Float] -> Float

-- applying functions to the data
magnitude xs = sqrt $ sum $ map (^2) xs

-- as a composition of functions
magnitude = sqrt . sum . map (^2)
```

![Function composition](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/FP/10_Prac_Prep_Tree_Traversal/composition.svg)

*`sqrt . sum . map (^2)` is one function built by chaining three; data flows right to left.*

Composing functions with several arguments with `.` is possible but far from trivial. (The lecturer's example `flip flip 2 . ((^) .) . (-)` is famously hard to read.)

Quiz: a valid inferred type for `sum . (map (+2))` is `Num a => [a] -> a`, and a lambda is an anonymous function.

### The Dungeon Problem

An evil (but fair) wizard has locked the class dog in his dungeon maze. Send him a Haskell program that guides the dog through, and she'll be returned.

A dungeon is like a maze whose rooms contain hazards (as in tabletop role-playing games such as D&D, Pathfinder or Dungeon Crawl Classics). The wizard promises:

| Promise | Consequence |
|---|---|
| Each room connects to 0–2 **child rooms** | The dungeon is a graph |
| There are no cycles; each room is reachable by only one path | It is an acyclic graph |
| Every room is reachable from the start room (no hidden rooms) | It is a **tree** |

Each room contains one of:

- nothing (empty),
- a monster,
- a health potion that helps the dog recover from fights,
- the exit, which the dog must reach.

**Goal**: calculate the best possible outcome for the dog by taking the best route. You don't need to record which turns were taken — only where the dog is, where it has been, and its current attributes.

### Modelling The Dungeon In Haskell

```haskell
-- A room has contents and may branch into 0, 1 or 2 rooms.
-- No cycles, so Room is a tree data structure.
data Room = Room RoomContents
                 (Maybe Room) (Maybe Room)
                 deriving Show

data RoomContents = Fight Monster
                  | Heal Int
                  | Empty
                  | Escape
                  deriving Show

-- Our dog :)
data Dog = Dog
  { dhp  :: Int   -- health points
  , ddmg :: Int   -- damage per hit
  } deriving Show

-- A monster! :o
data Monster = Monster
  { mhp  :: Int
  , mdmg :: Int
  } deriving Show

-- The result of the dog entering a room:
-- 1. Victory: escape
-- 2. Death: die
-- 3. Continue: alive, but not free yet
data VisitResult = Victory
                 | Death
                 | Continue Dog
                 deriving Show

-- The solution: the result of the dog visiting a room AND its child rooms
crawl :: Dog -> Room -> VisitResult
```

`Maybe Room` encodes "there may or may not be a child here", and `RoomContents` and `VisitResult` are sum types.

### Divide-And-Conquer Strategy

This is divide and conquer, as in TOA ([Divide And Conquer](../TOA/05_Divide_And_Conquer.md)):

- **Subproblem**: each subtree.
- **Combine**: decide which child rooms to visit and keep only the best results.

![Dungeon tree and results](../../diagrams/CSC3003S-Advanced%20Programming/FP/10_Prac_Prep_Tree_Traversal/dungeon-tree.svg)

*Each subtree is solved independently; at every room the best of the possible routes is passed up to its parent.*

### Fighting A Monster

```haskell
-- The result of our dog fighting a monster
fight :: Dog -> Monster -> VisitResult
fight dog mon
  | dhp dog <= 0 = Death
  | mhp mon <= 0 = Continue dog
  | otherwise    = fight dog' mon'
  where
    dog' = dog { dhp = (dhp dog) - (mdmg mon) }
    mon' = mon { mhp = (mhp mon) - (ddmg dog) }
```

Each round, both sides deal damage at the same time. The fight recurses until one side's HP drops to 0 or below; the dog's death is checked first.

### Worked Example On A Small Dungeon

The dog starts as `Dog { dhp = 10, ddmg = 5 }`. The root has a left child (a skeleton, whose own right child is empty) and a right child (a healing room `Heal 10` leading to the exit).

1. **Root → left**: the dog fights the skeleton and survives as `Continue (Dog 5 5)`.
2. **Root → left → right**: an empty room; nothing changes, so the result is still `Continue (Dog 5 5)`.
3. Propagating up: the best of the left side is `Continue (Dog 5 5)`, which has less health than the starting dog. So the real choice is between going right or staying in the root room (slowly starving).
4. **Root → right**: the healing room gives `Dog 20 5`, then the exit gives `Victory`. (The quiz answer for this subtree is **Victory**.)
5. **At the root**, the options are:

| Option | Result |
|---|---|
| Stay | `Continue (Dog 10 5)` |
| Go left | `Continue (Dog 5 5)` |
| Go right | `Victory` |

The overall result is **Victory**. To pick it in code, we need a way to compare and rank `VisitResult`s.

### Ranking Results With Eq And Ord

#### Definitions

> **`Eq`**: The typeclass for equality (`==`).

> **`Ord`**: The typeclass for ordering (`<=`, `max`, …). It requires `Eq`: `class Eq a => Ord a`.

> **Pattern guard**: A guard of the form `| Pattern <- expr`, which succeeds if `expr` matches `Pattern` and binds its variables.

Implementing `Ord` therefore also requires `Eq`. The dog's damage never changes, and dogs from different dungeons are never compared, so only HP matters:

```haskell
instance Eq VisitResult where
    Victory      == Victory      = True
    Death        == Death        = True
    (Continue a) == (Continue b) = (dhp a) == (dhp b)
    _            == _            = False
```

`x <= y` is defined to mean "x is a **worse** result than y for the dog":

```haskell
instance Ord VisitResult where
    a <= b
      -- == implies <=, since <= means < or ==
      | a == b = True
      -- If both continue, the better one has more health
      | (Continue dog) <- a, (Continue dog') <- b = (dhp dog) <= (dhp dog')
      -- Death is worse than anything
      | Death <- a = True     -- Death <= anything
      | Death <- b = False    -- anything <= Death fails
      -- Victory is better than anything
      | Victory <- b = True   -- anything <= Victory
      | Victory <- a = False  -- Victory <= anything fails
```

| Ranking | Result |
|---|---|
| Worst | `Death` |
| Middle | `Continue dog`, ordered by `dhp` |
| Best | `Victory` |

### Visiting One Room (Conquer)

`visitRoom` handles every kind of room, ignoring the children for now (they're dealt with in `fork`, which keeps the code neat). This is the **conquer** part.

```haskell
visitRoom :: Dog -> RoomContents -> VisitResult
visitRoom dog room = go room
  where
    go (Fight monster) = fight dog monster
    go (Heal hp)       = Continue dog { dhp = (dhp dog) + hp }
    go Escape          = Victory
    go Empty           = Continue dog
```

### Choosing Among Child Rooms (Divide)

`fork` is the **divide** part: it tries every way of continuing from a room and returns the best.

```haskell
fork :: Dog -> Maybe Room -> Maybe Room -> VisitResult
fork dog leftRoom rightRoom = bestPath
  where
    -- The best path is the highest-scoring valid path
    bestPath   = maximum validPaths
    -- Nothing = an invalid path (e.g. going left when there is no left room);
    -- catMaybes keeps only the paths that can actually be taken
    validPaths = catMaybes
                   [leftFirst, rightFirst, leftOnly, rightOnly, Just $ Continue dog]
    leftFirst  = fmap (uncurry $ crawlBoth dog) (both leftRoom rightRoom)
    rightFirst = fmap (uncurry $ crawlBoth dog) (both rightRoom leftRoom)
    leftOnly   = fmap (crawl dog) leftRoom
    rightOnly  = fmap (crawl dog) rightRoom

-- Combine two Maybes: Just only when both are Just
both :: Maybe a -> Maybe b -> Maybe (a, b)
both (Just a) (Just b) = Just (a, b)
both _        _        = Nothing

-- Visit the first room (and its subtree) and THEN the second
crawlBoth :: Dog -> Room -> Room -> VisitResult
crawlBoth dog first second = case (crawl dog first) of
    Continue dog' -> crawl dog' second
    Victory       -> Victory
    Death         -> Death
```

The five candidate paths:

| Path | Meaning |
|---|---|
| `leftFirst` | Do the whole left subtree, then the right subtree |
| `rightFirst` | Do the right subtree, then the left |
| `leftOnly` | Only the left subtree |
| `rightOnly` | Only the right subtree |
| `Just $ Continue dog` | Stay put |

`maximum` uses the `Ord` instance to pick the best.

### fmap And uncurry

#### Definitions

> **`fmap`**: A generalisation of `map`. On `Maybe` it applies a function to the value inside a `Just` and leaves `Nothing` alone.

> **`uncurry`**: Turns a curried two-argument function into one that takes a pair.

```haskell
fmap f (Just x) = Just (f x)
fmap _ Nothing  = Nothing

uncurry f = \(a, b) -> f a b
(uncurry (+)) (1, 2) == (+) 1 2
```

So `fmap (uncurry $ crawlBoth dog) (both leftRoom rightRoom)` calls `crawlBoth dog left right` only if both rooms exist.

### Putting It Together: crawl

```haskell
-- The result of the dog visiting a room AND its children.
-- This solves the subtree rooted at the room, so calling it
-- on the root solves the whole dungeon.
crawl :: Dog -> Room -> VisitResult
crawl dog (Room this left right) = case (visitRoom dog this) of
    Continue dog' -> fork dog' left right
    Victory       -> Victory
    Death         -> Death
```

`crawl` and `crawlBoth` repeat the same `case` pattern. The lecture exercise factors it out:

```haskell
flatmapContinue :: (Dog -> VisitResult) -> VisitResult -> VisitResult
flatmapContinue f (Continue dog) = f dog
flatmapContinue _ Victory        = Victory
flatmapContinue _ Death          = Death

crawlBoth dog first second =
    flatmapContinue (\dog' -> crawl dog' second) $ crawl dog first

crawl dog (Room this left right) =
    flatmapContinue (\dog' -> fork dog' left right) $ visitRoom dog this
```

This "continue only on success" pattern is exactly what monads generalise (see [Monads](#Monads)).

### Practical Format And Sample Test

The full base solution is provided (Amathuba: `Haskell - BaseGame.hs`). Each prac session has a different but equally difficult set of questions.

| Question | Format | Advice |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Heavily scaffolded and incremental (1.2 builds on 1.1, …) | Lots of partial marks **as long as your code compiles**. Start from a blank `Q1Ans.hs` and slowly copy and modify code from the skeleton |
| 2 | Not scaffolded; usually builds on Question 1 | Partial marks for a basic solution, more for handling harder inputs; a perfect answer compiles and handles all cases. Submit in a **separate file** — copy Q1 forward, never the other way |

**Sample test**: the wizard also stole a pet rat. Fetch it if possible, but only if it costs the dog nothing: the net health of the best path that gets the rat must be $\ge$ the best path that doesn't.

Question 1 (scaffolded):

1. Implement a `Rat` type with a member `ratName :: String` (accessor `ratName :: Rat -> String`).
2. Add a member `rat :: Maybe Rat` to `Dog`.
3. Add a `RoomContents` variant `Cage` holding a `Rat`.
4. Change `Eq` for `Dog` so two dogs are equal only if both have rats.
5. Change `Ord` for `Dog` so that, at equal health, a dog without a rat is less than one with a rat: `(Dog 10 2 Nothing) <= (Dog 10 2 (Just (Rat "Jeremy")))`, but `(Dog 4 2 (Just (Rat "Jeremy"))) <= (Dog 10 2 Nothing)`.

Question 2 (unscaffolded): **all** your rats have been taken, at most one per room.

1. Change `Dog` to hold multiple rats (member `rats`).
2. Write `ratCount :: Dog -> Int`.
3. Change `Victory` to hold a `Dog`, to show how many rats were fetched.
4. Change `visitRoom` to pick up a rat in a rat room.
5. Change `Eq` and `Ord` so the best solution picks up the most rats, where one rat is worth 2 HP (10 HP with 5 rats beats 19 HP with none). Still avoid death.
